When he was safe the next morning, they were encouraged and began demolishing with him until they reached the foundations of Ibrahim (Abraham).

The Crisis of the Black Stone: A War on the Brink of Eruption

Things went smoothly, and the tribes shared in the construction, each tribe building a section. Until they reached the decisive moment: the placement of the Black Stone (الحجر الأسود). The tribes differed and disputed fiercely, as each tribe wanted to attain the honor of lifting it and placing it in its spot. It was not just a stone, but a gem from the gems of Paradise as mentioned in the hadith, and it held great religious symbolism.

The dispute intensified and lasted for four or five nights, and a civil war was about to ignite in Makkah. The situation escalated to the point that Banu Abd al-Dar brought a bowl filled with blood, and they and their allies, Banu 'Adi, made a pact to die for this honor, dipping their hands in the blood in what was known as the "Pact of the Lickers of Blood" (لعقة الدم), a sign that they would not relinquish this honor even if it cost them their lives.

The Wise Arbitration: "This is Al-Amin, We Are Pleased"

In the midst of this crisis, Abu Umayyah ibn al-Mughirah al-Makhzumi (أبو أمية بن المغيرة المخزومي), who was the eldest and wisest of Quraysh, came forward with a proposal to end the dispute. He said, "O people of Quraysh, let the first person to enter through this gate of the mosque (the gate of Banu Shaybah) be the judge between you in what you differ." This was a compromising solution that satisfied everyone's pride. They agreed and sat waiting to see who would enter.

And divine providence willed that the first to enter was Muhammad ﷺ. When they saw him, their faces lit up, and they all exclaimed in one voice filled with joy and reassurance:

"This is Al-Amin (the Trustworthy), we are pleased with him. This is Muhammad (محمد)."

This choice was not a coincidence; it was a collective testimony to the status he had reached in the hearts of his people. They did not say "This is the son of Abdullah" or "This is the Hashimite," but they described him by the quality that was the title of his character: Trustworthiness.

The Prophet's Ingenious Solution

When he reached them and they informed him of the dispute, he handled the situation with unparalleled wisdom, a wisdom that was not born of the moment but was a divine guidance. He did not side with his tribe, nor did he impose his opinion. Instead, he presented a collaborative solution that saved everyone's face:

  • He asked for a cloak and spread it on the ground.
  • He placed the Black Stone in its center with his own noble hands.
  • Then he said, "Let each tribe take a side of the cloak, then lift it all together."

They did as he commanded, and thus all the tribes shared in the honor of lifting the stone. When they reached its position, he took it with his blessed hand and placed it in its spot, then they built around it.

With this ingenious solution that had not occurred to anyone, all the tribes gained the honor of participation, bloodshed was prevented, and Makkah avoided a devastating civil war. This incident, as narrated in books of Seerah like that of Ibn Hisham, was a great portent and a practical proof of his exceptional leadership personality. His wisdom and high standing among his people as a unique, unifying figure became apparent, just five years before his mission, as if Allah was psychologically preparing them to accept his leadership in the future, not just as a political ruler, but as a Prophet and Messenger who would unite their ranks and build their nation.

Recap: Rebuilding the Kaaba (Age 35)

  • A Collective Effort: Quraysh united to rebuild the Kaaba but were limited by their lawful earnings.
  • The Black Stone Crisis: A dispute over the honor of placing the stone nearly led to civil war.
  • The Trustworthy Arbitrator: By divine will, Muhammad ﷺ was chosen as the judge, and all tribes accepted him, calling him "Al-Amin."
  • An Inspired Solution: His wisdom in having all tribes share the honor saved Makkah from bloodshed and demonstrated his unique leadership qualities.

Divine Infallibility Before Prophethood: A Divine Preparation for the Seal of the Messengers

One of the greatest proofs of his prophethood ﷺ is his perfect life before the mission. His prophethood was not a sudden event in the life of an ordinary man, but the culmination of forty years of divine care and exceptional divine protection, which is known as Ismah (العصمة) - Infallibility. Allah the Almighty protected and cared for His Prophet ﷺ from his youth, preserving him from all the filth and impurities of the pre-Islamic era of ignorance, whether in beliefs, morals, or behaviors.

Comprehensive Purity and Absolute Chastity

This infallibility was comprehensive, covering all aspects of his life:

  • In Belief: He never prostrated to an idol, nor did he ever touch one. In fact, his pure nature intensely disliked idols. It is narrated that he told a man who asked him to swear by Al-Lat and Al-Uzza: "I have never sworn by them. I pass by them and turn away." He raised those under his care on this principle as well. Zayd ibn Harithah was circumambulating the Kaaba with him and touched an idol, and the Prophet ﷺ forbade him. When Zayd touched it again, the Prophet ﷺ forbade him sternly, and Zayd never touched an idol again.
  • In Morals and Conduct: He never drank alcohol, never ate what was slaughtered on stone altars for idols, and never participated in their vain gatherings or idolatrous celebrations. Most importantly, he was never known to have told a single lie in his life, neither in seriousness nor in jest, to the point that even his enemies testified to this. He also never betrayed a trust; they would deposit their valuables with him for safekeeping even while they were at war with him.
  • In Modesty and Integrity: He was known for his dignity, intense modesty, and sound mind. One of the most telling proofs of this is what happened to him as a young man during the rebuilding of the Kaaba. He was carrying stones with his people, who had uncovered their lower garments to place them on their shoulders as protection from the stones. He ﷺ said: "I uncovered my lower garment and placed it on my shoulder, and I fell down unconscious. After that day, I was never seen naked." In another narration, his uncle Al-Abbas said to him, "O son of my brother, why don't you place your garment on your shoulder over the stones?" He did so, and fell down unconscious, his eyes fixed on the sky. He then awoke saying, "My garment, my garment," and he fastened it securely. This incident was a direct divine protection of his modesty and covering.

Divine Protection Even from Fleeting Thoughts

This infallibility was not limited to major actions but even included minor things that a young man might consider. He narrated that he said: "I never intended to do anything that the people of the era of ignorance used to do except for two times, and in both instances, Allah intervened between me and them."

He mentioned that in his youth, he wanted to join other young men at a wedding celebration in Makkah as they used to do. The first time, as soon as he reached the place of the celebration, Allah made him fall asleep, and he was not awakened until by the heat of the sun the next day. The second time, the same thing happened to him. Then the Prophet ﷺ said, "By Allah, I never intended any evil after that until Allah honored me with His message." (The hadith is narrated by Al-Bayhaqi in Dala'il al-Nubuwwah and others. Although there is some debate about its chain of narration, its meaning is sound and consistent with his established perfect character).

There is a profound indication in this hadith: The thought and desire are natural human inclinations, and this proves his humanity. But the direct divine intervention preventing him from even attending proves his specialty and his infallibility. Allah was protecting and preserving him even from the simplest forms of amusement that might not be inherently forbidden but were not befitting of the station for which he was being prepared.

The Wisdom of Infallibility: His Life is the Greatest Proof of His Truthfulness

Thus, Allah prepared His Prophet ﷺ to be the Seal of the Prophets and Messengers, with an integrated, pure, wise personality, and a subject of trust and consensus among his people. His life before the mission was the practical proof of his truthfulness. When the revelation came to him and he called them to Allah, his staunchest enemies could not find in his forty-year history a single blemish or a flaw in his character or behavior to use as an argument against him.

His life was the irrefutable argument that silenced them, which is what Allah commanded him to confront them with in the Holy Qur'an:

﴾قُل لَّوْ شَاءَ اللَّهُ مَا تَلَوْتُهُ عَلَيْكُمْ وَلَا أَدْرَاكُم بِهِ ۖ فَقَدْ لَبِثْتُ فِيكُمْ عُمُرًا مِّن قَبْلِهِ ۚ أَفَلَا تَعْقِلُونَ﴿
"Say, [O Muhammad], 'If Allah had willed, I would not have recited it to you, nor would He have made it known to you. For I had remained among you a lifetime before it. Then will you not reason?'" (Yunus: 16)

The meaning of the verse is: How can you doubt my truthfulness as I bring you this Qur'an, when I have lived among you for forty years before revealing anything to you, during which you knew my truthfulness, my trustworthiness, and my perfection, and you never found me telling a single lie or committing a single betrayal? Will you not then use your intellect to realize that one who did not lie to people for forty years will not then come to lie about Allah at the peak of his maturity and perfection?

His entire life was a pure white page, and a speaking witness that he was "As-Sadiq Al-Amin" - The Truthful, the Trustworthy - even before he uttered the message.

Recap: Divine Infallibility Before Prophethood

  • A Perfect Life: Allah protected him from all the impurities of Jahiliyyah in belief, morals, and conduct.
  • Comprehensive Purity: He never worshipped idols, drank alcohol, lied, or betrayed a trust. His modesty was divinely guarded.
  • Protection from Minor Lapses: Divine intervention even prevented him from attending youthful gatherings unbecoming of his future status.
  • The Ultimate Proof: His 40-year flawless record was the greatest proof of his truthfulness when he received the revelation, a fact highlighted in the Qur'an.

From the Building of the Kaaba to the Cave of Hira: A Deep Study into the Precursors of Prophethood and the Beginning of Revelation

Praise be to Allah, we bear witness that there is no god but Allah alone, with no partner, and we bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger, may Allah's peace and blessings be upon him. To proceed:

The study of the Prophet's biography (Seerah) is not merely a review of historical events, but a profound journey of faith and intellect aimed at understanding the wisdom of the divine law and the methodology of prophethood. In this session, we continue our journey through the life of the Chosen One, peace be upon him, addressing a critical and decisive phase: the period immediately preceding his divine commission, with its harbingers and signs, leading up to the momentous instant when the earth met the heavens—the moment of the revelation's descent. Our approach in this study is comprehensive, seeking to connect the ethical, creedal, political, and social aspects of the Seerah to derive lessons and morals that illuminate our path in our contemporary reality.

The Wisdom of Keeping the Kaaba on the Foundation of Quraysh: A Prophetic Lesson in the Fiqh of Interests and Harms

The Holy Kaaba (الكعبة المشرفة) stands in the heart of Mecca (مكة المكرمة), majestic and revered, to which the hearts of millions of Muslims are drawn. It is the first house established for mankind, the focal point of their worship, and the symbol of their unity. However, many who contemplate its history might wonder: Why did the Prophet (محمد صلى الله عليه وسلم), after Allah empowered him on earth and he conquered Mecca, not rebuild it upon its original foundations laid by our father Abraham (إبراهيم عليه السلام)? And why did it remain in the form that Quraysh built it, which, due to a shortage of lawful funds, they were forced to shorten, leaving out a portion known today as the "Hijr of Ishmael" (حجر إسماعيل)?

The answer to this question lies not in weakness or inability, but in the depth of prophetic wisdom and its application of a great principle of Islamic law: "Warding off harm takes precedence over acquiring benefits" (Dar' al-mafasid awla min jalb al-manafi'). This prophetic stance is not just a historical event but a complete methodology in leadership and understanding reality, teaching the nation how to balance between an anticipated good and an expected evil.

Historical Context: From Abraham's Construction to that of Quraysh

To understand the wisdom of the Prophet's decision, we must revisit the history of the Kaaba's construction.

The Abrahamic Foundation: Abraham (إبراهيم) and his son Ishmael (إسماعيل), peace be upon them, built the Kaaba by Allah's command, as He says:

﴾And [mention] when Abraham was raising the foundations of the House and [with him] Ishmael, [saying], "Our Lord, accept [this] from us. Indeed, You are the Hearing, the Knowing."﴿ [Al-Baqarah: 127]
(﴿وَإِذْ يَرْفَعُ إِبْرَاهِيمُ الْقَوَاعِدَ مِنَ الْبَيْتِ وَإِسْمَاعِيلُ رَبَّنَا تَقَبَّلْ مِنَّا ۖ إِنَّكَ أَنتَ السَّمِيعُ الْعَلِيمُ﴾)
Its original structure was rectangular, including what is now the "Hijr of Ishmael" within it. It had two doors level with the ground—one on the east for entry and one on the west for exit, to make it easy for people.

The Construction of Quraysh: Five years before the Prophet's mission, the Kaaba was damaged by a severe flood that weakened its structure. Quraysh gathered to rebuild it, stipulating that they would only use lawful money, free from the proceeds of prostitution, usury, or injustice. Due to the scarcity of the lawful funds they collected, they fell short of completing the building on Abraham's original foundations. This resulted in several fundamental changes:

  • Shortening the Structure: They excluded the northern part, now known as the "Hijr of Ishmael," and surrounded it with a low wall to indicate it was part of the House.
  • Raising the Door: They raised the door of the Kaaba above ground level to control who entered, allowing whom they wished and preventing whom they wished.
  • Closing the Western Door: They settled for one door and sealed the other.

"This change in the door design was not merely an architectural necessity but a decision reflecting Quraysh's desire to impose their sovereignty over the House. Abraham's original design, peace be upon him, included two doors at ground level, one east for entry and one west for exit, facilitating ease for people and embodying the simplicity of the faith. As for Quraysh, they closed the western door and intentionally raised the single eastern door to control who entered and who was barred, saying amongst themselves: 'So that none may enter except those we wish.' They made the House of Allah exclusive to whomever they pleased, which was something the Prophet (صلى الله عليه وسلم) wished to change."

When the construction reached the location of the Black Stone (الحجر الأسود), the tribes disputed over who would have the honor of placing it. They were on the verge of fighting until the wise young Muhammad (صلى الله عليه وسلم) spread his cloak, placed the stone in its center, and commanded the chiefs of the tribes to lift it together. It was a solution that satisfied everyone and prevented bloodshed.

The Prophetic Wish and Profound Wisdom

After the conquest of Mecca, the Prophet (صلى الله عليه وسلم) possessed the power and ability to fulfill his wish of restoring the Kaaba to its state in the time of Abraham (عليه السلام). But he did not, for the reason he explained to the Mother of the Believers, Aisha (عائشة), may Allah be pleased with her, in the authentic hadith:

"O Aisha, were your people not recently from a time of ignorance (Jahiliyyah), I would have demolished the Kaaba and joined it to the ground, and I would have made for it two doors: an eastern door and a western door. And I would have added to it six cubits from the Hijr, for the Quraysh made it smaller when they built the Kaaba." (Agreed upon - متفق عليه).

This hadith reveals a delicate balance between benefit and harm:

"His statement, 'were your people not recently from a time of ignorance,' is not just a reference to their recent conversion to Islam, but a precise diagnosis of a deep psychological, creedal, and social condition. Jahiliyyah was not merely disbelief; it was an integrated system of customs, tribalism, and boasting of lineage and status. The rebuilding of the Kaaba by Quraysh was one of their greatest points of pride. Had the Prophet (صلى الله عليه وسلم) demolished the Kaaba, they would not have viewed it from the perspective of correcting the structure to its Abrahamic origin. Instead, they would have seen it from a purely ignorant perspective: 'Muhammad has destroyed our pride and glory to establish glory for himself.' This was the entry point for Satan that the Prophet (صلى الله عليه وسلم) feared, as it would strike the chord of tribalism and جاهلي pride that had not yet been fully eradicated from the souls of many."

  • The Benefit (Acquiring Benefits): Rebuilding the Kaaba on Abraham's foundations, facilitating entry and exit for people, and completing the House as Allah first intended. This is undoubtedly a great religious benefit.
  • The Harm (Warding off Harms): The harm the Prophet (صلى الله عليه وسلم) feared was not just a dispute, but a trial (fitna) in the religion that could lead to apostasy. Quraysh and those around them were new to Islam, and faith had not yet been firmly established in their hearts. If he had demolished the Kaaba, Satan would have found a wide-open door to whisper to them: "Look, Muhammad was not a prophet, but was ambitious for worldly matters. After gaining power over you, he began demolishing your sanctities to achieve personal glory." This doubt was capable of shaking their weak faith and opening a door to apostasy and disbelief.

Here, the great prophetic wisdom is manifest: preserving the people's faith and safeguarding their creed is the greatest of all interests, and it takes precedence over any other interest, no matter how great, even if it relates to the holiest place on Earth. The Kaaba remaining in an incomplete form with the people remaining firm in their faith was better than completing its structure at the risk of them being tested in their religion.

This prophetic stance teaches us a practical lesson in the concept of "moral incapacity" (العجز المعنوي). Incapacity is not always physical. A person may have the full ability to do something but refrains, not from weakness, but because they see that the moral, social, or religious harm resulting from their action is far greater than the benefit hoped for. We use this delicate balance in our daily lives without realizing it. The young man who is able to travel abroad for a better-paying job may refrain, becoming "morally incapable" of traveling because his sick mother needs him; caring for his mother is a greater interest than his material gain. Similarly, a merciful father holds his child for a painful injection; he overcomes the temporary harm of his child's pain and crying because the benefit of his healing is greater and more lasting. The Prophet (صلى الله عليه وسلم) was physically capable of demolishing the Kaaba, but he was morally incapable due to the fear of causing a trial in the people's faith.

This موقف نبوي teaches us a precise jurisprudence needed especially in times of tribulation, as expressed by Sheikh al-Islam Ibn Taymiyyah (شيخ الإسلام ابن تيمية): "The wise person is not the one who knows good from evil, but the one who knows the better of two goods and the worse of two evils." In many cases, the choice is not between pure good and pure evil, but between a good that carries some harm, or an evil that prevents a greater evil. The wise person chooses the action whose good outweighs its harm and avoids the action whose harm outweighs its good, even if it means tolerating some minor harm. The ignorant person, however, focuses on this minor harm and condemns its doer, ignoring the great good that was achieved or the greater evil that was prevented.

Evidence: One of the clearest Quranic proofs of this principle is the story of Al-Khidr (الخضر) with Moses (موسى), peace be upon them. Al-Khidr's actions appeared evil and corrupt on the surface, but their inner reality was to prevent a greater evil. Allah says regarding the scuttling of the boat:

﴾As for the ship, it belonged to poor people working at sea. So I intended to cause defect in it as there was after them a king who seized every [good] ship by force.﴿ [Al-Kahf: 79]
(﴿أَمَّا السَّفِينَةُ فَكَانَتْ لِمَسَكِينَ يَعْمَلُونَ فِي الْبَحْرِ فَأَرَدتُّ أَنْ أَعِيبَهَا وَكَانَ وَرَاءَهُم مَّلِكٌ يَأْخُذُ كُلَّ سَفِينَةٍ غَصْبًا﴾)
The harm of damaging the ship (a minor evil) was to ward off the harm of it being seized completely (a greater evil).

Grounding the Principle in the Quran and Sunnah

This prophetic stance is a practical application of a major jurisprudential principle derived from the texts of the Sharia: "Warding off harm is مقدم on acquiring benefits."

From the Holy Quran: This principle is clear in Allah's words about wine and gambling:

﴾They ask you about wine and gambling. Say, "In them is great sin and [yet, some] benefit for people. But their sin is greater than their benefit."﴿ [Al-Baqarah: 219]
(﴿يَسْأَلُونَكَ عَنِ الْخَمْرِ وَالْمَيْسِرِ ۖ قُلْ فِيهِمَا إِثْمٌ كَبِيرٌ وَمَنَافِعُ لِلنَّاسِ وَإِثْمُهُمَا أَكْبَرُ مِن نَّفْعِهِمَا﴾)
Allah acknowledges that there are "benefits" in wine (like trade) and gambling, but He forbade them because their "sin" (the resulting harms of losing one's mind, enmity, and hatred) is greater than their benefit.

Another magnificent Quranic proof is the verse:

﴾And do not insult those they invoke other than Allah, lest they insult Allah in enmity without knowledge.﴿ [Al-An'am: 108]
(﴿وَلَا تَسُبُّوا الَّذِينَ يَدْعُونَ مِن دُونِ اللَّهِ فَيَسُبُّوا اللَّهَ عَدْوًا بِغَيْرِ عِلْمٍ﴾)
In this verse, Allah forbids the believers from an apparent benefit—insulting the false gods of the polytheists—to ward off a greater and more dangerous harm: the polytheists responding by insulting Allah out of ignorance and aggression.

From the Prophetic Sunnah: Another application of this principle is the Prophet's (صلى الله عليه وسلم) refusal to kill the leader of the hypocrites, Abdullah ibn Ubayy ibn Salul (عبد الله بن أبي بن سلول), despite knowing his hypocrisy and the harm he caused the Muslims. When Umar ibn al-Khattab (عمر بن الخطاب) suggested killing him, the Prophet (صلى الله عليه وسلم) said: "Leave him, so that people will not say that Muhammad kills his companions." (Agreed upon - متفق عليه). The benefit of killing the hypocrite and warding off his evil was outweighed by the greater harm of tarnishing Islam's image and deterring people from entering it.

What Happened Afterwards?

Interestingly, this prophetic wish was briefly fulfilled during the era of the great companion Abdullah ibn al-Zubayr (عبد الله بن الزبير), may Allah be pleased with them both. When he assumed the caliphate in Mecca, based on his knowledge of the hadith from his aunt Aisha, he demolished the Kaaba and rebuilt it on the foundations of Abraham, incorporating the Hijr and giving it two doors at ground level.

However, after he was killed by Al-Hajjaj ibn Yusuf al-Thaqafi (الحجاج بن يوسف الثقفي), Al-Hajjaj wrote to the Umayyad Caliph Abd al-Malik ibn Marwan (عبد الملك بن مروان), who, thinking Ibn al-Zubayr had innovated, ordered Al-Hajjaj to return the Kaaba to its state during the time of Quraysh. Al-Hajjaj demolished the added section, sealed the western door, and raised the eastern door.

Later, when the Abbasid caliphs learned of the authentic hadith, some of them (like Al-Mahdi or Harun al-Rashid) wanted to demolish it and rebuild it according to Ibn al-Zubayr's construction. However, Imam Malik ibn Anas (الإمام مالك بن أنس) advised against it, saying his famous words: "O Commander of the Faithful, do not make the Kaaba a plaything for kings, such that anyone who wishes to change it does so." The caliph heeded his advice, and the Kaaba remained in its current form, applying the same principle: warding off the harm of tampering with the House of Allah and shaking its sanctity in people's hearts took precedence over the benefit of restoring it to its original form.

Recap: Wisdom of the Kaaba's Reconstruction

  • Prophetic Principle: The decision not to rebuild the Kaaba on its original Abrahamic foundations teaches a key Islamic legal principle: "Warding off harm takes precedence over acquiring benefits."
  • The Dilemma: The benefit was restoring the Kaaba's original form. The potential harm was causing a great trial (fitna) for the new Muslims of Quraysh, who might think the Prophet was destroying their heritage for personal glory, potentially leading them to apostasy.
  • The Wisdom: Preserving the faith of the people was a greater priority than perfecting the physical structure of the building. This demonstrates a deep understanding of human psychology and priorities in leadership.
  • Historical Legacy: The Kaaba's form has remained, reflecting a consensus on preventing it from becoming a "plaything for kings," thereby protecting its sanctity.

Harbingers of Dawn: The Great Precursors that Paved the Way for the Final Messenger

The commission of Prophet Muhammad (صلى الله عليه وسلم) was not a sudden event in human history but the culmination of divine preparation. These "harbingers" (Irhasat - إرهاصات) are the early signs and extraordinary portents that precede a prophet's appearance, heralding their imminent mission and alerting minds and hearts to receive the coming light.

1. The Migration of the Jews to Yathrib: An Anticipation of Prophethood

The presence of significant Jewish tribes (like Banu Qurayza, Banu al-Nadir, and Banu Qaynuqa) in the heart of the Arabian desert in Yathrib (المدينة المنورة) was not random. Their ancestral homeland was in the Levant and Palestine. Their migration was driven by clear prophecies in their scriptures about the final prophet, describing his place of migration as a "land between two harras (volcanic fields) with palm trees." This description perfectly fits Yathrib. They came to wait for the awaited Prophet, hoping to be the first to believe in and support him, thereby gaining leadership over the Arabs.

This anticipation was part of their daily culture and a psychological weapon against their neighbors, the tribes of Aws (الأوس) and Khazraj (الخزرج). Whenever they were losing in battle, they would pray for victory by him, saying, "O Allah, grant us victory over them by the Prophet who will be sent in the end of times." The Quran describes this precisely:

﴾And when there came to them a Book from Allah confirming that which was with them - although before they used to pray for victory against those who disbelieved - but when there came to them that which they recognized, they disbelieved in it. So the curse of Allah is upon the disbelievers.﴿ [Al-Baqarah: 89]
(﴿وَلَمَّا جَاءَهُمْ كِتَابٌ مِّنْ عِندِ اللَّهِ مُصَدِّقٌ لِّمَا مَعَهُمْ وَكَانُوا مِن قَبْلُ يَسْتَفْتِحُونَ عَلَى الَّذِينَ كَفَرُوا فَلَمَّا جَاءَهُم مَّا عَرَفُوا كَفَرُوا بِهِ ۚ فَلَعْنَةُ اللَّهِ عَلَى الْكَافِرِينَ﴾)

This 'recognition' followed by 'disbelief' is evident in the account of Safiyyah bint Huyayy (صفية بنت حيي بن أخطب), who related that after the Prophet's arrival in Medina, her father and uncle went to see him. They returned exhausted and disheartened. She heard her uncle ask her father, "Is it him?" He replied, "Yes, by Allah." The uncle asked, "Do you recognize him and affirm it?" He replied, "Yes." He then asked, "So what do you feel towards him?" Her father replied, "Enmity, by Allah, as long as I live." This dialogue reveals that their disbelief was not from doubt, but from certainty followed by envy and arrogance, making their long wait a more profound harbinger and a stronger proof against them.

By Allah's wisdom, this constant talk of the awaited Prophet prepared the hearts of the people of Yathrib. When a delegation from Aws and Khazraj met the Prophet (صلى الله عليه وسلم) in Mecca, they said to one another, "This is, by Allah, the Prophet whom the Jews have been warning you about. Do not let them beat you to him." Thus, they were among the quickest to believe.

2. The Guarding of the Heavens with Shooting Stars: Cutting off News from the Unseen

Before the Prophet's mission, divination and soothsaying were widespread, aided by jinn who would eavesdrop on news from the heavens. They would snatch a word of truth and mix it with a hundred lies. However, as the Prophet's commission drew near, this cosmic system was fundamentally altered. The heavens were intensely guarded by angels and shooting stars, completely sealing off the channels of eavesdropping. This was a divine measure to protect the final revelation from any distortion and to nullify the arguments of soothsayers. The jinn themselves noticed this sudden and dramatic change, as the Quran relates from their perspective:

﴾And we have sought [to reach] the heaven but found it filled with powerful guards and burning flames. * And we used to sit therein in positions for hearing, but whoever listens now will find a burning flame lying in wait for 1him.﴿ [Al-Jinn: 8-9]
(﴿وَأَنَّا لَمَسْنَا السَّمَاءَ فَوَجَدْنَاهَا مُلِئَتْ حَرَسًا شَدِيدًا وَشُهُبًا * وَأَنَّا كُنَّا نَقْعُدُ مِنْهَا مَقَاعِدَ لِلسَّمْعِ ۖ فَمَن يَسْتَمِعِ الْآنَ يَجِدْ لَهُ شِهَابًا رَّصَدًا﴾)

This cosmic event was not hidden; it was a general phenomenon that caught everyone's attention, preparing minds for a momentous event that would change the world.

3. The True Dream and Love of Seclusion: The Prophet's Spiritual Preparation

The descent of revelation was not abrupt but was preceded by intense spiritual and psychological preparation for the Prophet (صلى الله عليه وسلم).

  • The True Dream: Six months before the angel Gabriel's (جبريل) arrival, the first stage of revelation began with true dreams. Aisha (عائشة) said, "The first with which the revelation to the Messenger of Allah (صلى الله عليه وسلم) began was the true dream in his sleep. He would not see a dream except that it came true like the breaking of dawn." (Agreed upon - متفق عليه). These dreams were a prelude and training for his heart to receive the revelation. The 23 years of prophethood comprise 46 half-year periods. This six-month phase of true dreams constitutes one of these 46 parts, confirming its status as a form of divine inspiration. The Prophet (صلى الله عليه وسلم) said: "The righteous dream is one of the forty-six parts of prophethood." (Agreed upon - متفق عليه).
  • Love of Seclusion and Worship (Tahannuth): During this same period, he (صلى الله عليه وسلم) was made to love seclusion. Distressed by the idolatry and corruption he saw, he would retreat to the cave of Hira (غار حراء) on the Mountain of Light (جبل النور) to spend nights in worship (yatahannath - يتحنث). This was not a prescribed form of worship but a retreat for contemplation, reflection upon the Creator, and purification of the soul. This seclusion was a necessary "disconnection" from the material world and a deep "connection" with the spiritual realm to prepare him for the great task ahead.

This seclusion was not an escape from reality but a divine method of nurturing. Mecca was a noisy hub of trade, poetry, pride, and idolatry. To prepare the Prophet's heart to be a pure vessel for Allah's word, it first had to be 'emptied' of all this material and social noise. The seclusion in Hira was a divine isolation of the soul from all earthly influences, allowing it to become pure and qualified for contact with the celestial assembly. This is Allah's way with His chosen ones; Moses (موسى عليه السلام) was appointed for a forty-night seclusion on Mount Tur before Allah spoke to him directly. For Prophet Muhammad (صلى الله عليه وسلم), this seclusion was the stage of purification before the stage of commissioning.

The Momentous Encounter: The Beginning of the Revelation's Descent

On the Night of Decree in that year, while he was in the cave of Hira, Gabriel (جبريل عليه السلام) came to him for the first time in the form of a man. The scene was awe-inspiring and terrifying. The Prophet (صلى الله عليه وسلم), alone in a desolate place, was suddenly approached by a stranger who commanded him, "Read!" (اقْرَأْ!). The unlettered Prophet, who could not read, replied, "I am not a reader" (مَا أَنَا بِقَارِئٍ).

Aisha (رضي الله عنها) describes what happened: "He took me and embraced me forcefully until I was exhausted, then he released me and said, 'Read!' I said, 'I am not a reader.' He took me a second time and embraced me forcefully until I was exhausted, then he released me and said, 'Read!' I said, 'I am not a reader.' He took me a third time and embraced me, then he released me and said:

﴾Read in the name of your Lord who created * Created man from a clinging substance. * Read, and your Lord is the most Generous * Who taught by the pen * Taught man that which he knew not.﴿ [Al-Alaq: 1-5]
(﴿اقْرَأْ بِاسْمِ رَبِّكَ الَّذِي خَلَقَ * خَلَقَ الْإِنسَانَ مِنْ عَلَقٍ * اقْرَأْ وَرَبُّكَ الْأَكْرَمُ * الَّذِي عَلَّمَ بِالْقَلَمِ * عَلَّمَ الْإِنسَانَ مَا لَمْ يَعْلَمْ﴾)

Herein lies one of Islam's greatest miracles: the first word of the final message is "Read," revealed to an unlettered Prophet in a predominantly illiterate nation. It was a resounding message from the first moment that this nation would be a nation of knowledge. The message began by establishing that all knowledge must be connected to God, "in the name of your Lord" (بِاسْمِ رَبِّكَ), directed towards His pleasure.

This forceful embrace (الغط) served several great purposes:

  • To awaken all his senses, transitioning him from his normal human state to one of full readiness to receive the weighty Word of Allah.
  • To confirm the reality of the encounter, removing any doubt that it was a dream or illusion. It was a real meeting with a being of immense physical power.
  • A symbolic gesture, as if to empty his noble heart of all worldly matters to prepare it for what would fill it with the words of the Lord of the heavens.

He returned to his beloved wife Khadijah (خديجة رضي الله عنها), his heart trembling, saying, "Cover me, cover me" (زَمِّلُونِي، زَمِّلُونِي). Here, the role of the wise, righteous wife shines. She did not panic but received him with wisdom and tranquility, speaking her immortal words that were the first to comfort his heart: "Nay, by Allah, Allah will never disgrace you. You keep good relations with your kith and kin, help the poor and the destitute, serve your guests generously, and assist those afflicted by calamity."

The stance of Lady Khadijah (رضي الله عنها) presents an integrated model of crisis management. Her methodical steps were:

  1. Immediate Containment: She responded to his direct request, "Cover me," providing physical security first.
  2. Emotional and Rational Stabilization: After he calmed down, she offered a logical argument based on his character. She turned his moral record into a source of strength and reassurance.
  3. Seeking Expert Consultation: Recognizing the matter was beyond her expertise, she took him straight to a reliable person of knowledge at that time, her cousin Waraqah ibn Nawfal.

She was truly a minister of truth on the very first day of prophethood, providing psychological support, logical analysis, and a practical solution.

She then took him to her cousin Waraqah ibn Nawfal (ورقة بن نوفل), an elderly man who had become a Christian in the pre-Islamic era and could read the Hebrew scriptures. When the Prophet (صلى الله عليه وسلم) told him what he had seen, Waraqah said without hesitation, "This is the Namus (الناموس) whom Allah sent to Moses (موسى)." (Namus means the keeper of secrets, referring to Gabriel). With this, Waraqah connected the message of Muhammad (صلى الله عليه وسلم) to that of Moses (عليه السلام), affirming its single divine source.

But Waraqah did not only bring good tidings; he also warned him, saying words that shocked the Prophet (صلى الله عليه وسلم): "I wish I were young and could be alive when your people will drive you out." The Prophet (صلى الله عليه وسلم), known as the Trustworthy and the Truthful, asked in astonishment, "Will they drive me out?" (أوَمُخرجيّ هم؟). Waraqah's reply was a historical law and a divine custom for the calls of all prophets: "Yes. No man has ever come with what you have brought without being met with hostility."

These words from Waraqah were the first psychological preparation for the Prophet (صلى الله عليه وسلم) for the next stage—the stage of proclaiming the truth and the enmity, persecution, and expulsion that would follow. From the very first day, it was made known that the path would not be strewn with roses.

Here we stand at the threshold of a new era in human history, one that would change the face of the world forever. In our next session, Allah willing, we will discuss the period of the pause in revelation, the stages of the secret and public call to Islam, and other related events.

May Allah reward you with good.

Recap: The Eve of Revelation

  • Harbingers (Irhasat): Signs like the Jewish migration to Yathrib, the guarding of the heavens with shooting stars, and the Prophet's true dreams prepared the world and the Prophet himself for the final message.
  • Spiritual Seclusion: His retreat to the Cave of Hira was a divine preparation, purifying his soul and connecting him with the spiritual realm, away from the noise of Jahiliyyah.
  • The First Revelation: The encounter with Gabriel in the cave marked the beginning of the message with the command "Read!", establishing knowledge as the foundation of Islam.
  • The First Support System: Khadijah's wise and comforting response, and Waraqah's scholarly confirmation, provided the crucial initial support and context for the Prophet's mission.
  • The Prophetic Path: Waraqah's warning foretold the inevitable hostility and struggle that accompanies the call to truth, preparing the Prophet from day one for the challenges ahead.

session 9: The Prophetic Biography, a Way of Life

Praise be to Allah, the Lord of the Worlds. I bear witness that there is no god but Allah, alone without a partner, and I bear witness that Muhammad is His servant and Messenger. May the peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, his family, and his companions in abundance.

To proceed:

The study of the fragrant Prophetic Biography (the Seerah) is not an intellectual luxury or a mere review of past events. Rather, it is a great challenge that confronts us: to transform this rich biography into a practical way of life. The ultimate goal of studying the life of its subject, upon whom be the best of prayers and peace, is not for it to be a mere historical narration of events, but for it to be a beacon that illuminates our paths, identifies our real problems, and presents us with healing solutions for what we face in our contemporary reality.

Just as the Holy Quran is a vast ocean full of treasures, requiring a guide to lead us to the sources of solutions within it, so too is the Prophetic Biography. It is a precious treasure that has been told and presented many times, yet it still requires much effort and contemplation to become a true remedy and guidance, especially for the problems of the youth and the challenges of our age.

Therefore, the desired goal of this series is that no event from the Seerah passes us by without us having clarified its profound effect on the heart and its direct reflection on behavior, thought, and creed. It is an invitation to live by the biography of the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, not just to read it. We ask Allah, the Glorified and Exalted, for acceptance and success.

The Divine Preparation for Prophethood: Seclusion in the Cave of Hira

Before the earth met the heavens in that great encounter, and before the revelation descended with words that changed the course of history, there was a divine preparation and heavenly readiness for the one who would carry the greatest trust, Muhammad ibn Abdullah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him. Prophethood was not a sudden event in the life of an ordinary man; rather, it was the culmination of a long phase of preparation, divine protection, and guidance.

The Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, lived in a society of polytheism and ignorance (Jahiliyyah), but his pure innate nature rejected and detested what his people were upon. He was discontent with their condition, seeing idols being worshipped besides Allah, alcohol being consumed, usury being practiced, and immorality spreading, and his pure heart was repulsed by all of that. He was upon the remnants of the religion of his father Ibrahim, peace be upon him, a hanif who worshipped Allah alone. He knew in his heart that what his people were doing was falsehood and misguidance, but as Allah, the Exalted, said:

﴾And thus We have revealed to you an inspiration of Our command. You did not know what the Book was or what faith was.﴿ [Ash-Shura: 52]
(﴿وَكَذَٰلِكَ أَوْحَيْنَا إِلَيْكَ رُوحًا مِّنْ أَمْرِنَا ۚ مَا كُنتَ تَدْرِي مَا الْكِتَابُ وَلَا الْإِيمَانُ﴾)

This means he did not know the details of the divine law and the correct path out of this darkness.

In the midst of this environment, Allah, the Exalted, inspired him with a matter that was the beginning of the direct preparation to receive the revelation: seclusion and solitude. The Mother of the Believers, Aisha, may Allah be pleased with her, says in the hadith about the beginning of revelation:

"Then, seclusion was made beloved to him. He used to go in seclusion in the cave of Hira where he used to worship (Allah alone) continuously for many days and nights before returning to his family, and he would take provisions for that. Then he would return to Khadijah to take provisions for the like period" (Agreed upon).

This seclusion holds within it great wisdom and profound lessons, including:

First: Protecting the Heart from the Familiarity of Sin

Constant mixing with sinners leads, after a period, to a dulling of the senses and the loss of the heart's fervor in denouncing evil. One begins by denouncing the wrong, then the denunciation weakens, then one becomes accustomed to it, and then one may even fall into it. This seclusion was like periodic maintenance for the heart of the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him. By distancing himself from the scenes of polytheism and immorality, the flame of hatred for sin remained alight in his heart.

This is a lesson for us today. Whoever lives in an environment full of violations, whether at work or in the street, needs periods of "separation" and spiritual isolation to recharge their heart and renew its vitality. Anyone who has experienced I'tikaf (seclusion in the mosque) in Ramadan understands this meaning well. After ten days of worship and purity, one comes out into the world and finds things strange that they had been accustomed to before, which is proof that their heart has regained its spiritual fervor and sensitivity towards evil.

Second: Spiritual Preparation for Receiving the Great Command

This seclusion was a period of contemplation and reflection on the kingdom of the heavens and the earth, a time to purify the soul and empty the heart of worldly distractions and people's concerns, to be ready to receive the word of Allah, the Mighty and Majestic. The revelation is heavy, as Allah, the Exalted, said:

﴾Indeed, We will cast upon you a heavy word.﴿ [Al-Muzzammil: 5]
(﴿إِنَّا سَنُلْقِي عَلَيْكَ قَوْلًا ثَقِيلًا﴾)

This heavy word requires a great soul prepared for it. These nights in the cave of Hira were the intensive preparatory course that Allah chose for His Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him.

The choice of the cave of Hira, the love of seclusion, and the month of Ramadan were not mere coincidences. Rather, it was divine guidance and inspiration, and a subtle arrangement to prepare the best of His creation for the greatest of messages. It was a divine prelude to this momentous event, through which the world would transition from one state to another, from the darkness of ignorance to the light of Islam.

The First Encounter: The Descent of Jibril and the Beginning of Revelation

In the quiet depths of a night in the blessed month of Ramadan, atop the Mountain of Light overlooking Makkah, was Muhammad ibn Abdullah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him. He had reached the age of forty, the age of perfection and maturity, detached from the hustle of life and the misguidance of his people, immersed in his contemplation and worship. He did not know that this night would not be like any other. It was an appointment with destiny; it was the Night of Decree (Laylat al-Qadr), which is ﴾better than a thousand months﴿, in which ﴾The angels and the Spirit descend therein by permission of their Lord for every matter﴿ [Al-Qadr: 3-4]. In those sublime moments, the era of preparation and waiting ended, and the greatest dialogue between heaven and earth began.

The Mother of the Believers, Aisha, may Allah be pleased with her, the closest witness to these events from the mouth of the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, himself, narrates the details of this awe-inspiring encounter, saying: "...until the Truth came to him while he was in the cave of Hira. The angel came to him..." The "Truth" came to him, which is the clear and indubitable revelation. Here, the chapters of the scene begin:

1. The Sudden Appearance and the Wisdom of the Human Form

In that secluded place, which required effort and hardship to ascend and was not frequented by people, the angel appeared to him suddenly. The mere appearance of a stranger at this time and in this place was enough to cause intense fear. But out of Allah's mercy and kindness to His Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, Jibril, peace be upon him, came to him in the form of a man, so that the shock of the surprise would not be combined with the shock of seeing the magnificent angelic form. This was the first touch of divine kindness in this momentous situation, to enable the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, to comprehend what was about to happen.

2. The First Divine Command: "Read" and the Three Embraces

The angel initiated by saying: "Read." It was a direct and decisive command. The reply came from the unlettered Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, who had never read a book nor written a letter with his right hand, a sincere and spontaneous response expressing his reality: "I am not a reader." It was not a refusal, but an expression of astonishment and a statement of fact.

Here, the unexpected happened. The narration says: "So he took hold of me and squeezed me until I was exhausted, then he released me." It was not an ordinary embrace, but a severe, violent squeeze that shook his very being, despite being known for his immense physical strength. For the embrace to reach such a point—"until I was exhausted"—means it was beyond human capacity. This powerful act was like a shock to bring him out of his ordinary human state and prepare him to receive the extraordinary. It was also a physical confirmation that what he was experiencing was not a dream or an illusion, but a tangible reality.

The command and the embrace were repeated three times, each time increasing in intensity, and each time the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, answered with the same sincerity: "I am not a reader." In these three embraces lies a great symbolic message and a practical lesson in the methodology of prophethood:

  • It is a message that this religion would be built on effort, hardship, and intense struggle.
  • It is a sign that the path of calling to Allah would pass through hardships and trials that almost suffocate, but as soon as the hardship reaches its peak, relief comes from Allah. It is as if each embrace represents a stage of great distress (like the day of Ta'if, the siege in the valley, or the battle of Uhud), and each release afterward represents the resolution of the crisis and the victory of the truth.

3. The Revelation of the First Verses of Light

After the third embrace, and after the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, was physically and psychologically prepared, the angel released him and recited to him the first words of revelation that would remain eternal until the Day of Judgment:

﴾Read in the name of your Lord who created (1) Created man from a clinging substance (2) Read, and your Lord is the most Generous (3) Who taught by the pen (4) Taught man that which he knew not (5)﴿ [Al-'Alaq: 1-5].
(﴿اقْرَأْ بِاسْمِ رَبِّكَ الَّذِي خَلَقَ (1) خَلَقَ الْإِنسَانَ مِنْ عَلَقٍ (2) اقْرَأْ وَرَبُّكَ الْأَكْرَمُ (3) الَّذِي عَلَّمَ بِالْقَلَمِ (4) عَلَّمَ الْإِنسَانَ مَا لَمْ يَعْلَمْ (5)﴾)

These five verses were a declaration of the entire divine methodology.

  • They linked reading and knowledge from the very first moment to the name of Allah, the Creator, to announce that any knowledge that does not lead to Allah is deficient.
  • They reminded man of his humble origin, ﴾from a clinging substance﴿, to discipline his pride and remind him of his constant need for his Creator.
  • They then coupled the command to read a second time with the attribute of divine generosity, ﴾and your Lord is the most Generous﴿, as if it were a message of reassurance to the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, that the Lord who tasked him with this heavy mission is the Most Generous, who will aid him and supply him with strength and knowledge.
  • They concluded by honoring knowledge and its tool, ﴾taught by the pen﴿, in a nation that was mostly illiterate, to be a declaration that the civilization of Islam would be founded on knowledge and learning.

4. The Aftermath of the Encounter

The encounter ended, and the angel disappeared. Lady Aisha says: "The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, returned with it, his heart trembling." The Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, returned from his seclusion not just carrying words, but carrying the weight of the message of the heavens, carrying speech with an authority and majesty unlike the speech of humans. He descended from the cave quickly, his heart pounding intensely, his body shivering, not knowing the nature of what had happened to him. All he knew was that he had gone through a great and awe-inspiring experience. From that moment, the story of prophethood and the message began—the story of struggle and proclamation that would change the face of the world forever.

The Wisdom of the Prophet's Illiteracy: Perfection in a Trait of 'Deficiency'

When the first divine command came to the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, with the word "Read," his sincere and innate answer was: "I am not a reader." This sentence is not merely a statement of fact; it is a key to understanding one of the greatest proofs of his prophethood and the brightest evidence of the truthfulness of his message: the quality of being "unlettered" (ummi). Some might think, especially in our age that sanctifies reading and writing, that illiteracy is a deficiency. Some may even be hesitant to describe the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, with it, trying to find alternative interpretations for the word "al-ummi" mentioned in the Quran. But the truth is that his being unlettered, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, was an intended perfection and a masterful divine plan, not a flaw or a defect. The wisdom of this is manifested in several ways:

1. The Conclusive Proof of the Divine Source of the Message

The greatest wisdom in the Prophet's, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, illiteracy is that it makes the Holy Quran a self-evident miracle that can only be explained as a revelation from Allah. How could an unlettered man, who grew up in an unlettered nation, who never read a book nor wrote a letter, and who never sat with a teacher or a scholar, suddenly bring forth this miraculous book—in its eloquence, its legislation, its sciences, and its news of the unseen—with which he challenged the most articulate and eloquent Arabs, who were unable to produce anything like it?

A fair mind finds no answer except that this Quran is not from him, but is a revelation from the All-Wise, the Praiseworthy. His illiteracy, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, was the divine seal on his message, confirming that every word he uttered from the Quran was purely a revelation from Allah, as He, the Exalted, said:

﴾Nor does he speak from [his own] inclination. (3) It is not but a revelation revealed. (4)﴿ [An-Najm: 3-4].
(﴿وَمَا يَنطِقُ عَنِ الْهَوَىٰ (3) إِنْ هُوَ إِلَّا وَحْيٌ يُوحَىٰ (4)﴾)

2. Cutting Off the Path for Doubters and Falsifiers

Allah knew with His eternal knowledge that some would come to doubt this message and claim that Muhammad, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, had derived his Quran from the books of the ancients or learned from the People of the Book. So his illiteracy came to demolish this suspicion from its roots and close this door completely. The Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, lived for forty years among his people; they knew his comings and goings and were certain that he had never read a book. The Quran came to establish this irrefutable proof:

﴾And you did not recite before it any scripture, nor did you inscribe one with your right hand. Otherwise, the falsifiers would have had [cause for] doubt.﴿ [Al-'Ankabut: 48].
(﴿وَمَا كُنتَ تَتْلُو مِن قَبْلِهِ مِن كِتَابٍ وَلَا تَخُطُّهُ بِيَمِينِكَ ۖ إِذًا لَّارْتَابَ الْمُبْطِلُونَ﴾)

The meaning is clear and decisive: O Muhammad, had you been a reader and a writer, the doubters and falsifiers would have found an opening for doubt and suspicion and would have said, "He read the books of the previous peoples and copied from them." But because you were not, their argument is refuted, their doubt is baseless, and nothing remains for them but to submit that this Quran is ﴾clear verses [memorized] within the breasts of those who have been given knowledge﴿ [Al-'Ankabut: 49]. It is received through revelation, not by reading, and it is preserved first in the hearts, not on pages.

3. Illiteracy as Perfection in its Historical Context

Judging illiteracy as a deficiency is a judgment stemming from the culture of our time. However, in the Arab environment of that era, it was not necessarily so. The Arab nation was an oral and narrative one, relying on strong memory and eloquent tongues, not on the pen and the book. Their memories were their records, and their poems were their collections. Therefore, not knowing how to read and write was not synonymous with ignorance; many of their wise men, poets, and leaders did not read or write.

The Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, referred to this nature in his saying:

"We are an unlettered nation; we neither write nor calculate" (Agreed upon).

This is not a call to ignorance, but a description of the state they were in, which made their reliance on memorization and direct reception the norm. In this atmosphere, his, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, illiteracy was a natural trait that did not arouse surprise, but at the same time, it was preparing the way for the greatest miracle.

Thus, it becomes clear to us that the illiteracy of the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, was not an incidental trait but was part of the masterful divine plan, a profound proof, a protective shield for the message, and a shining demonstration that this noble Prophet was none other than a Messenger from Allah, receiving His revelation pure and clear from the heavens, to convey it to the people as it was revealed to him.

The Role of Lady Khadijah (may Allah be pleased with her): The Righteous Wife in Times of Crisis

The Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, returned from the cave of Hira, the place that had witnessed the greatest transformation in human history. He did not return as he had ascended; he returned carrying the weight of the first revelation, his heart pounding from the terror of what he had seen and heard, and his pure body shivering as he called out at the top of his voice: "Zammilooni, zammilooni" (Cover me, cover me).

In this critical moment, where fear mixed with awe, and astonishment with reverence, representing the most difficult and strangest experience a human could endure, the true mettle and the firm mountain, Lady Khadijah bint Khuwaylid, may Allah be pleased with her, emerged. She was not just a wife; she was a divine destiny that Allah sent to His Prophet to be his first support, his protective shield, and his compassionate heart. Her great role is manifested in several areas:

First: Wise Tranquility and Spiritual Affirmation

She did not meet him with screams or panic, nor did she increase his anxiety with eager questions. Instead, she received his fear with tranquility, his shivering with embrace, and his distress with steadfastness and wisdom. She was a true source of tranquility (sakan) for him, as Allah described spouses:

﴾And of His signs is that He created for you from yourselves mates that you may find tranquility in them; and He placed between you affection and mercy.﴿ [Ar-Rum: 21]
(﴿وَمِنْ آيَاتِهِ أَنْ خَلَقَ لَكُم مِّنْ أَنفُسِكُمْ أَزْوَاجًا لِّتَسْكُنُوا إِلَيْهَا وَجَعَلَ بَيْنَكُم مَّوَدَّةً وَرَحْمَةً﴾)

After the terror had subsided and he told her what had happened, she spoke her immortal words, which were the first affirmation for the heart of the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him. These were not just words of emotional comfort, but an intellectual proof and a faithful methodology, stemming from a deep knowledge of his character that had lasted for fifteen years before this moment:

"Never! By Allah, Allah will never disgrace you. You keep good relations with your kith and kin, help the poor and the destitute, serve your guests generously, and assist the deserving calamity-afflicted ones." (Agreed upon).

She established a conclusive logical argument: Allah, who is Truth and Justice, could never abandon or disgrace someone with such qualities. She deduced from his beautiful actions towards creation the beautiful care of the Creator for him, thereby establishing a great principle in life: "Good deeds protect from evil fates."

Second: Financial Support and Complete Sacrifice

Her support was not only moral but also absolute material support. Lady Khadijah, the pure one, the leader of the women of Quraysh, was a wealthy and noble businesswoman. From the first moment she believed in her husband's message, she dedicated herself and all her wealth to the service of this call. In the early years of Islam, when the Muslims were a small, oppressed minority, Khadijah's wealth was the fuel that sustained the call. She was the one who consoled him with her wealth when people deprived him, thus becoming the first to sacrifice wealth for the sake of Allah. This sacrifice reached its peak during the harsh years of the boycott in the valley of Abu Talib, where she spent her entire fortune to feed and protect the Banu Hashim and the besieged Muslims with them until she had nothing left.

Third: The Courage of Faith and Being the First to Believe

The greatest thing Lady Khadijah offered was being the very first to believe. Not just the first woman, but the first human being to affirm the message. Imagine the weight of that moment: to be the first person to believe in a message that would change the face of the world and challenge established beliefs and traditions. This required rare courage, absolute trust in her husband's truthfulness, and unwavering faith in Allah. With this precedence, she became the cornerstone in the building of the nation of Islam.

Fourth: Unending Loyalty and an Immortal Memory

Khadijah's role was not transient in the life of the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him; her impact was engraved in his heart and memory until the last day of his life. For many years after her death, the Prophet's, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, loyalty to her was proverbial. Lady Aisha, may Allah be pleased with her, used to say:

"I did not feel jealous of any of the wives of the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, as much as I did of Khadijah, although I had never seen her. But the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, used to mention her very often, and whenever he slaughtered a sheep, he would cut its parts and send them to the women friends of Khadijah" (Agreed upon).

When Aisha once asked him about the reason for mentioning her so much, he gave her the answer that summarizes all her virtues. Imam Ahmad narrated in his Musnad that he, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, said:

"Indeed, I was blessed with her love... She believed in me when the people disbelieved. She affirmed my truthfulness when the people called me a liar. She supported me with her wealth when the people withheld from me. And Allah, the Mighty and Majestic, blessed me with children from her when He deprived me of children from other women."

For all this, she earned a status no other woman reached. Allah the Exalted sent greetings of peace to her with Jibril and gave her the glad tidings of a house in Paradise made of hollowed pearls, "wherein there is no noise nor any toil" (Agreed upon). Just as her house in this world was a place of tranquility and comfort for the Messenger of Allah, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, without clamor or fatigue, Allah rewarded her with a house in Paradise of the same nature as her deed—a fitting reward.

Lady Khadijah, may Allah be pleased with her, was truly a ministry of truth in the most difficult of times, serving as the best example of a righteous wife, who is the best provision of this world.

A Divine Principle: Good Deeds Protect from Evil Fates

The words with which Lady Khadijah, may Allah be pleased with her, comforted the heart of the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, were not just passing consolation. They were a declaration of a great divine law and an ongoing divine custom (sunnah) in the universe: that goodness has a lasting effect, and kindness has an echo that is never lost. With the power of her certainty and pure nature, she established a principle that there are hidden links and a precise balance between what a person does and what is done to them.

This principle has two faces: a bright face that brings glad tidings of good, and a dark face that warns of evil.

The First Face: The Preserved Reward of Good

Every righteous deed you perform, every act of kindness you offer, and every distress you relieve from a Muslim are seeds you plant in the field of life. Allah the Exalted takes care of them to grow them for you as good fruits in this world and the Hereafter. You may think your deed has been lost or forgotten, but Allah does not forget. He, the Glorified, says:

﴾And whatever good you put forward for yourselves - you will find it with Allah. It is better and greater in reward.﴿ [Al-Muzzammil: 20]
(﴿وَمَا تُقَدِّمُوا لِأَنفُسِكُم مِّنْ خَيْرٍ تَجِدُوهُ عِندَ اللَّهِ هُوَ خَيْرًا وَأَعْظَمَ أَجْرًا﴾)

What is amazing about Allah's generosity is that the reward for this good may come to you from where you least expect. You might exert effort in a certain direction hoping for a specific result, but you don't see it, so you think your effort was in vain. But the truth is that Allah has saved your reward to grant it to you in a better form and at a more appropriate time.

Consider Lady Hajar, peace be upon her, running between Safa and Marwa searching for a drop of water for her infant, exerting her utmost effort in one direction. But the water did not spring from where she ran; rather, it gushed forth from under the feet of her child, Isma'il. Her effort was not lost; it was the reason for the spring's emergence, but from where Allah willed, not from where she expected.

And the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, himself went to Ta'if to call them to Allah, and they met him with the worst possible treatment, expelling him and throwing stones at him until his noble feet bled. He returned sad and worried, and on his way to Makkah, Allah directed a group of Jinn to him who listened to the Quran and believed in it. His toil was not in vain; the harvest came to him from another realm he had not anticipated.

Such is our life. Allah might bless you with a righteous spouse, good offspring, or a blessed job, or He might save you from a fatal accident, and you may not know that the reason could be a sincere prayer from your mother in the dead of night, a charity you concealed from people, or a distress you relieved for someone afflicted, whom you have forgotten but Allah has not. Therefore, "Do not belittle any good deed" (Narrated by Muslim). A smile in your brother's face might be your salvation, and a branch you remove from the road might be the reason Allah forgives you and admits you to Paradise.

The Second Face: The Consequences of Evil that are Not Neglected

Just as good has an effect, evil has consequences. Every injustice committed, every right usurped, and every sin committed openly without repentance are debts recorded in the servant's record. The demand for payment may be delayed, but it is not lost. It is the law of Allah which states:

﴾So whoever does an atom's weight of good will see it, (7) And whoever does an atom's weight of evil will see it. (8)﴿ [Az-Zalzalah: 7-8].
(﴿فَمَن يَعْمَلْ مِثْقَالَ ذَرَّةٍ خَيْرًا يَرَهُ (7) وَمَن يَعْمَلْ مِثْقَالَ ذَرَّةٍ شَرًّا يَرَهُ (8)﴾)

A person may be tried in their life—in their spouse, children, or livelihood—and search for solutions but find none. The problem may lie in an old injustice they did not rectify, the right of an orphan they consumed, or a tie of kinship they severed. It is the prayer of an oppressed person that traveled through a night the oppressor was heedless of, but Allah was not. That is why the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, gave the sternest of warnings, saying:

"Beware of the supplication of the oppressed, for there is no veil between it and Allah" (Agreed upon).

Even more dangerous is that Allah may give respite to the sinner and the oppressor, not out of negligence, but as a gradual leading (istidraj), then seize him at the moment he is weakest and most in need of help, making the seizure more severe and harsh. The Quran portrays this terrifying scene in His saying:

﴾Would one of you like to have a garden of palm trees and grapevines... while he is afflicted with old age and has weak offspring, and it is struck by a whirlwind containing fire and is burned?﴿ [Al-Baqarah: 266].
(﴿أَيَوَدُّ أَحَدُكُمْ أَن تَكُونَ لَهُ جَنَّةٌ مِّن نَّخِيلٍ وَأَعْنَابٍ... وَأَصَابَهُ الْكِبَرُ وَلَهُ ذُرِّيَّةٌ ضُعَفَاءُ فَأَصَابَهَا إِعْصَارٌ فِيهِ نَارٌ فَاحْتَرَقَتْ﴾)

The punishment came at the moment of old age, weakness, and the need of his children, to make the regret greater and the pain more severe.

So let this divine principle be a beacon for us in life: Be keen to contribute to every door of goodness, whether small or large, for you may not know which good deed will elevate you and which act of kindness will save you. And be wary of injustice and sins, for Allah gives respite but does not neglect, and their burdens may suddenly appear to destroy your life when you need it most.

The Testimony of Waraqah ibn Nawfal: The Insight of the People of Knowledge

Lady Khadijah, may Allah be pleased with her, was not content with providing emotional and psychological support. With her practical wisdom, she moved to the next step: seeking an explanation for this momentous event from the people of knowledge and learning. At that time in Makkah, there was no one more knowledgeable about the previous religions and heavenly scriptures than her cousin, the old and venerable Waraqah ibn Nawfal.

Waraqah was a unique figure. He had been a hanif in the pre-Islamic era, rejecting the worship of idols. He then became a Christian, read the Gospel, and wrote from it in Hebrew as much as Allah willed for him to write. He was not a Christian by tradition but a seeker of truth, following the correct religion brought by 'Isa, son of Maryam, peace be upon him, and he was awaiting the appearance of the final Prophet prophesied in the scriptures.

Lady Khadijah took the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, by the hand to him and said, "O my cousin, listen to what your nephew has to say." After the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, told him what he had seen, Waraqah's response was calm, profound, and confident, without a shred of doubt. It was the calmness of a scholar who sees the signs he had read about manifesting before him as a tangible reality. Here, the insight of true people of knowledge is revealed in three stances:

1. The Immediate Diagnosis: "This is the Namus"

Waraqah did not hesitate for a moment but uttered his decisive words that put things into perspective: "This is the Namus whom Allah sent to Musa."

  • An-Namus: This means the keeper of secrets, a title the People of the Book used for Jibril, peace be upon him, because he descends with the revelation, which is a secret between Allah and His prophets. With this word, Waraqah identified the visitor as the trustworthy conveyor of heaven's revelation.
  • To Musa: He specifically mentioned Musa, peace be upon him, because Musa's message was comprehensive, containing law, commandments, and struggle (jihad), making it the most similar to the message Muhammad, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, would carry, unlike the message of 'Isa, peace be upon him, which was a completion and fulfillment of the law of Musa. This indicates the precision of his knowledge and his deep understanding of the nature of the divine messages.

2. The Truthful Prophecy: "...will be met with hostility"

Waraqah did not stop at the diagnosis. He moved on, with the insight he derived from reading history and the laws of Allah concerning the prophets, to reveal to the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, the nature of the arduous path that awaited him: "I wish I were a young man. I wish I could be alive when your people will drive you out."

These words were a shocking surprise to the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, who was known among his people as the truthful and the trustworthy, beloved by all. He asked in astonishment and surprise: "Will they drive me out?"

Waraqah's reply came as a fixed law and an established custom: "Yes. No man has ever come with anything like what you have brought without being met with hostility."

History had taught him that truth always clashes with falsehood and that callers to reform are inevitably met with enmity from people of vested interests and desires. This truthful prophecy was an early psychological preparation for the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, to let him know that enmity and expulsion were not signs of his failure, but the price of carrying the truth and a sign of the sincerity of his path.

3. The High Aspiration: "I would support you with all my might"

In this situation, Waraqah ibn Nawfal, an old man who had lost his sight, sets the most wonderful example of high aspiration and sincere intention. After recognizing the truth, he did not wish for a long life to gain worldly benefits, but wished for it for one sublime purpose: "And if your day comes while I am alive, I will support you with all my might."

What an aspiration! An old man wishing for the strength of youth for no other reason than to be a soldier in the ranks of this call, to defend its Prophet and die for the cause of its victory. His state should make many strong young people today feel ashamed of themselves and their shortcomings in supporting the religion of Allah. He teaches us that supporting the truth is not tied to age or physical strength, but to the sincerity of the heart and the fervor of faith.

Fate did not grant Waraqah much time; he passed away a few days after this meeting, before the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, was commanded to proclaim the message. But he died upon faith and affirmation and received the reward for his sincere intention. The Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, reassured his nation about Waraqah's fate when asked about him, saying:

"I saw him in a dream, and he was wearing white garments. I think that if he had been among the people of the Fire, he would not have been wearing white garments" (Narrated by Ahmad and Tirmidhi).

Waraqah ibn Nawfal was a witness of truth, an insight of knowledge, and the aspiration of a believer. His testimony was the first testimony from the People of the Book to the truthfulness of this final prophethood.

The Period of the Interruption of Revelation (Fatur al-Wahy) and its Wisdom

After the awe-inspiring first encounter in the cave of Hira, and after the Prophet's, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, soul was calmed by the words of Lady Khadijah and the testimony of Waraqah ibn Nawfal, he entered a new and unique phase: the stage of divine silence, or what is known as "Fatur al-Wahy" (the interruption of revelation). The coming of Jibril, peace be upon him, ceased, and the heavenly messages paused for a period of time. Scholars differ on its duration, but the most likely view is that it was for a few days.

This interruption might seem puzzling. Why would the revelation stop after beginning with such force? However, within this silence lay profound wisdom and a gentle divine plan to prepare the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, for the next stage of the call.

The Wisdom of the Interruption of Revelation:

  1. Calming the Fear and Increasing the Longing:
    The first encounter was a violent and shocking human experience that left in the Prophet's, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, soul a mixture of fear and awe. A period of calm and tranquility was necessary for this natural fear to dissipate and be replaced by another, greater and stronger feeling: the longing and eagerness to resume this connection with the heavens. This interruption transformed the experience from a frightening event into a cherished memory and ignited in his heart, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, a powerful yearning to hear the words of Allah again. Thus, when the revelation returned, the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, would receive it with a longing heart, not a fearful one.
  2. Preparing for the Next Stage of the Message:
    The first verses, ﴾Read﴿, were a commission of prophethood. But the next stage would be a commission of the message and public proclamation: ﴾O you who covers himself [with a garment], (1) Arise and warn (2)﴿ [Al-Muddaththir: 1-2]. This commission to warn and confront the world requires psychological strength, steadfastness, and unwavering certainty. This period of interruption was like a rest for the warrior, an opportunity to reflect on what had happened, and to solidify the certainty in his heart that what he had experienced was not a dream or an illusion, but the truth from his Lord.
  3. Confirming the External Source of the Revelation:
    This interruption was a conclusive practical proof that the Quran was not emanating from the Prophet's, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, own self, nor was it a product of his thoughts or internal inspiration. If it were, he would have been able to summon it whenever he wished or fabricate more of it. But its sudden cessation and then its sudden return confirmed to him and to the worlds after him that this matter was not subject to his human will, but was a pure revelation that descends by Allah's command: ﴾Say, "If Allah had willed, I would not have recited it to you, nor would He have made it known to you."﴿ [Yunus: 16].

Discussion of the Narration of "Throwing Himself from Mountaintops":

It is mentioned in some books of Seerah and Tafsir, as an addition to the hadith of the beginning of revelation in Sahih al-Bukhari, a narration stating that the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, became so deeply saddened during the interruption of revelation that he intended several times to throw himself from a mountaintop. Each time, Jibril would appear to him and say, "O Muhammad, you are indeed the Messenger of Allah," and he would calm down and return.

This narration, despite its prevalence, requires careful scholarly examination:

  • Regarding its chain of transmission (Sanad): This addition is not from the words of Lady Aisha, the narrator of the original hadith. Rather, it is from the "balaghat" (unconnected reports) of Imam Az-Zuhri, who was a Tabi'i (from the generation after the companions). A "balagh" means he said, "It has reached us that..." without mentioning who informed him. This means the chain is broken, and in the science of hadith, a report with a broken chain is considered a weak narration that cannot be relied upon to establish such a serious matter concerning the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him. Major hadith scholars have stated its weakness.
  • Assuming its authenticity for the sake of argument: Even if we overlook the weakness of the chain and assume the narration is authentic, it does not undermine the infallibility of the Prophet, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him; rather, it confirms it. The infallibility of the prophets does not mean that they do not experience human feelings of sadness or distress, but it means that Allah protects and preserves them from committing a sin or falling into error. The narration itself says that whenever he intended to do so, Jibril came to him to comfort and prevent him. This, if anything, indicates the direct divine care that surrounded and protected him, just as happened with the Prophet Yusuf, peace be upon him: ﴾And she certainly determined [to seduce] him, and he would have inclined to her had he not seen the proof of his Lord.﴿ [Yusuf: 24].

In conclusion, this narration is weak and not authentic. The period of interruption was a wise, educational phase that increased the Prophet's, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, longing, solidified his certainty, and prepared his soul to receive the greater commission of proclamation and warning.

The Second Encounter and the Commission to Proclaim: {O you who covers himself [with a garment], * Arise and warn}

The period of the interruption of revelation ended, having fulfilled its wise, educational purpose. The fear had left the Prophet's, peace and blessings of Allah be upon him, heart and was replaced by a great longing and firm certainty that what he had experienced was the clear truth. While he was walking one day, the moment came when he would receive the command to undertake the heavy burden—the moment of being commissioned with the message.
