One day, the Messenger of Allah ﷺ came to pray, and Abu Jahl set out to fulfill his oath. But as he approached him, he "retreated on his heels," shielding himself with his hands in terror.

His people asked him, "What is the matter with you, O Abu al-Hakam?" He replied, "Verily, between me and him is a trench of fire, terror, and wings!" He had seen what they could not see—a direct divine protection that made him flee in horror. Commenting on this scene, the Messenger of Allah ﷺ said, "Had he come any closer to me, the angels would have snatched him limb by limb." (Narrated by Muslim).

These incidents were a declaration that Mecca was no longer a safe place to practice the simplest rituals of the religion and that the harm had reached a stage that could not be tolerated, compelling the Prophetic leadership to search for a strategic alternative to protect the lives of the believers and ensure the continuity of the message.

Recap: The Escalation of Harm

  • Shift in Strategy: When harming followers failed, Quraysh began targeting the Prophet ﷺ personally.
  • Psychological & Familial War: Harm from his uncle's wife, Umm Jamil, and the forced divorce of his two daughters.
  • Direct Physical Assault: Desecration of his prayer by placing camel intestines on his back while he prostrated.
  • Divine Protection: Miraculous intervention shielded him from Umm Jamil and from Abu Jahl's attempt to trample him during prayer.

2. The Philosophy of Victory in the Heart of Tribulation: The Dialogue of Khabbab ibn al-Aratt with the Messenger of Allah ﷺ

In the midst of suffering that had reached its peak, when the bodies of the believers had become a stage for the most heinous forms of torture, a natural and urgent question arises in the soul of every oppressed person: When will victory come? This critical moment was the platform from which the Prophet ﷺ delivered a timeless lesson in the "philosophy of victory," a lesson that redefines the concept of triumph, moving it from mere material victory and political empowerment to a broader and deeper horizon linked to steadfastness in principle and certainty in the heart.

The Cry of One Burned by the Fire of Torment: A Scream of Pain and a Search for Hope

Khabbab ibn al-Aratt (may Allah be pleased with him) was a living example of legendary sacrifice and patience. Being a blacksmith, his enemies used the tools of his trade to torture him; they would heat iron skewers and brand his bare back with them, and lay him on burning embers, which were only extinguished by the melting fat of his back. After tasting torment that could make mountains crumble, and in a moment of understandable human weakness, he and some companions went to the Prophet ﷺ, who was resting on his cloak in the shade of the Kaaba. With voices full of pain and hope, they said, "Will you not seek victory for us? Will you not pray to Allah for us?"

This was not just a complaint; it was a cry for help from souls exhausted by injustice, a search for a glimmer of hope in the dark tunnel of their ordeal. They were asking for "victory" in its direct sense: salvation from torment, the destruction of the oppressors, and the manifest triumph of Allah's religion.

The Timeless Prophetic Lesson: Redefining the Concept of Victory

Here, the Prophet ﷺ did not respond to their request with an immediate prayer for destruction as one might expect. Instead, he presented them with a complete methodology for understanding Allah's universal laws in conflict, and he divided victory for them into two types: one that was present and already achieved, and another that was coming at the time ordained by Allah.

First: The "Victory of Steadfastness" - You Are Already Victorious!

The first thing the Prophet ﷺ did was to correct their perception of their current situation. Instead of consoling them for their weakness, he reminded them of their true strength, which they were overlooking. He became angry—an educational anger—not at their complaint, but at their haste, which could imply a lack of certainty in Allah's promise. His face turned red, and he said:

"Among the nations before you, a man would be taken and a pit would be dug for him, and he would be placed in it. Then a saw would be brought and placed on his head, and he would be sawn into two halves, yet that would not turn him away from his religion. And he would be combed with iron combs between his flesh and bones, yet that would not turn him away from his religion..." (Narrated by Al-Bukhari).

With these powerful words, the Prophet ﷺ was telling Khabbab and his companions: You are not the first to walk this path, and what you face, as harsh as it is, has been faced in even more severe forms by those before you. More importantly, they were victorious. How? "Yet that would not turn him away from his religion." This is the "victory of steadfastness." The first true victory is not in defeating your enemy, but in your enemy not defeating you. To remain standing on your principles, firm in your religion despite all attempts to break and humiliate you, is in itself the greatest victory. He was telling them that at that very moment, while under the lashes of torment, they were victorious because their faith had not been defeated and their spirits had not been broken.

Second: The "Victory of Empowerment" - A Promise to Come, But with Conditions

After establishing the foundation of the "victory of steadfastness" as a present reality, he moved on to give them the good news of the second type of victory they sought: the "victory of empowerment" and manifest dominance. He affirmed that it was a divine promise that would inevitably come, but it was subject to Allah's timing and wisdom, not human haste. He swore to them, saying:

"...By Allah, this matter will be completed until a rider travels from Sana'a to Hadramawt, fearing none but Allah, or a wolf for his sheep. But you are impatient."

Here, he painted for them a picture of the bright future that would be realized because of their steadfastness today. He assured them that the religion of Allah would reach a level of strength and empowerment that would bring an unprecedented degree of security. However, he concluded with a key phrase: "But you are impatient." Empowerment is not a fruit to be picked easily; it is the natural result of stages of nurturing, trial, and patience. When the ranks mature and become worthy of carrying the trust, Allah will grant victory.

This is the divine law that the Quran clearly states:

﴿Or do you think that you will enter Paradise while such [trial] has not yet come to you as came to those who passed on before you? They were touched by poverty and hardship and were shaken until [even their] messenger and those who believed with him said, "When is the help of Allah?" Unquestionably, the help of Allah is near.﴾ (Al-Baqarah: 214)
. Victory is near, but it comes after the most intense moments of being "shaken" and tested.

Khabbab left that gathering with a new heart and a different perspective. He learned that his steadfastness in saying "Ahadun Ahad" (One, One) under the scorching rock was a victory in itself, and that this patience was the price paid in advance for Allah's promise of empowerment. The Prophet ﷺ had liberated him from the burden of waiting for an immediate result and made him focus on what he truly possessed: the worship of patience and absolute trust in Allah's wisdom and promise.

Recap: The Philosophy of Victory

  • The Question of the Oppressed: Khabbab ibn al-Aratt, while being brutally tortured, asked the Prophet ﷺ, "When will victory come?"
  • Redefining Victory: The Prophet ﷺ taught that victory has two forms.
  • Victory of Steadfastness: Remaining firm in one's faith despite torture is the first and greatest victory, which they had already achieved.
  • Victory of Empowerment: A guaranteed divine promise of future security and dominance, which requires patience and is not subject to human haste.

3. The Migration to Abyssinia: The Search for a Safe Haven and the Beginning of the Message's Universality

When the persecution in Mecca reached its peak, and holding onto one's faith meant facing a slow death under the whips of torture, the solution was not submission or an unbalanced military confrontation. Rather, the wisdom of the Prophetic leadership was manifested in making a strategic decision that would change the course of the message and protect its followers: migration. This migration was not a random escape but a carefully calculated move, carrying deep political, geographical, and da'wah dimensions, becoming the first practical declaration of the universality of the Islamic message.

Why Abyssinia? The Strategic Dimensions of Choosing the Land of Migration

The choice of Abyssinia (modern-day Ethiopia) as the first destination for Muslim migrants was a brilliant decision, based on a precise knowledge of the surrounding political and geographical reality, not mere chance. The Prophet ﷺ pointed to the direct reason for this choice when he told his companions: "If you were to go out to the land of Abyssinia, for in it is a king under whom no one is wronged. It is a land of truthfulness. Stay there until Allah grants you relief from your situation." (Narrated by Al-Bayhaqi and others with a good chain). Through this Prophetic guidance, several strategic dimensions become clear:

  1. The Political and Humanitarian Dimension: The Search for Justice: The deciding factor was the reputation of Abyssinia's ruler, the Negus (Najashi) Ashama ibn Abjar. The phrase "a just king under whom no one is wronged" was an international testimony guaranteeing the Muslims the right to political asylum and safety for their religion and their lives. The Prophet sought a system of governance, though non-Muslim, that was based on the principle of justice, a shared human value. This teaches us the jurisprudence of alliances and dealing with non-Muslims on the basis of common values.
  2. The Geographical Dimension: Exiting Quraysh's Sphere of Influence: Abyssinia was located outside the Arabian Peninsula, separated from Quraysh by the Red Sea. This geographical distance made it a true "safe haven," far from the reach of Quraysh and its tribal influence. Migrating to any place within the peninsula would have left the Muslims vulnerable to pursuit and pressure on the host tribes. Abyssinia, however, represented another continent and an independent political power not subject to Quraysh's authority.
  3. The Da'wah and Strategic Dimension: Establishing a Rear Base: The migration was not just for survival; it aimed to establish a "reserve base" for the message. In the event that the Muslims in Mecca were eliminated—a real possibility at the time—a Muslim nucleus would remain in Abyssinia, capable of carrying the message and continuing the journey. It was also an opportunity to open a new horizon for the message beyond the Arabs and to test the interaction of the Islamic message with a non-Arab society that had its own civilization and religion, which was a practical prelude to the universality of Islam.

The First Migration: Testing the Waters and the Premature Return

In compliance with the Prophetic command, the first group of migrants set out in the month of Rajab in the fifth year of the Prophethood. It was a secret migration under the cover of darkness, fearing the tyranny of Quraysh. This group consisted of twelve men and four women, led by two figures of special status: Uthman ibn Affan (may Allah be pleased with him) and his wife Ruqayyah, the daughter of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ. The Prophet's choice of his daughter and son-in-law to be at the forefront of the migrants was a powerful message to the rest of the Muslims: that he was not sacrificing their children while keeping his own family safe; rather, his family was the first to step forward on the path of sacrifice. The Prophet ﷺ commented on their migration, saying, "They are the first to migrate for the sake of Allah after Lot and Abraham."

The migrants reached Abyssinia and found the safety and justice their Prophet had promised them. However, their stay was cut short by news from Mecca rooted in a complex and widely misunderstood event. In Ramadan of that same year, the Prophet ﷺ stood before an assembly of Quraysh near the Kaaba and began to recite the newly revealed chapter, Surah An-Najm (The Star). The sublime eloquence and profound power of the words captivated the listeners, both believers and polytheists. The chapter's verses, speaking of the heavens, the certainty of revelation, and the power of God, left them in a state of awe. When the Prophet ﷺ reached the final verse, "So prostrate to Allah and worship [Him]," the spiritual force of the moment was so overwhelming that the entire assembly, without conscious thought, fell into prostration.

For the polytheists, this involuntary act of worship was a source of deep embarrassment and a contradiction of their public stance. To save face and rationalize their actions, a story was concocted and spread. This fabrication, famously known as the "story of the Gharaniq" (or the "Satanic Verses"), claimed that the polytheists had only prostrated because they heard the Prophet ﷺ praise their deities. According to this false narrative, after reciting the verses "Have you then considered al-Lat and al-Uzza? And Manat, the third, the other?" the Prophet ﷺ supposedly added the words: "These are the exalted Gharaniq (high-flying cranes), and their intercession is to be hoped for." This phrase would have validated their core belief that their idols were intercessors with God, providing them with a perfect excuse for their prostration.

However, this story is rejected as a complete fabrication by the vast majority of Muslim scholars for numerous compelling reasons:

  • Theological Incompatibility: The story directly contradicts the foundational Islamic doctrine of 'ismah—the divine protection of the Prophet ﷺ from error in conveying the message of God. The Quran itself affirms, "Nor does he speak from [his own] inclination. It is not but a revelation revealed" (Surah An-Najm, 53:3-4). The idea that Satan could interfere and insert words into the Divine Revelation is fundamentally incompatible with the nature of prophethood and the promise of Allah to preserve His word.
  • Internal Contradiction within the Surah: The verses that immediately follow the mention of the idols in Surah An-Najm are a powerful and direct refutation of them. The surah continues by condemning the polytheists for naming female deities and following conjecture instead of divine guidance. It is entirely illogical that the Quran would praise the idols in one line only to condemn them in the very next.
  • Contradiction with the Prophet's Life and Mission: The Prophet Muhammad ﷺ endured years of brutal persecution, a punishing social and economic boycott, and was offered kingship and immense wealth, all on the condition that he compromise on the principle of absolute monotheism (Tawhid). He steadfastly refused every offer. It is historically and psychologically inconceivable that he would suddenly make the single greatest concession to polytheism, thereby undermining his entire life's mission.
  • Unreliable Chains of Narration: From a historical-critical perspective, the story is transmitted through extremely weak and broken chains of narration (isnad). The great masters of Hadith criticism, such as al-Qadi 'Iyad, Ibn Kathir, an-Nawawi, and countless others, have thoroughly analyzed these narrations and declared them baseless and unreliable.

The reality of the event was simply the prostration of the Quraysh, born from the overpowering beauty of the Quran. This news, stripped of its context and embellished by the fabricated excuse, traveled until it reached the migrants in Abyssinia in a distorted and simplified form: "The people of Mecca have all converted to Islam."

Based on this joyful but false news, the migrants decided to return to their homeland and families. But upon reaching the outskirts of Mecca, the bitter truth was revealed: disbelief still prevailed, and the persecution had only intensified. They found themselves in a predicament. Some turned back to Abyssinia, some managed to enter Mecca under the "jiwar" (protection) of one of its chieftains, and others entered secretly, only to suffer again the chapters of torment.

The Second, Larger Migration: Towards Broader Stability

The failed return and the intensified tyranny of Quraysh made it clear that Mecca was no longer tenable. This led the Prophet ﷺ to permit the second migration to Abyssinia, which was a direct, practical application of the divine encouragement for the oppressed. The Quran had already established the principle that leaving a land of persecution is an act of faith, reminding believers that ﴿...the earth of Allah is spacious...﴾ (Az-Zumar: 10) and promising a great reward: ﴿And those who emigrated for the cause of Allah after they had been wronged - We will surely settle them in this world in a good place; and the reward of the Hereafter is greater, if only they could know.﴾ (An-Nahl: 41).

Responding to this divine call, a large contingent of eighty-three men and eighteen (or nineteen) women migrated, led this time by the Prophet's cousin, Ja'far ibn Abi Talib (may Allah be pleased with him), a man known for his wisdom, eloquence, and resemblance to the Messenger of Allah ﷺ.

This major migration was no longer just a test but a strategic transfer of a significant part of the Muslim body to the land of safety. Abyssinia had truly become a second home for the Muslims and a firm base for the message. They remained there for many years, only returning after the Islamic state was established in Medina and its foundations were secure. Their long-awaited return in the seventh year of the Hijra coincided with one of the Muslims' greatest victories: the conquest of Khaybar. The Prophet's ﷺ joy at their arrival was immeasurable. Upon seeing Ja'far after so many years, he embraced him and uttered his famous words: "By Allah, I do not know which of the two events makes me happier: the conquest of Khaybar or the arrival of Ja'far."

To honor their long years of sacrifice and patience, the Prophet ﷺ bestowed upon them a unique status. As recorded in a hadith narrated by Abu Musa al-Ash'ari, the Prophet ﷺ declared to Ja'far and his companions (who were known as "the people of the ship"): "The people migrated once, but you, the people of the ship, have migrated twice." (Narrated by Al-Bukhari and Muslim). He clarified that they had the immense reward for their initial migration to Abyssinia to protect their faith, and a second reward for their subsequent migration to join him in Medina, cementing their high rank and acknowledging their unique and prolonged journey in the path of Allah.

The migration to Abyssinia, in both its stages, proved to be not just a reaction to harm but a brilliant move in the political and religious chessboard. It preserved the message in its cradle, paved the way for its universality, and presented the world with a unique model of interfaith dialogue based on justice and mutual respect.

Recap: The Migration to Abyssinia

  • A Strategic Decision: Faced with existential threat, the Prophet ﷺ commanded migration as a strategic move, not an escape.
  • Why Abyssinia?: Chosen for its just king (the Negus), its geographical distance from Quraysh's control, and its potential as a rear base for the message.
  • The First Migration: A small group, led by Uthman ibn Affan and the Prophet's daughter Ruqayyah, tested the waters but returned prematurely based on a false rumor.
  • The Second Migration: A much larger group, led by Ja'far ibn Abi Talib, established a stable and secure Muslim community abroad.

4. A Diplomatic Battle in the Court of the Negus: The Argument of Islam vs. the Cunning of Quraysh

Quraysh could not bear to see the Muslims enjoying safety in Abyssinia. They formed a high-level diplomatic delegation to retrieve them, turning the conflict from the bloody fields of Mecca to the court of the Negus, in a unique battle where the weapons were not swords, but words and arguments. It was a confrontation between the political cunning of Quraysh, represented by Amr ibn al-'As, and the radiant wisdom of faith, represented by Ja'far ibn Abi Talib.

Amr ibn al-'As's Meticulous Plan: Political Cunning in the Service of Falsehood

Quraysh chose its most cunning men for this mission: Amr ibn al-'As and Abdullah ibn Abi Rabi'ah. They did not go empty-handed but were laden with precious gifts for which Mecca was famous, especially tanned leather, which the Negus and his courtiers cherished. Amr's plan was meticulous and based on two axes:

  1. Building an Internal Lobby: Before meeting the king, Amr generously distributed gifts to the "patriarchs" (senior religious and state officials close to the Negus). These were not mere gifts but blatant political bribes. After securing their loyalty, he presented his case to them, saying: "Some foolish youths from among us have sought refuge in the king's land. They have abandoned the religion of their people and have not entered your religion. The nobles of their people have sent us to you to return them. So when I speak to the king about them, advise him to hand them over to us without speaking to them." He wanted to settle the matter before it even began, denying the Muslims a chance to defend themselves.
  2. The First Speech Built on Distortion: When he entered the Negus's court, he presented the gifts and then delivered his first speech, a masterpiece of political cunning. He focused on points that would provoke any ruler:
    • Questioning their Competence: He described them as "foolish youths" to diminish their status and portray them as a group of rebellious teenagers whose words should not be taken seriously.
    • Stirring Religious and Nationalistic Sentiments: He said, "They have abandoned the religion of their people and have not entered your religion." This was a malicious statement aimed at isolating the Muslims. They were not on their forefathers' religion (so they were traitors to their traditions), and they were not on your religion, O King (so they have no religious loyalty to you). Rather, they have brought an invented religion that poses a threat to the stability of both realms.
    • Playing the Tribal Sovereignty Card: He concluded by requesting their extradition, saying that "the nobles of their people... have a better eye on them and know best what they have faulted them for." In other words, this is an internal matter that concerns us, and we know our own children best, so do not interfere between us.

Indeed, the patriarchs supported his words, saying, "He speaks the truth, O King. Hand them over to him." The plan almost succeeded, were it not for the justice of the Negus, who refused to judge a case without hearing from the other party.

Ja'far ibn Abi Talib's Historic Speech: Presenting Islam in its Most Beautiful Form

When the Negus summoned the Muslims, Ja'far ibn Abi Talib stepped forward to be their spokesman. His speech was not merely a defense but a comprehensive presentation of the message of Islam and a model for dialogue and da'wah, characterized by intelligence and profound sincerity:

  1. Systematic Comparison (From Darkness to Light): Ja'far began with an accurate and honest description of their condition before Islam to show the king the magnitude of the civilizational and ethical leap the new religion had brought about. He said, "O King, we were a people of ignorance (Jahiliyyah); we worshipped idols, ate the flesh of dead animals, committed abominations, severed family ties, treated neighbors badly, and the strong among us consumed the weak..." He painted a grim picture of a decaying society. Then he shifted to the opposite: "...until Allah sent to us a Messenger from among ourselves, whose lineage, truthfulness, trustworthiness, and chastity we knew. He called us to Allah, to worship Him alone... He commanded us to speak the truth, to fulfill our trusts, to maintain ties of kinship, to be good to our neighbors, and to refrain from forbidden acts and bloodshed..." He transformed the issue from a mere religious dispute into a comparison between a system of corruption and a system of righteousness.
  2. Focus on Shared Values: Ja'far was intelligent in his choice of attributes to describe Islam. He did not begin with details of creed that might be contentious but focused on the sublime human and ethical values that no heavenly religion or sound mind would disagree with: truthfulness, trustworthiness, kinship, good neighborliness, and refraining from injustice. By doing so, he won the sympathy of the Negus, who ruled with justice and valued these principles.
  3. The Emotional Impact (Surah Maryam): The Negus asked him, "Do you have with you anything of what he has brought from Allah?" Ja'far replied, "Yes." The Negus said, "Then recite it to me." Here, Ja'far did not choose verses about war or legal punishments. With brilliant insight, he chose the opening of Surah Maryam, which tells the story of Zechariah and John, and then the story of the birth of Jesus Christ, son of the virgin Mary—a story held sacred by Christians.
  4. Ja'far recited with a humbled voice:
    ﴿Kāf, Hā, Yā, 'Ayn, Ṣād. * [This is] a mention of the mercy of your Lord to His servant Zechariah...﴾
    . The narrators say, "By Allah, the Negus wept until his beard was soaked, and his bishops wept until they soaked their scrolls when they heard what he recited to them." The impact of the Quran was direct and profound because it addressed their hearts with what they knew and revered, but with a miraculous eloquence they had never heard before. The Negus then uttered his decisive words: "Indeed, this and what Jesus brought forth come from the same niche (mishkāh)."

The Decisive Round: The Stance on Jesus (peace be upon him) and the Triumph of Truth

Amr ibn al-'As did not give up. The next day, he decided to play his final and most dangerous card: the card of creed, which he thought would be the final blow. He went to the Negus and said maliciously, "O King, they say a grave thing about Jesus, son of Mary!" He wanted to engineer a direct clash between the Muslims' creed and the Christian king's creed, expecting that their "blasphemy" against Christ as a deity would turn the tables against them.

The Negus summoned the Muslims again, and this time they sensed the danger. They were in a dilemma: if they spoke the truth as they believed it (that Jesus is the servant and messenger of Allah), the king might become angry and expel them. If they lied or were diplomatic, they would betray their religion and their message. But they resolved to speak the truth, no matter the consequences.

When they entered the Negus's court, he asked them directly, "What do you say about Jesus, son of Mary?" Ja'far stepped forward with firmness and certainty and declared the pure creed of monotheism without hesitation: "We say about him what our Prophet brought to us: he is the servant of Allah, His Messenger, His spirit, and His word which He bestowed upon Mary, the chaste virgin."

Upon hearing these words, the Negus struck the ground with his hand, picked up a small twig from it, and then said his historic sentence that settled the battle: "By Allah, Jesus, son of Mary, does not exceed what you have said by [the length of] this twig." The Negus, by Allah's guidance, was upon the correct monotheistic creed that had not been tainted by the Trinity, so Ja'far's words came as a light that resonated with the innate faith in his heart.

At that moment, the battle ended with a crushing victory for the argument of Islam. The Negus turned to Amr ibn al-'As and his companion in anger and said, "Go, for by Allah, I will never surrender them to you." He then ordered their insulting gifts to be returned to them. The delegation of Quraysh departed, defeated and disgraced, while the Muslims remained in Abyssinia, enjoying safety and freedom under the protection of a just king who secretly declared his Islam to the Prophet ﷺ. So much so that on the day of his death in Medina, the Prophet ﷺ announced it to his companions, saying, "Today, a righteous man from Abyssinia has passed away. Come, let us pray for him." And he and his companions performed the funeral prayer in absentia for him, in honor of this king who protected Islam in its cradle.

Recap: The Diplomatic Battle in Abyssinia

  • Quraysh's Cunning Plan: Amr ibn al-'As led a delegation with gifts, bribing the king's court and framing the Muslims as divisive outcasts to secure their extradition.
  • Ja'far's Eloquent Defense: He contrasted the darkness of Jahiliyyah with the light and ethics of Islam, focusing on shared values.
  • The Power of the Qur'an: His recitation of Surah Maryam deeply moved the Negus and his bishops, establishing a common ground.
  • The Triumph of Truth: When questioned about Jesus (AS), Ja'far's honest declaration of pure monotheism resonated with the Negus, who then granted the Muslims full protection.

Conclusion: From the First Migration... Lasting Lessons in Wisdom, Steadfastness, and Dialogue

The migration to Abyssinia was not merely a passing event in the Prophet's biography or just a journey of survival from persecution. It was a complete school from which the first Muslims graduated—and the Ummah after them—with timeless lessons in understanding reality, the wisdom of leadership, strategies of da'wah, and steadfastness in principle. It is a story that confirms that the light of Allah cannot be confined to a geographical location and that truth, whenever it finds a sincere tongue and a courageous heart, is capable of penetrating the strongest fortresses of falsehood.

  1. The Wisdom of Leadership: Strategic Vision and Making the Hard Decision

    In the most difficult circumstances, the Prophet ﷺ demonstrated a sharp leadership vision that transcended the immediate moment. His decision to migrate was not submission but a "strategic maneuver" aimed at protecting lives, opening a new front for the message, and establishing a secure base. His choice of Abyssinia, based on precise information about its ruler's justice, teaches us that a successful leader bases his decisions on a conscious study of the political and geographical reality and does not throw his followers into the unknown. Furthermore, sending his own daughter and son-in-law at the forefront of the migrants establishes the principle of "leading by example," where the leader is the first to sacrifice, not the last to reap the rewards.

  2. The Courage of the First Generation: Sacrificing Homeland for Creed

    This migration reveals the greatness of the first generation of companions, who presented the world with a profound lesson in setting priorities. Leaving one's homeland, family, and wealth was not an easy decision; it is one of the most difficult things for the human soul. But they proved that creed is more precious than land and that the integrity of the religion takes precedence over all worldly ties. They chose exile and seeking refuge in a land whose language and people they did not know, for no reason other than to say "Our Lord is Allah" in freedom. They were the practical application of the verse:

    ﴿Say, [O Muhammad], "If your fathers, your sons, your brothers, your wives, your relatives, wealth which you have obtained, commerce wherein you fear decline, and dwellings with which you are pleased are more beloved to you than Allah and His Messenger and jihad in His cause, then wait until Allah executes His command..."﴾ (At-Tawbah: 24)
    .

  3. The Power of Dialogue: Presenting Islam in its True Form

    In the court of the Negus, Ja'far ibn Abi Talib presented a timeless model of the art of dialogue and da'wah. His speech was not merely a rebuttal of accusations but a positive and systematic presentation of the message of Islam. He taught us how to begin with shared human values that unite hearts, how to clearly compare the darkness of ignorance with the light of Islam, and how to use both rational argument and emotional proof together. His intelligent choice of Surah Maryam was the key to their hearts because he addressed the king and his court in a language they knew and revered, showing them that Islam was not an invented religion but an extension and correction of the heavenly messages in which they believed. It is a lesson that presenting Islam in its true form, free from tension and complexity, is the most powerful weapon against distortion.

  4. The Justice of a Non-Muslim... A Protective Wall for the Message

    In one of history's most wonderful ironies, Islam in its cradle found protection and safety not in the land of the Arabs but under the wing of a just Christian king. The stance of the Negus represents a milestone in the history of interfaith relations, showing that the value of "justice" is a universal human value that transcends religions and cultures and that it can be a safety valve to protect the truth, even if the one in power does not believe in it. The Negus refused to wrong a people who sought refuge with him and refused to judge before hearing from both sides, thereby setting the most magnificent example of the ethics of governance. His story reminds us that Allah may employ those who are not of its people to serve His religion, and that seeking a just environment, even if it is non-Muslim, is a legitimate religious objective to preserve one's faith and life, which is at the core of the jurisprudence for minorities and the Meccan stages.

In the end, the story of the migration to Abyssinia remains a beacon that illuminates the path for every caller to faith and for every person persecuted for their religion. It teaches us that doors closed in one place may open vast horizons in another, that steadfastness on the truth combined with presenting it beautifully is the key to hearts, and that Allah's promise to grant victory to His religion and His allies is true, even if it is delayed, and even if it comes at the hands of those we did not expect.

Introduction: After the Migration… The Wrath of Quraysh and the Glad Tidings of Relief

After the success of the Muslims' migration to Abyssinia (Al-Habasha الحبشة) and the failure of the Qurayshi diplomatic delegation to retrieve them, Amr ibn al-'As (عمرو بن العاص) returned to Mecca, trailing failure and defeat. This was not merely a diplomatic loss; it was a powerful blow to the prestige and authority of Quraysh (قريش), which no one had dared to challenge. How could "insolent youths," as they described them, find refuge and protection beyond their influence, and in the court of a great king like the Negus (Al-Najashi النجاشي) no less?

Faced with this dual humiliation, Quraysh was driven into a frenzy. Their suppressed anger transformed into blind brutality and escalating harm. The persecution was no longer limited to the vulnerable who had no protectors; in their desperation, they dared to harm those with tribal immunity and protection, in a hopeless attempt to restore their lost prestige and terrorize anyone who thought of defying their authority. The abuse reached its peak, and the ordeal intensified to the point that some thought there was no way out.

But in the heart of this culminating crisis, the glad tidings of relief were appearing on the horizon. Victory did not come from outside Mecca but dawned from the core of its power and from among its own nobles. At the very moment Quraysh was displaying its utmost brute force, Allah Almighty was preparing the means to shake its very foundations from within. This came through the conversion of two of its most formidable and esteemed men: Hamza ibn Abd al-Muttalib (حمزة بن عبد المطلب) and Umar ibn al-Khattab (عمر بن الخطاب), may Allah be pleased with them. Their acceptance of Islam was not just a numerical addition to the Muslims; it was a strategic shift in the balance of power and a turning point that moved the call to Islam from a phase of patience and secrecy to the beginning of a phase of public declaration and honor. Thus, the divine way of Allah was realized: from the womb of the most severe hardship, the most beautiful forms of ease are born.

1. Revelation Descends as Solace and Guidance: The Qur'an as a Constitution for the Oppressed

Studying the Prophet's biography (Sirah) in isolation from the Holy Qur'an, which was revealed in tandem with its events, is like reading a story severed from its spiritual and intellectual context. For every ordeal the early Muslims faced and every decision the Prophet (peace be upon him) made, revelation was powerfully present—interpreting events, charting the path, and instilling certainty in weary hearts. Amidst the pain that preceded the migration to Abyssinia, clear verses were revealed that were not just for consolation but served as a divine constitution, guiding the oppressed to Allah's ways in trials and migration.

"Allah's earth is spacious": A Divine Signal and a Gateway of Hope

As the weight of torture in Mecca intensified, despair began to creep into some souls. Were the believers destined to remain under this oppression forever? At this critical juncture, verses from Surah Az-Zumar, a Meccan chapter, were revealed, carrying a clear message and a gateway of hope for the tormented believers. Allah Almighty says:

“Say, ˹O Prophet, that Allah says,˺ ‘O My servants who have believed, be mindful of your Lord. For those who do good in this world, there is good. And Allah’s earth is spacious. Only those who are patient will be given their reward without limit.’” (Az-Zumar: 10)

The phrase "And Allah’s earth is spacious" was not merely a geographical description. It was a psychological preparation and a subtle divine signal that the solution was not confined within the walls of Mecca. The verse was telling the Muslims: If one land becomes too constrained for you, the entire earth of Allah is open for you to worship Him. Do not tie your faith to a geographical location where injustice is practiced; rather, seek another place where you can worship your Lord in freedom. This verse was a heavenly green light, preparing minds and hearts for the decision to migrate, and affirming that leaving one's homeland for the sake of faith is not a defeat but a door that opens to the boundless reward of the patient.

These verses taught them that the measure of value in the sight of Allah is not in remaining in the land of one's ancestors but in preserving the purity of one's creed. The Prophet's (peace be upon him) decision to migrate to Abyssinia thus came as a practical translation of this Qur'anic signal, turning pain into hope and siege into liberation.

"Seek refuge in the cave": A Lesson in Spiritual Seclusion

Concurrent with this difficult period, Surah Al-Kahf was revealed, carrying within it the story of youths who believed in their Lord in a disbelieving society. When they found no way to practice their religion without facing persecution, they made a fateful decision: to withdraw from their society entirely. Allah relates their words:

“Now that you have withdrawn from them and what they worship other than Allah, seek refuge in the cave. Your Lord will extend His mercy to you and accommodate you in your affair.” (Al-Kahf: 16)

This story was a direct message to the Muslims in Mecca. Just as the People of the Cave found no alternative but to leave their people and seek refuge in a dark cave to preserve their faith, you too, if you are unable to worship your Lord openly, then migration and withdrawal from the environment of polytheism and tribulation is the solution. The story provided a practical model that justified the idea of temporary "faith-based isolation" and confirmed that preserving one's religion in a secluded cave is better than living in luxurious palaces while compromising on one's creed.

Thus, the Holy Qur'an was the primary guide in this critical phase. It did not leave the believers to stumble in their confusion but presented them with historical models (the People of the Cave) and direct guidance (Surah Az-Zumar), teaching them that migration is not an innovation but one of Allah's established ways for empowerment, and that leaving the land for the sake of the heavens is the essence of honor and dignity.

2. After the Diplomatic Battle: Seeds of Islam in the Hearts of Enemies and Allies

The outcome of the confrontation in the court of the Negus (Al-Najashi النجاشي) was not just a diplomatic victory for the Muslims and a defeat for the Qurayshi delegation. It went far beyond that, leaving deep impacts and blessed seeds that sprouted in unexpected hearts. This battle proved that the power of sincere truth is not limited to convincing followers but extends to penetrating the fortresses of adversaries, turning enemies into neutrals, and neutrals into allies and brothers in faith.

From a Just King to a Brother in Faith: The Conversion of the Negus

The Prophet's (peace be upon him) testimony about the Negus (Al-Najashi النجاشي) before the migration was that he was "a king under whom no one is wronged." But after the Negus listened to the sincere presentation of Ja'far ibn Abi Talib (جعفر بن أبي طالب) and his moving recitation of Surah Maryam, he was no longer just a just ruler; his heart was transformed as it absorbed the light of faith. He wept until his beard was soaked and declared with timeless words that what Muhammad (محمد) and Jesus (عيسى) (peace be upon them) brought "indeed emanate from the same niche of light."

Although he did not publicly announce his Islam at that moment to maintain the stability of his kingdom against a revolt by his conservative bishops, his heart had submitted to the Lord of the worlds. He transformed from a mere "political ally" offering protection into a "brother in faith" who believed in the message. The greatness of his faith was manifested years later when the news of his death was revealed to the Prophet (peace be upon him) in Medina. At that moment, the Messenger of Allah stood among his companions and said with a tone of sorrow and appreciation, "A righteous servant of Allah, Ashama (أصحمة), has died today." He then said, "Ask forgiveness for your brother," arranged his companions in rows, and performed the funeral prayer in absentia (Salat al-Gha'ib).

This prayer was not just an honor; it was a heavenly declaration of the status of this man who had never seen the Prophet (peace be upon him) but had supported and sheltered him and his followers in the darkest of times. The Negus (Al-Najashi النجاشي) had died alone in his faith among his people, with no one to perform the Muslim funeral prayer for him. So Allah honored him by having the best of His creation and his noble companions pray for him, ensuring his name remains immortal in the history of Islam as a symbol of justice and innate faith.

A Private Counsel: The First Seeds of Doubt in the Heart of Amr ibn al-'As

The most surprising and astonishing impact was on the heart of the leader of the Qurayshi delegation himself, the cunning strategist of the Arabs, Amr ibn al-'As (عمرو بن العاص). He returned defeated and disgraced, but he did not return the same man he was when he left. Although he was a close friend of the Negus (Al-Najashi النجاشي), what he witnessed of the Muslims' powerful arguments, what he heard of the word of Allah, and what he saw of the king's justice and emotional response, planted the first seeds of doubt in his heart about the beliefs of his people.

Some accounts narrate that after dismissing the Qurayshi delegation, the Negus summoned his friend Amr ibn al-'As (عمرو بن العاص) for a private meeting. Away from officialdom, he addressed him with the sincerity of a loyal advisor, saying, "O Amr, obey me and follow him, for by Allah, he is upon the truth, and he will surely prevail over his opponents just as Moses (موسى) prevailed over Pharaoh and his armies." This was not just advice; it was a testimony from a wise man who saw with the light of his insight what Amr could not see with the eyes of politics and pride.

These words, coming from a king he trusted and revered, left a deep impact on Amr's heart. Although his pride prevented him from responding immediately, this seed continued to grow within him for many years. This encounter haunted him, as he compared the stubbornness of his people with the conviction of these "oppressed" believers. A full sixteen years later, after a long journey of hostility towards Islam, this seed finally bore fruit and was among the many factors that ultimately led him to submit his face to Allah and join the ranks of the religion he had long fought against.

Thus, we see how sincerity in presenting the truth is never in vain. In a single battle, Islam won a great king and planted doubt in the heart of one of its staunchest enemies, fulfilling Allah's promise that His light will reach the hearts, even if after some time.

3. Jahiliyyah of Yesterday and Today: The Criteria of Ja'far ibn Abi Talib as a Mirror to Modern Civilization

When Ja'far ibn Abi Talib (جعفر بن أبي طالب) stood in the court of the Negus (Al-Najashi النجاشي), he did not describe the state of his people before Islam with vague, emotional words. Instead, he provided a precise and specific diagnosis of the moral and social collapse they were living in. He laid out five clear criteria that represented the essence of "Jahiliyyah" (the Age of Ignorance): "We were a people of ignorance (Jahiliyyah); we worshipped idols, we ate the flesh of dead animals, we committed abominations, we severed family ties, we treated our neighbors badly, and the strong among us devoured the weak."

Some might think this description belongs to a distant past, and that the material and scientific progress humanity has achieved today has eradicated these primitive phenomena. However, when we use these five criteria as a critical lens to examine the reality of modern materialistic civilization, we discover a shocking paradox: while this civilization boasts of its veneer of technological advancement, in its human and ethical core, it suffers from the same diseases Ja'far diagnosed, perhaps in a more virulent form.

Ja'far's Mirror Reveals the Reality of Materialistic Civilization:

  1. "We worshipped idols": Today's idols may not be carved stones, but they have taken on more modern and influential forms. Modern man worships other kinds of idols: the idol of materialism, which has become the ultimate goal; the idol of lust, liberated from all moral and religious constraints; and the idol of the ego, which sanctifies absolute individualism at the expense of family and society. Man has shifted from worshipping God to worshipping his own desires, which is the essence of idolatry in every time and place.
  2. "We committed abominations": What was done in the Jahiliyyah of the Arabs in secret and with shame is now practiced openly and proudly under the banner of "personal freedom." Abominations have transformed from individual deviations into legally protected social phenomena. Statistics from the heart of the Western world point to a horrifying reality:
    • Sexual Harassment: In the European Union, over 55% of women experience physical harassment, and a third of these cases involve children under the age of fifteen.
    • Infidelity: In Britain, 60% of men cheat on their wives, and 42% of women have relationships with more than one man, threatening the very fabric of the family.
    • Sexually Transmitted Diseases: The United States alone records 56 million cases of STDs, a number reflecting the scale of the health collapse resulting from promiscuity.
    • Organized Prostitution: 50,000 women are trafficked annually to America alone to work in prostitution, turning human beings into commodities to be bought and sold.
  3. "We severed family ties and treated our neighbors badly": The family has disintegrated in many modern societies, and children often do not know their fathers. Older statistics indicate that a quarter of Americans do not know who their fathers are, a number likely to have increased. As for neighborly relations, they have been replaced by isolation and withdrawal, where a person can live next to their neighbor for years without knowing their name. Nursing homes are filled with fathers and mothers abandoned by their children.
  4. "The strong among us devoured the weak": In Jahiliyyah, this principle manifested as tribal raids. Today, it has taken on more organized and brutal forms under the guise of "international law" and "economic interests." Powerful nations exploit the resources of weaker nations, wage destructive wars for hegemony, and the lives of millions become mere numbers in political and economic equations. The "veto power" in the Security Council remains a testament that the logic of power is still the de facto ruler of the world.
  5. "We ate the flesh of dead animals": While eating carrion in Jahiliyyah was sometimes a necessity, today it has become a philosophy of life. Materialistic civilization, disconnected from divine revelation, is dead in its spiritual and moral values and treats humans as bodies without souls. This spiritual void explains the terrifying rates of suicide. In France alone, 32 people commit suicide daily, and 160,000 attempt it annually. In America, 11 million people suffer from major depression, 76 million are addicted to alcohol, and 10 million to marijuana. These are numbers that reveal societies devouring their own spiritually dead bodies.

This shocking comparison confirms a fundamental truth: material progress does not create a true civilization. Civilization is not merely skyscrapers and advanced technology; it is a value system that preserves human dignity, safeguards morality, and elevates the soul. Ja'far's mirror has proven that humanity today, despite all its tools of material power, is still in desperate need of the light of revelation brought by Muhammad (peace be upon him), which Ja'far described as coming to lead them out of all this darkness.

4. The Great Turning Point: The Conversion of the Lion of Allah and His Messenger, Hamza ibn Abd al-Muttalib

In the sixth year of the mission, while Quraysh was escalating its tyranny and persecution of the Muslims, a monumental event occurred that no one had anticipated—an event that shook the entity of Mecca and brought honor, for the first time, to the hearts of the oppressed believers. It was the conversion of "The Lion of Allah and the Lion of His Messenger," Hamza ibn Abd al-Muttalib (حمزة بن عبد المطلب), the uncle of the Prophet (peace be upon him) and his foster brother. His conversion was not merely an individual transformation but the dawn of a new era in the history of the call to Islam.

The Spark of Rage: From Tribal Pride to a Declaration of Faith

Hamza (حمزة), may Allah be pleased with him, was known as "the most formidable young man in Quraysh," not only for his immense physical strength and unrivaled bravery but also for his high status that made everyone fear him. He had not yet embraced Islam, and his stance on his nephew's call was one of sympathetic neutrality. Although he followed the religion of his forefathers, he would not tolerate Muhammad (peace be upon him) being harmed.

One day, as Hamza (حمزة) was returning from a hunting trip, his bow slung over his shoulder as usual, he was met on the road by a female servant of Abdullah ibn Jud'an, who had just witnessed a painful scene. She said to him urgently, "O Abu Ammarah, if only you had seen what your nephew Muhammad just endured from Abu al-Hakam ibn Hisham (Abu Jahl أبو جهل)! He found him sitting here, so he insulted him, cursed him, and did to him what he detests." Hamza (حمزة) had barely heard these words when he was consumed by a rage unlike any he had felt before. This was not just an insult to his nephew; it was a direct challenge to his honor and the honor of Banu Hashim.

He spoke to no one and did not stop, but went straight away in search of Abu Jahl (أبو جهل), finding him sitting in his assembly among his people. Hamza (حمزة) stood over him, then brought his bow down on his head, striking him a severe blow that split his head open, and blood began to flow. Then, he issued a thunderous challenge: "Do you insult him while I am on his religion? Do you say of him what you say while I believe in what he has brought? Now, return that blow to me if you can!"

The sentence "while I am on his religion" was not just a phrase uttered in a moment of anger; it was the "point of no return." Hamza (حمزة) had declared it out of pride and honor, but it was a word of truth that Allah willed to be spoken at that moment, becoming his inescapable destiny. Faced with this humiliating scene for their leader, men from Banu Makhzum (Abu Jahl's clan) rose to defend him, and men from Banu Hashim rose to defend Hamza (حمزة). A great fight was about to erupt. But Abu Jahl (أبو جهل), realizing the situation was not in his favor and that provoking Hamza (حمزة) would cost him dearly, said with the wisdom of a frightened man, "Leave Abu Ammarah alone, for by Allah, I did indeed insult his nephew grievously."

The Night of Transformation: From Doubt to Certainty

Hamza (حمزة) returned to his home, his storm of anger having subsided. But within him, a different kind of battle began—a battle of the soul and the heart. He had declared before everyone that he was on Muhammad's religion. Was he sincere in that? Or was it merely a word spoken out of pride? Hamza (حمزة) spent that night in a conflict he had never known before, torn between his loyalty to the religion of his fathers, the declaration to which he had committed himself, and the truth whose light was beginning to seep into his heart.

It was a night of supplication and deep reflection. In some narrations, he prayed to his Lord, saying, "O Allah, if this matter is good, then make a way out for me and cast certainty into my heart." Allah did not leave him in his confusion but opened his heart to Islam. The next morning, he went to the Prophet (peace be upon him), told him what had happened and what had transpired within him. The Messenger of Allah continued to call him to the faith and remind him of Allah until his heart was filled with the light of certainty, and he declared his Islam with sincerity and devotion.

Hamza's (حمزة) conversion was like an earthquake that struck the pillars of polytheism in Mecca. The Muslims rejoiced greatly, for they knew that Hamza (حمزة) would protect them and would be a sword of Allah defending the call. A great gloom fell upon Quraysh, for they realized they had lost one of their most formidable men and that the scales were beginning to tip in favor of the Muslims. His conversion was the first ray of honor to illuminate the dark sky of Mecca, and the beginning of a new phase where Muslims dared to do what they had not dared to do before, for they now had a "lion" to protect them and intimidate their enemies.

5. Al-Faruq Shines with His Light: The Multiple Factors Behind the Conversion of Umar ibn al-Khattab

Just three days after the conversion of Hamza ibn Abd al-Muttalib (حمزة بن عبد المطلب), Mecca witnessed another monumental event that was a clear victory for Islam in its Meccan phase. The light of faith shone in the heart of a man who had been one of the fiercest enemies of the call and one of the most brutal in his persecution of Muslims, to the extent that his conversion seemed impossible to some. It was the conversion of "Al-Faruq" Umar ibn al-Khattab (عمر بن الخطاب), may Allah be pleased with him. His transformation was not the story of a single individual but the result of a series of accumulating factors that Allah had arranged to lead this giant into the fold of faith.

Umar's conversion was not the product of chance or a fleeting incident. It was the tip of an iceberg, concealing beneath it deep accumulations of internal conflict and a subtle influence from the call of truth. Umar's strong and rigid personality needed multiple, successive shocks to crack the walls of Jahiliyyah that had surrounded his heart.

The Factors that Paved the Way for Al-Faruq's Conversion:

  1. The Subtle Impact of the Qur'an: Despite his outward hostility, Umar (عمر) was not immune to the effect of the Holy Qur'an. One night, he snuck to listen to the Prophet (peace be upon him) praying at the Ka'bah (الكعبة) and heard him reciting Surah Al-Haqqah. Umar (عمر) was mesmerized by the power of its eloquence and the greatness of its meanings, saying to himself, "By Allah, this is a poet, as Quraysh says." The Prophet then recited: “It is truly the word of a noble messenger. It is not the word of a poet—little do you believe!” Umar (عمر) thought, "Then he is a soothsayer." The Prophet recited: “Nor the word of a soothsayer—little do you take heed! It is a revelation from the Lord of all worlds.” Umar (عمر) later said, "At that moment, Islam settled deep within my heart." This was the first seed of certainty planted in his heart, even though he tried to hide it beneath the harshness of his enmity.
  2. The Prophet's Answered Prayer: The Prophet (peace be upon him), with his prophetic insight, recognized the potential for strength and sincerity in Umar's (عمر) character that could be a great support for Islam. He turned to his Lord in prayer, saying: "O Allah, strengthen Islam with the one of these two men whom You love more: Umar ibn al-Khattab or Abu Jahl ibn Hisham." This prayer was a request for divine reinforcement and a heavenly choice for one of the two poles of power in Quraysh. It was Umar (عمر) for whom divine care was destined.
  3. The Shock of Hamza's Conversion: The conversion of Hamza (حمزة), a man equal to Umar (عمر) in strength and standing, was a violent shock to Umar. He felt a sense of rivalry and pride; how could Hamza (حمزة) precede him in this matter, leaving him in the camp of polytheism that was beginning to weaken? Hamza's conversion made him feel isolated and forced him to think more seriously: Could all these people be wrong and only we are right?
  4. The Decisive Confrontation at His Home: All these factors converged and culminated on that fateful day. Umar (عمر) left his house, his sword girded, determined to kill the Prophet (peace be upon him) and end this matter. On his way, he was met by a man from Banu Zuhra (Nu'aym ibn Abdullah), who informed him that his own sister, Fatimah, and her husband, Sa'id ibn Zayd, had converted to Islam. Umar's (عمر) fury was unleashed, and he changed his course towards his sister's house to settle the matter there first.
  5. He stormed into the house and found the companion Khabbab ibn al-Aratt teaching his sister and her husband the Qur'an from a scroll containing Surah "Ta-Ha." Umar (عمر) brutally attacked his brother-in-law, Sa'id. When his sister tried to defend him, he struck her a blow that drew blood. Here, the great transformation occurred. When Umar (عمر) saw the blood on the face of his sister, whom he never imagined he would lay a hand on, he was overcome with deep remorse and his heart softened. Her steadfastness and firmness before him, and her words in his face—"Yes, we have become Muslims and believed in Allah and His Messenger, so do what you will"—was a shock that awakened his conscience.
  6. Umar (عمر) calmed down and asked for the scroll to read what was in it. After performing a ritual wash, he read the beginning of Surah Ta-Ha. When he reached the verse:
    “Indeed, I am Allah. There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Me. So worship Me ˹alone˺, and establish prayer for My remembrance.” (Ta-Ha: 14)
    , he uttered the words that changed history: "How beautiful and noble are these words! Lead me to Muhammad."

From the House of Al-Arqam to the Ka'bah: The Declaration of Honor

When Khabbab came out of hiding and gave him the good news of the Prophet's prayer for him, Umar (عمر) set off for the house of Al-Arqam ibn Abi al-Arqam. There, in the presence of the Prophet (peace be upon him), he pronounced the two testimonies of faith. The Muslims exclaimed "Allahu Akbar!" (God is the Greatest) so loudly that the people of Mecca heard it and knew that something momentous had happened.

Umar (عمر) was not content with his Islam remaining a secret; his nature abhorred concealment. He asked about the "biggest spreader of news in Mecca," and they directed him to Jamil ibn Ma'mar al-Jumahi. Umar (عمر) went to him and said in private, "Do you know that I have submitted to Islam?" Jamil did nothing but run to the Ka'bah (الكعبة) and shout at the top of his lungs, "Listen! The son of Al-Khattab has apostatized!" Umar (عمر) emerged from behind him and said, "He lies! Rather, I have embraced Islam!"

The men of Quraysh rose up against him, and he fought them, and they fought him from morning until noon. He fought nearly three hundred men alone until they were exhausted. This was not just a declaration of faith; it was an open challenge to all of Quraysh and a clear message that the era of weakness was over, and that Islam now had a sword that would not be sheathed.

6. The Impact of the "Two Islams": The Beginning of the Phase of Public Proclamation with Honor and Strength

The conversions of Hamza ibn Abd al-Muttalib (حمزة بن عبد المطلب) and Umar ibn al-Khattab (عمر بن الخطاب) were not merely an increase in the number of Muslims; they represented a qualitative and strategic transformation in the entire journey of the Islamic call. The conversion of these two "lions" within a span of three days dealt two devastating blows to the prestige of Quraysh. It was an explicit declaration that Islam was no longer the religion of the oppressed who practiced their rituals in secret, but had become a force to be reckoned with, capable of proclaiming the truth and challenging falsehood in its own heartland.

"We Have Remained Honored Since Umar Embraced Islam": A Testimony to the Transformation

The noble companion Abdullah ibn Mas'ud (عبد الله بن مسعود), one of the earliest Muslims who tasted the most severe forms of torment, summarized this historic shift in a timeless, eloquent phrase: "We have remained in a state of honor ever since Umar embraced Islam." In another narration, he said, "Umar's conversion was a victory, his migration was a triumph, and his leadership was a mercy."

These were not just emotional words; they were an accurate description of a new reality experienced by the Muslims. Before Umar's (عمر) conversion, fear and secrecy were the dominant features. Ibn Mas'ud (ابن مسعود) himself narrated, "We were not able to pray at the Ka'bah until Umar became a Muslim." Merely performing prayers in the most sacred place was a distant dream, fraught with danger and humiliation. But as soon as Umar (عمر) joined the ranks of the Muslims, everything changed. His conversion gave the Muslims a boldness they had never known and a sense of security they had never felt, for they now had a strong backbone to protect them and a loud voice to defend them.

From Secrecy to Public Proclamation: The First Demonstration of Faith-Based Power

Umar (عمر) could not patiently endure the state of concealment the Muslims were living in. Immediately after his conversion, he went to the Prophet (peace be upon him) and said with his characteristic audacity, "O Messenger of Allah, are we not on the truth, whether we live or die?" The Prophet replied, "Yes, indeed." Umar (عمر) then asked, "Then why the secrecy?"

This question was the launching point for a new phase. The Prophet (peace be upon him) responded to this powerful logic and decided that the time had come to display the honor of Islam. For the first time, the Muslims emerged in two rows, marching openly through the streets of Mecca towards the Ka'bah (الكعبة). Hamza ibn Abd al-Muttalib (حمزة بن عبد المطلب) was at the head of one row, and Umar ibn al-Khattab (عمر بن الخطاب) was at the head of the other, with the Messenger of Allah (peace be upon him) between them.

It was a majestic scene that Mecca had never witnessed before. The Muslims walked with steadfastness and strength, fearing no one, while Quraysh looked on in stunned silence and shock, overcome by a gloom they had never experienced. They realized in that moment that the balance of power had changed forever. The Muslims entered the Sacred Mosque, prayed openly at the Ka'bah (الكعبة), circumambulated the House, and then departed, and Quraysh did not dare to stand in their way.

This procession was the first "demonstration of faith-based power" in the history of Islam. It was not a show of weapons but a display of truth, certainty, and courage. For this act, the Prophet (peace be upon him) named Umar (عمر) "Al-Faruq"—the one who distinguishes—because he distinguished between truth and falsehood, between the era of secrecy and the era of proclamation, and between the stage of weakness and the stage of honor.

Conclusion: The Divine Patterns of Victory

The story of the migration to Abyssinia and the momentous events that followed reveal profound divine patterns (Sunan) in victory and empowerment. The Islamic history of this decisive phase teaches us that the path of great calls is fraught with trials, and that the darkest moments are those that precede the breaking of dawn. The conversion of two men of the stature of Hamza (حمزة) and Umar (عمر) in the most difficult of circumstances, at a time when it seemed that disbelief had tightened its grip on Mecca, teaches us that victory does not always come from the outside or through alliances of power. Rather, Allah may plant it in the heart of the enemy's camp, bringing forth from the loins of polytheists those who become unsheathed swords for the cause of Allah.

These events confirmed that when Allah Almighty wills to grant victory to His religion, He prepares the means for it from sources we could never imagine. He answers the prayer of His Prophet, opens hearts that were sealed shut, and turns the staunchest adversaries into the most loyal soldiers. It is a message to every believer facing hardship: do not despair, and do not look only at the material balance of power. For how many fortified castles has Allah demolished from within, and how many locked doors has He opened with a key that was never anticipated?

The true power that changed the course of history in Mecca was not the power of weapons or numbers. It was the power of faith that overturned the most formidable hearts, the power of the sincere word that shook the thrones of falsehood, and the power of steadfastness on principle that earned the believers the respect of their enemies before their allies. From migrating in search of safety to proclaiming the truth in the heart of Mecca, the Sirah teaches us that victory begins from within: from a steadfast heart, a truthful tongue, and an unwavering trust in the promise of Allah.

1. Negotiations of Despair: When Falsehood Tries to "Tailor" Religion to Fit Desires

When Quraysh exhausted its reserves of "coarse power"—represented by torture and siege—and collided with the rock of the believers' steadfastness upon which their whips were broken, the leaders of polytheism resorted to "soft power" and political bargaining. They realized that direct confrontation only made Islam more solid, so they decided to try a strategy of "containment and dilution," thinking that creed was a tradable commodity and that the Prophet ﷺ (peace be upon him) was a seeker of kingship or wealth who could be satisfied halfway.

First: The Strange Deal... Exchanging a "Son" for a "Son"

In an attempt that revealed a materialistic, "idol-worshipping" mentality that placed no value on human worth, a delegation of Quraysh leaders set out to Abu Talib (أبو طالب), led by Al-Walid bin Al-Mughirah (الوليد بن المغيرة), accompanied by a young man named 'Umarah bin Al-Walid (عمارة بن الوليد). 'Umarah was not an ordinary youth; accounts describe him as "the most vigorous youth in Quraysh," meaning the most youthful, beautiful, strong, and poetic.

They stood before Abu Talib and said with the audacity of a merchant: "O Abu Talib, this is 'Umarah bin Al-Walid, the most vigorous and beautiful youth in Quraysh. Take him, for you shall have his intelligence and support, and adopt him as a son, for he is yours. In exchange, hand over to us this nephew of yours who has opposed your religion and the religion of your forefathers, divided the unity of your people, and mocked their dreams, so that we may kill him. It is merely a man for a man."

The offer displayed political and social stupidity. Abu Talib’s response was crushing and sarcastic, exposing the moral paradox in their offer: "By Allah, what a miserable bargain you offer me! Do you give me your son to feed for you, and I give you my son to kill? By Allah, this shall never be."

Here, the attempt to buy "protection" with flesh and blood failed, so they moved to an attempt to buy "creed" with concessions.

Second: The Theory of "Religious Mixing"... (We Worship Your God for a Year, You Worship Ours for a Year)

The polytheists did not object to worshipping Allah, but their problem was with "singling Him out" for worship and rejecting all else. Therefore, four of the cunning leaders of disbelief gathered: Al-Walid bin Al-Mughirah (الوليد بن المغيرة), Al-Aas bin Wa'il Al-Sahmi (العاص بن وائل السهمي), Al-Aswad bin Al-Muttalib (الأسود بن المطلب), and Umayyah bin Khalaf (أمية بن خلف). They intercepted the Messenger of Allah ﷺ while he was circumambulating the Kaaba.

They offered him a deal that appeared on the surface to be "tolerance and coexistence," but internally blew up the essence of Monotheism (Tawhid). They said: "O Muhammad, come, let us worship what you worship, and you worship what we worship, and we will share the matter with you. If what you worship is better than what we worship, we will have taken a share of it, and if what we worship is better than what you worship, you will have taken a share of it." In a narration by Ibn Abbas (ابن عباس): "You touch our gods (Al-Lat and Al-Uzza) and we will believe in your God."

They thought religion was a "joint-stock company" whose shares could be divided. At this decisive moment, Quranic revelation descended like a separating sword, not only to reject the offer but to cut off their ambitions forever. Surah Al-Kafirun was revealed, which scholars have named "The Surah of Disavowal from Polytheism."

Allah Almighty said:

“Say, 'O disbelievers, I do not worship what you worship. Nor are you worshippers of what I worship...”
The negation was repeated to emphasize that the difference is not superficial but fundamental. The Surah concluded with an eternal constitutional rule:
“For you is your religion, and for me is my religion.”
Allah also revealed in Surah Az-Zumar, responding to their ignorance:
“Say, 'Is it other than Allah that you order me to worship, O ignorant ones?'” [Az-Zumar: 64].

Third: The Attempt to "Amend the Constitution"... (Bring a Quran Other Than This or Change It)

When they despaired of merging the two religions, they tried to intervene in the "founding text" itself. The grandees of Quraysh sat with the Prophet ﷺ and said to him in the tone of an advisor: "O Muhammad, you have brought a grave matter, by which you have made enemies of the clan... If you have brought this speech seeking wealth, we will gather it for you... and if you seek honor, we will make you our chief." But the most dangerous part was their request to amend the Quran.

They said: "Bring a Quran other than this" (i.e., a Quran that does not insult our gods, does not fault our fathers, and does not mention Al-Lat and Al-Uzza with evil), "or change it" (replace the verse of punishment with a verse of mercy, and the forbidden with the permissible).

They wanted a "tailored Quran" fitted to their desires, one that did not clash with their interests or change their lifestyle. The Divine response came decisively in Surah Yunus, defining the function of the Messenger as "delivery" (Balagh) not "authorship," and that Revelation (Wahi) is sacred and not subject to human negotiation.

Allah Almighty said:

“And when Our verses are recited to them as clear evidences, those who do not expect the meeting with Us say, 'Bring us a Quran other than this or change it.' Say, 'It is not for me to change it on my own accord. I only follow what is revealed to me. Indeed I fear, if I should disobey my Lord, the punishment of a tremendous Day.'” [Yunus: 15].

Projecting onto Reality: The "Abrahamic Religion" and Attempts at Modernization

One who contemplates these historical negotiations finds that they match perfectly with what our contemporary reality is witnessing, where only the "title" has changed while the "content" remains the same:

  1. The Abrahamic Religion and Unity of Religions: What Al-Walid bin Al-Mughirah offered of "mutual worship" is offered today in international conferences under glittering slogans like the "New Abrahamic Religion" or "Shared Values," aiming to dissolve doctrinal differences, dilute the doctrine of "Loyalty and Disavowal" (Al-Wala' wal-Bara'), and melt Islam into a pot of a hybrid global religion that pleases dominant powers and does not anger Allah (so they claim).
  2. Renewing Religious Discourse (Western Concept): What the polytheists requested regarding "changing the Quran" is repeated today through pressure from international institutions and research centers (like the Rand Corporation) demanding a "Modern Islam." They ask for the removal of verses of Jihad from curriculums, the cancellation of laws regarding inheritance and family under the pretext of "keeping up with the times," and emptying the religion of its legislative content so it becomes mere individual spiritual rituals unrelated to public life.

The lesson learned from the "Negotiations of Despair" in Mecca is that power does not lie in flexibility and concession, but in "superiority through faith." A religion that accepts half-solutions ends up vanishing, while the creed that says "No" in the face of bargains is the one destined to survive and spread because it derives its strength from Heaven, not from the approval of the masters of the Earth.

2. The Policy of Siege and Starvation: When "Empty Stomachs" Become a Battlefield

In Muharram of the seventh year of Prophethood, after Quraysh had exhausted attempts at "individual harm" and "political bargaining," they decided to shift to a strategy of "slow mass genocide." The tyrants of Mecca realized that killing the Prophet ﷺ was impossible without causing a bloody war with Banu Hashim, so their satanic minds devised a trick to break the will without directly shedding blood: "Total Siege."

The Alliance of "Al-Khayf" and Writing the Document of Injustice

The heads of disbelief gathered in "Khayf Banu Kinanah" (خيف بني كنانة) in the valley of Al-Muhassab and pledged allegiance to an unjust scroll in which they wrote clauses cutting off all ties of humanity and kinship:

  1. Not to marry with them (neither giving women to them nor marrying from them).
  2. Not to buy from them nor sell to them (total economic blockade).
  3. Not to sit with them nor mix with them (social isolation).
  4. Not to accept any peace settlement from Banu Hashim ever, nor show them mercy, until they surrender the Messenger of Allah ﷺ to be killed.

The writing of this hateful scroll was undertaken by Baghid bin Amir bin Hashim (بغيض بن عامر بن هاشم) (and in other narrations: Mansour bin Ikrimah or An-Nadr bin Al-Harith). The Prophet ﷺ prayed against him saying: "O Allah, paralyze his hand," so his hand became paralyzed, and he died unable to move it, serving as an early sign of the ominous nature of this act. The scroll was hung inside the Kaaba to lend religious sanctity to their crime.

Entering the "Shi'b" (Valley): Distinction of Ranks

Banu Hashim and Banu Al-Muttalib—both believers and disbelievers among them—retreated to the "Shi'b of Abu Talib" (شِعب أبي طالب) (a narrow mountainous area between the mountains of Mecca). Here a strange phenomenon manifested; the believers entered seeking reward and patience for the sake of religion, while the disbelievers from the Prophet's cousins (headed by Abu Talib) entered out of tribal loyalty and to preserve "Arab dignity," refusing to hand over their son to the enemy. The only exception to this tribal consensus was Abu Lahab (أبو لهب), who broke away from his tribe and joined the camp of Quraysh, declaring hostility toward his nephew.

Three Years of "Eating Leather"

The siege lasted for three full years, during which the suffering intensified until the sound of children (the crying babies) screaming from hunger could be heard from behind the mountain, reaching the ears of Quraysh in their meeting place. Some, like Abu Jahl (أبو جهل), were pleased by this, while others were pained but powerless to change it.

Supplies were completely cut off, and nothing reached them except in secret. The situation reached a point where the Companions of the Prophet ﷺ and his family ate tree leaves (Khabat) until the corners of their mouths became ulcerated just as the mouths of camels do.

Sa'd bin Abi Waqqas (سعد بن أبي وقاص), may Allah be pleased with him, narrates a heartbreaking scene from those days: "I went out one night to urinate, and I heard the rattling of urine on something. It turned out to be a piece of dry camel leather. I took it, washed it, then burned it, crushed it between two stones, and swallowed it with water, gaining strength from it for three days."

Security Caution: Abu Talib's Nightly Sacrifice

Hunger was not the only enemy; the fear of assassination was present. Abu Talib feared a night attack on the Prophet ﷺ. So, when people went to their beds, he would order the Prophet ﷺ to lie down in his bed (Abu Talib’s bed) in front of everyone, so spies would see his location. Once people fell asleep and eyes were calm, he would order one of his sons or brothers (like Ali bin Abi Talib - علي بن أبي طالب) to get up and lie in the Prophet's place, and he would take the Prophet ﷺ to another hidden bed, sacrificing his sons for him every night.

The Prophet ﷺ: The Call Does Not Stop Under Siege

Despite the harshness of the siege, the Prophet ﷺ did not withdraw from life, nor did his Dawah stop. He would go out during the "Hajj seasons" (when Arabs were committed to a ceasefire) to invite the tribes coming to Mecca. He would follow them to their camps and markets (Ukaz, Majanna, and Dhu al-Majaz), while his uncle Abu Lahab followed behind him screaming: "Do not obey him, for he is a lying Sabian," and telling the merchants: "O merchants, raise the prices against the companions of Muhammad so they cannot buy anything from you." They would increase the prices until the Companion returned to his starving children empty-handed, his tears preceding him.

Civilizational Comparison: Between "Jahiliyyah of Abu Jahl" and "Jahiliyyah of the Age"

When projecting the event of the "Shi'b" onto our contemporary reality—specifically the siege and starvation occurring in Gaza and elsewhere—a stark moral paradox appears in favor of the "polytheists of Mecca" compared to the "tyrants of today":

  1. The "Breathing Room" of Sacred Months: The polytheists of Mecca possessed remnants of religion and chivalry that prevented them from fighting or fully restricting people during the Sacred Months and Hajj seasons. They would partially lift the siege, allowing the besieged to go out and buy, a "breathing space" that the besieged today do not find under deaf international laws.
