As soon as the caravan left, her husband Abu Ma'bad (أبو معبد) returned, driving lean sheep swaying from weakness. When he entered the tent, he was shocked to see pure white milk filling the vessel. He cried out in amazement: "Where did you get this, O Umm Ma'bad, when the sheep is distant (at pasture) and there is no milking sheep in the house?!"

She said to him with words full of awe: "No, by Allah, except that a blessed man passed by us, whose speech was such and such..."

Abu Ma'bad said with Arab intelligence: "Describe him to me, O Umm Ma'bad."

Why is Umm Ma'bad's Testimony the Most Reliable?

Here lies the greatness of this situation. Umm Ma'bad did not know that this was the "Messenger of Allah" whom Quraish was seeking. She had no prior acquaintance with him, nor did she follow his religion yet. She was describing a "stranger" who passed by her tent. Consequently, her description was completely "objective," stripped of the halo of pre-existing sanctity that might influence the descriptions of the believing Companions. Her eye was a precise camera capturing human, aesthetic, and charismatic details with total detachment.

She described what her eye saw and what her heart felt of awe and dignity. Her description became the most complete historical document of the Prophet's ﷺ physical characteristics, causing her husband to say immediately upon hearing it: "By Allah, this is the man of Quraish whose affair has been mentioned to us... and I have resolved to accompany him, and I will do so if I find a way."

2. "The Most Beautiful and Splendid of People": A Precise Reading of the Noble Features

If the "face" is the mirror of the soul and the title of personality, then the face of the Prophet ﷺ was a "miracle of beauty" that captivated hearts before minds. His beauty ﷺ was not merely a harmony of features, but a "light" that covered him and a majesty that rose above him, such that whoever saw him suddenly was filled with awe, and whoever mixed with him grew to love him. In Umm Ma'bad's tent, this woman stood to scrutinize this strange face, producing for us a precise artistic portrait wherein later describers did not omit a single letter except to elaborate on what she had summarized.

First: The Halo of Light and Color (Radiant, Bright-Faced)

The first thing that struck Umm Ma'bad was the "Radiance." She said: "Manifestly radiant, bright-faced" (Zahir al-Wada'ah, Ablaj al-Wajh). The radiance here is not just cleanliness, but a "luminosity" that catches the eye. His face ﷺ was "luminous white" (Azhar al-Lawn); a clear, shining white not mixed with yellow or dullness, neither chalky white (Amhaq) nor dark (Adam), but a white imbued with redness, as if his cheeks were dyed with rose water, or as the Companions described: "As if pomegranate seeds were crushed on his cheeks."

This "bright" (Ablaj) face shimmered like the moon on a full-night. Al-Bara' bin 'Azib (البراء بن عازب) was asked: "Was the face of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ like a sword?" (meaning in shine, sharpness, and length). He said: "No, rather like the moon"; combining shine with roundness and a calm beauty that does not hurt the eye. Some Companions went further, as Abu Huraira (أبو هريرة) said: "I have not seen anything more beautiful than the Messenger of Allah ﷺ; it was as if the sun was running in his face."

Second: The Engineering of the Head and Face (Handsome, Well-Proportioned)

Umm Ma'bad described the general shape of the face and head by saying: "No potbelly disgraced him, and no smallness of head disparaged him" (Lam Ta'ibhu Thujlah, wa lam Tuzri bihi Sa'lah). This is a negation of flaws before affirming virtues:

  • Thujlah: Meaning he did not have an ugly, excessive largeness of the head, nor a large belly disrupting his symmetry.
  • Sa'lah: Meaning his head was not too small in a way that diminished his presence, nor was it gaunt.

Rather, his head ﷺ was "large," indicating perfection, leadership, and abundant intellect, proportionate to his strong body. His face had "smooth cheeks" (Sahl al-Khaddayn), meaning his cheeks were even and soft, without protruding cheekbones nor sunken, but his face tended towards "roundness" without being perfectly round like a ball.

This precise symmetry is what she expressed with the phrase "Handsome, well-proportioned" (Waseem Qaseem). Wasamah is stable beauty, and Qaseem is one whose features are divided by a precise scale, so every organ (nose, eye, mouth) is placed in its optimal space, with perfect area, so no feature overpowers another.

Third: The Magic of the Eyes (Intense Blackness, Contrast, Divine Kohl)

Umm Ma'bad paused for a long time at his eyes ﷺ, for they are the center of attraction in his face. She said: "In his eyes, there is deep blackness" (Da'aj).

  • Da'aj: Is the intense blackness of the pupil, combined with the wideness of the eye. His eyes ﷺ were wide, and their blackness was pitch-black like a still night.
  • Hawar: Ali bin Abi Talib (علي بن أبي طالب) described him as having Hawar. This is the intense whiteness of the sclera (white of the eye) contrasting with the intense blackness of the iris. This stark contrast is the peak of beauty for the Arabs.
  • Shuklah: The Companions added (visible to those very close) that in the white of his eyes were fine red lines (Shuklah), a praiseworthy trait indicating alertness and strength.
  • Divine Kohl: Umm Ma'bad said: "Naturally kohl-rimmed" (Akhal). Meaning his eyelids were black at the roots of the eyelashes by creation, without applying kohl. He appeared as if he were always wearing kohl, giving the eyes depth and definition that captivated the viewer.
  • Wataf: The beauty was increased when she said, "And in his eyelashes, there is length" (Wataf). His lashes ﷺ were long and thick, shading his eyes; if he looked down, it increased his dignity, and if he looked up, it increased his beauty.

Fourth: The Eyebrows (Arched and Connected)

As for the eyebrows, they were a miracle of precise drawing. She said: "Arched, connected" (Azajj, Aqran).

  • Azajj: Meaning his eyebrows were long and arched precisely (like a crescent moon), not straight. This arch frames the eye and highlights its beauty.
  • Aqran: Meaning the eyebrows were long enough to almost meet in the middle (between the eyes). Some describers clarified saying "Arched without being connected," and combining the narratives suggests they were very close, appearing connected (Aqran) from afar, but with a fine separation visible only up close. In this space was a vein that swelled when he was angry, and this spot radiated light and purity.

Fifth: The Nose and Mouth (Pride and Eloquence)

  • The Nose: His nose ﷺ was not flat or broad (Aftas), but "Aquiline" (Aqna). Qana is a rise in the nasal bone (bridge) with fineness at the tip. His nose was straight and high. It is narrated that "one who did not look closely would think him Ashamm (having a high nasal bridge)" due to the light emanating from it; the bright light from his face and nose suggested an additional height and grandeur to the viewer.
  • The Mouth and Teeth: He ﷺ had a "wide mouth" (Dale'e al-Fam), a great trait of praise among Arabs indicating eloquence and the ability to pronounce letters clearly (opposite to a small, constricted mouth).
  • Teeth: His teeth were brilliant white, described by narrators as "lustrous front teeth." He had a gap between his front teeth (Aflaj al-Thanayya); a small, beautiful spacing between the two front incisors. When he spoke ﷺ, it looked as if light was emerging from between his teeth.
  • Saliva: His saliva was pleasant and blessed, curing the sick and quenching the thirsty, as happened on the day of Khaybar when he spat lightly into the eye of Ali bin Abi Talib (علي بن أبي طالب) and it healed.

Sixth: The Neck (The Silver Pitcher)

The details of the head concluded with the neck supporting this noble head. Umm Ma'bad said: "In his neck, there is a shine" (Sata').

Sata' implies length and height. His neck was long, not short where the head sinks into the shoulders. The color of his neck was intensely clear and white, likened by Ali bin Abi Talib (علي بن أبي طالب) and others to "a pitcher of silver," or like the "neck of a statue" in its purity and firmness, shining in the sunlight, reflecting light and beauty, harmonizing with the whiteness of the face and the blackness of the hair and beard.

This wondrous blend of "deep black eyes," "radiant skin," "aquiline nose," and "gapped teeth" formed a complete human masterpiece, rendering Hassan bin Thabit (حسان بن ثابت) unable to describe him except to say:

And more beautiful than you, my eyes have never seen... And more perfect than you, no woman has ever given birth to.
You were created free from every defect... As if you were created exactly as you wished.

3. Engineering Human Perfection: Body Structure and Quiet Strength

If the face is the title of beauty, the body is the title of strength and presence. An observer of the Prophet's ﷺ physical attributes finds themselves before a "standard model" of human perfection, combining the bulk of heroes, the agility of youth, and a softness of touch unmatched. He ﷺ was not merely an eloquent preacher; he was a knight of complete build, prepared by Allah to bear the heavy burdens of the Message, to engage in battles, and to endure the hardships of life. His creation was balanced—neither excessive nor lacking—in a divine engineering described with extreme precision by the Companions, led by Ali bin Abi Talib (علي بن أبي طالب) and Hind bin Abi Hala (هند بن أبي هالة).

First: Stature and Moderation (A Branch Between Two Branches)

The Prophet ﷺ was neither excessively tall (appearing awkward) nor hesitatingly short (a defect). He was of "medium stature among the people" (Rab'a), meaning average height leaning towards tallness, described as "closer to being tall."

However, he had a wondrous divine characteristic noted by the Companions; if he walked alone, he appeared "medium" (Rab'a), but if he walked with tall people, he "towered over them." No one walked with him, no matter their height, except that the Prophet ﷺ matched or exceeded them. When he separated from them, he returned to his attribute. This phenomenon led Umm Ma'bad to describe him with poetic precision: "A branch between two branches; he was the most radiant of the three in appearance, and the finest of them in stature." She likened him to a fresh, green branch amidst tree branches; not the longest to be awkward, nor the shortest to be weak, but the "most beautiful" and most vibrant. "No eye would despise him for length, nor look down upon him for shortness."

Second: Skeleton and Musculature (Broad Shouldered, Large Jointed)

The Prophet's body ﷺ was "built" strongly and solidly, combining bulk with symmetry:

  • Shoulders and Chest: He was "broad-shouldered" ('Azeem al-Manikib), with "distance between the shoulders," indicating a broad chest and strong build, traits of knights and leaders. His chest was broad and full, not frail. His chest and stomach were even (Sawa' al-Batn wa al-Sadr); neither having a protruding belly nor being sickly thin, but rather possessing a splendid athletic flatness.
  • Joints and Bones: Ali bin Abi Talib (علي بن أبي طالب) described him as having "large joints" (Dakhm al-Karadis). Karadis are the heads of bones and joints (like knees, elbows, and shoulders). Large joints indicate a massive, strong skeletal structure; his bones were not fragile or thin but strong enough to endure hardship.
  • Limbs: He had "thick palms and feet" (Shathn al-Kaffayn wa al-Qadamayn). Shathuna implies thickness, a tendency towards shortness, and fullness. This is a praiseworthy trait in men indicating strength and the capability for work and combat. His palm was full, and his foot was solid and strong.

Third: Silk Touch and Musk Scent (The Soft Palm)

Despite the size of his bones, the strength of his muscles, and the thickness of his palms, his noble hand possessed a softness that baffled describers. Anas bin Malik (أنس بن مالك)—who served him for ten years and touched his hand often—said: "I have not touched brocade nor silk softer than the palm of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ."

This wondrous paradox (a strong, thick hand, yet soft as silk) is one of the characteristics of his perfection ﷺ.

As for his scent, it was another marvel. The Prophet ﷺ was "the best smelling of people," whether he applied perfume or not. Anas said: "I have never smelled musk or ambergris more fragrant than the scent of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ." His sweat was called "pearls" for its clarity and whiteness. Umm Sulaym (أم سليم) used to collect his sweat and put it in her perfume, saying: "It is the best of perfumes." If he walked a path, people knew he had passed by due to the lingering pleasant scent in the air. If he wiped the head of a child, the child would be known by the pleasant scent among his peers for days.

Fourth: Hair and Beard (Blackness of Night)

  • Head Hair: His hair ﷺ was "intensely black," with only a few gray hairs (said to be no more than twenty in his temples, chin tuft, and the parting of his head). His graying was due to the fear of Allah and the verses of warning revealed to him ("Surah Hud and its sisters have turned me gray"). As for the nature of his hair, it was neither "lank" (Sabt—very straight and soft like the hair of the Romans) nor "kinky" (Ja'd Qatat—coarse and tightly curled), but rather "wavy" (Ja'd Rajil), meaning it had gentle, beautiful waves. Its length varied with his conditions; sometimes reaching his "earlobes" (Wafra), sometimes descending "between his ears and shoulders" (Limma), and sometimes hitting his "shoulders" (Jumma). He parted it in the middle, sometimes let it hang, and cared for it by oiling and combing it.
  • The Majestic Beard: He had a "thick" (Kaththa) beard, meaning dense hair with abundant growth, described as "great," filling his noble chest. His beard framed his face like a black crown, adding indescribable dignity and awe. He took care of it and ordered it to be left to grow while trimming the moustache (Ihfa' al-Shawarib), so his moustache was fine and did not cover the upper lip, highlighting the beauty of his mouth and smile.

Fifth: Body Details (The Line of Hair and Seal of Prophethood)

  • Body Hair: The Prophet ﷺ was "hairless" (Ajrad), meaning he did not have much body hair (on the chest or stomach), except for a fine line of hair extending from the top of the chest to the navel, called al-Masrubah. This fine black line adorned the whiteness of his chest and stomach as if drawn. He had light hair on his arms, legs, and upper chest.
  • Legs and Feet: His shins were like "palm pith" (Jummar), likened to the white, shiny heart of the palm tree in whiteness, clarity, and fullness. He had "lean heels" (Manhus al-'Aqibayn), meaning little flesh on the heels, indicating agility and lightness in movement. His foot was "large" and flat (not having a high arch or severe curve), treading the ground with the full foot, signifying stability and groundedness.
  • The Seal of Prophethood: The defining mark sought by Salman al-Farsi (سلمان الفارسي) and the monks was located "between his shoulders" (closer to the left shoulder). It was a "protruding piece of flesh" (Bad'ah Nashizah), reddish in color, with hairs on it, sized "like a pigeon's egg" or a "partridge button." This seal was the divine stamp on his body, a tangible physical sign confirming his selection and the sealing of messages.

Sixth: Movement and Walk (As if Descending a Slope)

The description of his body was not static but dynamic, pulsing with life and power. The Prophet's ﷺ walk was unique, reflecting his decisive character and purpose.

  • Speed and Power: He was a "fast walker," not walking like the lazy or the feignedly weak. Abu Huraira (أبو هريرة) described him: "I have never seen anyone faster in his walk than the Messenger of Allah ﷺ, as if the earth folded up for him; we would exhaust ourselves while he was indifferent."
  • Method of Walking: He walked "leaning forward" (Takaffu'), and "lifting his legs forcefully" (Yaqla' Rijlahu), meaning he lifted his foot strongly off the ground and did not drag it. The most famous description was: "As if he were descending from a slope" (Sabab). Imagine someone walking down a hill; their body leans slightly forward, steps are wide, strong, and fast due to gravity. This is how the Prophet ﷺ walked on flat ground: self-propulsion, a slight forward lean indicating seriousness, and high energy as if always pursuing an important matter. His walk had neither the swaying of the arrogant nor the slowness of the hesitant, but the walk of a leader who knows his destination.

This blessed body, with its massive joints, broad shoulders, soft palm, pleasant scent, and fast walk, was the "physical vessel" chosen by Allah to carry the "Great Spirit" and the Final Message. He was a miracle in creation just as he was a miracle in character.

4. The Magic of "Silence and Speech": Prophetic Charisma and Majestic Dignity

The magic of the Prophet ﷺ was not limited to facial beauty or physical strength alone; he possessed an "overwhelming presence" and exceptional "charisma" that captivated you whether he was silent or speaking. It is the awe of Prophethood that attracts souls and makes them submit to his majesty without coercion or force. In this part, we delve into the world of "Voice," "Performance," and the "Psychological Impact" of the Prophet's ﷺ personality, to see how he filled a space with light and awe merely by his existence.

First: The Symphony of the Voice (In His Voice was Resonance)

Voice is half of presence, and the Prophet ﷺ had a voice that could not be forgotten—not just a tool for speech, but a tool for influence. Umm Ma'bad described his voice with a very precise word: "In his voice, there is a resonance" (Sahal).

Meaning of Sahal: Linguists differed on the exact nuance, but agreed it is a trait of praise and beauty. It is said to be a "slight huskiness" in the voice, giving it depth and a pleasant roughness without harshness, increasing masculinity and attractiveness. Others said it means "sharpness and solidity," meaning his voice was strong and resonant, not faint or weak, nor pathologically "hoarse."

It was that melodious, deep voice which, if you heard it, pierced your ear and settled in your heart, compelling you to listen. His voice ﷺ was not "boisterous" (Sakhab—shouting loudly and annoyingly), as described in the Torah: "Neither rude, nor coarse, nor shouting in the markets." Rather, his voice carried a "quiet authority," reaching ears clearly without the affectation of yelling.

Second: The Philosophy of Speech (Sweet Speech, Decisive)

How did the Master of the Eloquent speak? The Prophet ﷺ was not a chatterbox, nor a "babbler" (Hathar—too much useless talk), nor "taciturn" (Nazr—too little talk to convey meaning). Umm Ma'bad described his speech as: "Sweet of speech, decisive (Fasl), neither too little nor too much."

  • Fasl: Meaning his speech was "distinct" and "specific," separating truth from falsehood, and one sentence from another. He spoke slowly and deliberately, such that if a listener wanted to count his words, they could. He did not "rush speech" (Sard) as many do, but gave every word its due and every meaning its space.
  • Repetition to Embed Meaning: From his habit in speech, as narrated by Aisha and Anas (عائشة وأنس), is that "if he spoke a word, he repeated it three times until it was understood from him." This refined educational style fixes the idea and gives the listener a chance for full comprehension.
  • String of Pearls: Umm Ma'bad likened his flowing, connected speech to "beads of a necklace falling in succession." Imagine a pearl necklace whose string breaks, and the beads follow one another in regularity and smoothness; this is how his words emerged—harmonious, beautiful, arranged, with no contradiction or confusion.
  • Comprehensive Words: He ﷺ was given "Comprehensive Speech" (Jawami' al-Kalim); saying short phrases that carry limitless meanings and constitutions (like: "No harm and no reciprocating harm," "Religion is advice").

Third: The Dignity of Silence (Dignity Overcame Him)

If his speech was magic, his silence was another kingdom. Umm Ma'bad said: "If he was silent, dignity overcame him."

His silence was not the silence of a "vacant mind" or "dullness," nor the silence of "inability" to reply. Rather, it was the silence of "contemplation" and "awe." His silence ﷺ was divided into four types (as per some traditions): silence of forbearance, silence of caution, silence of estimation, and silence of contemplation.

When he fell silent, he was clothed in majesty and stillness that made those around him afraid to interrupt him. The Companions in his assembly, if he spoke, would lower their heads "as if birds were perched on their heads" (from the intensity of stillness, listening, and politeness). No one moved, no one whispered, out of awe, reverence, and love, not out of fear of oppression. This natural dignity made a woman like Umm Ma'bad—a strong Bedouin—feel that this "Silent One" filled the tent with his overwhelming presence more than those who were speaking.

Fourth: Radiance in Speech (Splendor Overcame Him)

And if he broke his silence and spoke, the equation changed. She said: "And if he spoke, splendor (Baha') overcame him."

Baha' is beauty combined with magnificence and illumination. When he spoke, his features interacted, his face shone, his mouth (with gapped teeth) smiled releasing light, and his noble hand moved (he used to strike the palm of his right hand on the thumb of his left if amazed), turning the entire gathering into a mass of fascination. He was not just a speaker conveying information, but a "spirit" breathing life into words, clothed in a state of enchanting beauty that snatched sight away.

Fifth: The First Awe (Whoever Saw Him Suddenly, Feared Him)

The Prophet's ﷺ effect on strangers was a phenomenon worthy of study. Ali bin Abi Talib (علي بن أبي طالب) and others narrated: "Whoever saw him suddenly (Badiha) stood in awe of him, and whoever mixed with him with knowledge loved him."

  • The Shock of the First Meeting: Badiha is the sudden encounter. Any person laying eyes on the Prophet ﷺ for the first time, without prior acquaintance, would be struck by a "tremor" or intense "awe." The heart trembles, and the muscles quake before this unusual human majesty. It happened that a man came to speak to him ﷺ, and he was seized by a severe tremor (shaking from fear and awe). The Prophet ﷺ said to him with humility that melts ice: "Calm yourself, for I am but the son of a woman from Quraish who used to eat dried meat (Qadid)." With one word, the Prophet ﷺ broke the barrier of fear and restored the man's balance, reminding him of his humanity and the simplicity of his origin.
  • The Magic of Association: However, once interaction and mixing (Mukhalata) began, that awe turned into "sweeping love." The companion discovers his humility, gentleness, lighthearted joking, and care for the speaker, so the Prophet ﷺ becomes more beloved to him than his family, wealth, and self.

Sixth: The Art of Interaction (Companions Surround Him)

The Prophet ﷺ did not sit isolated in an ivory tower; rather, he was "served and gathered around" (Mahfud Mahshud) as Umm Ma'bad described.

  • Mahfud: Meaning his companions serve him and hasten to obey him (from Hafd, meaning speed).
  • Mahshud: Meaning people gather around him and crowd together.
  • Neither Frowning nor Refuted: He ﷺ was not "frowning" ('Abis), but rather "smiling of countenance," "the most smiling of people." His laughter was a smile; he did not guffaw or raise his voice in laughter. Nor was he "refuted" (Mufannad—attributed to ignorance or weakness of opinion, or one whose speech is useless); rather, all his speech was wisdom.
  • Leadership with Love: She said: "If he spoke, they listened to his speech; if he commanded, they hastened to his command." This immediate compliance was not the product of a "strict military system," but the product of "love and trust." They surrounded him (Yahuffuna bihi), like a halo around the moon, racing to fulfill his desires before he spoke them, out of love for his person, desire for his call, and belief in his message.

This "Prophetic Charisma" combined opposites in miraculous harmony: Awe with humility, dignified silence with eloquent speech, seriousness with a smile, and firm leadership with sweeping love. He ﷺ was a "magnetic" personality in every sense of the word, attracting hearts and souls to orbit around him, unable to break away.

5. Beyond Yusuf and the Moon: Settling the Debate of Beauty and Longing for the Vision

After this precise journey through the contours of the face, the engineering of the body, and the symphony of the voice, we arrive at the question that has long occupied lovers and admirers of Muhammadan beauty: Where does the Prophet's beauty ﷺ stand in the scale of human beauty? Did he surpass the "Icon of Beauty," Yusuf (Joseph) peace be upon him? Was his voice sweeter than the "Psalms of Dawud (David)"? In this conclusion, we will clarify matters and settle the debate with the sayings of scholars and the testimonies of the Companions, opening a window of hope for everyone longing to see him.

First: The Debate of Yusuf and Muhammad (Who is More Beautiful?)

It is famous among people that Yusuf (peace be upon him) is the "most beautiful human" absolutely, based on the Hadith of the Ascension (Isra and Mi'raj): "And lo, he had been given half of beauty." However, upon investigation and scrutiny of the sayings of verifying scholars (like Ibn al-Qayyim and al-Qurtubi), we find a consensus that the Prophet Muhammad ﷺ is "the most beautiful of Allah’s creation absolutely."

How do we reconcile this with the "half of beauty" Hadith?

  1. "Relative" Half of Beauty: Some scholars view that Yusuf was given half of beauty relative to the people of his time, or half the beauty distributed among humanity. However, Allah distinguished the Prophet ﷺ with "exceptional" beauty beyond the usual human division—an "absolute perfection" of beauty in which no one shared.
  2. Beauty of Majesty vs. Beauty of Allure: Scholars say: Yusuf's beauty was an overt, "tempting" beauty that drove the women of the city to cut their hands. As for the Prophet's ﷺ beauty, it was clothed in "Majesty," "Awe," and "Dignity." Allah cast a cloak of awe upon him, preventing people from being tempted by him in a worldly way. Had Allah revealed the "reality of his beauty" ﷺ fully without the veil of awe, necks might have been cut instead of hands, and no one could have borne looking at him.
  3. Testimony of the Knowers: Al-Abbas (العباس), the Prophet's uncle, was asked: "Are you older (greater) or the Messenger of Allah?" He replied with utmost politeness: "He is greater (Akbar) than me, but I was born before him." Then the Companions said regarding the comparison: "Yusuf was more handsome (Ahsan), but Muhammad was more striking/attractive (Amlah)." Malaha to the Arabs is "attractiveness" and acceptance that exceeds mere symmetry of features. It is established that the Prophet ﷺ combined "Handsomeness," "Attractiveness," and "Perfection," thus surpassing Yusuf in the total sum.

Second: Sweetness of Voice (Sweeter than Dawud)

Dawud (David) peace be upon him was known for a beautiful voice, such that mountains and birds would echo with him, and his voice was called a "Mizmar" (Psalm/Flute). Abu Musa al-Ash'ari (أبو موسى الأشعري) (a companion of the Prophet ﷺ) was given "a Mizmar from the Mazamir of the House of Dawud."

But what about the voice of the Prophet ﷺ?

Anas bin Malik (أنس بن مالك) says: "Allah did not send a prophet except with a handsome face and a handsome voice, and your Prophet ﷺ was the most handsome of them in face and the most handsome of them in voice."

Al-Bara' bin 'Azib (البراء بن عازب) narrates: "I heard the Prophet ﷺ reciting 'By the Fig and the Olive' in the Isha prayer, and I never heard anyone with a better voice or recitation than him."

His voice was not merely a musical tune, but a "tool of humility" (Khushu') that melted hearts. When he recited Surah At-Tur, Jubayr bin Mut'im (جبير بن مطعم) (who was a polytheist at the time) said: "My heart almost flew." His voice combined sweetness with humility, power with influence, surpassing by stages what Dawud was given, because it was a voice carrying the "Miraculous Qur'an" in a clear Arabic tongue.

Third: More Beautiful than the Moon (Eyewitness Testimonies)

The Companions could not find an earthly comparison that did justice to the Prophet's ﷺ beauty, so they resorted to the sky.

  • Jabir bin Samura and the Live Comparison: Jabir (جابر بن سمرة) says: "I saw the Messenger of Allah ﷺ on a clear moonlit night (Lailah Adhyan), wearing a red garment. I began looking at him and looking at the moon, and he was, in my eyes, more beautiful than the moon." (Imagine the scene: A companion shifting his gaze between the full moon in the sky and the Prophet's face on earth, to issue a final verdict that the light of the Prophetic face is more beautiful and splendid).
  • Abu Huraira and the Sun: He says: "I have not seen anything more beautiful than the Messenger of Allah ﷺ; it was as if the sun was running in his face."
  • Al-Rubay' bint Mu'awwidh: When asked to describe him, she said with dazzling brevity: "If you saw him, you would see the sun rising."
  • Ka'b bin Malik: He says: "When he was happy, his face lit up, as if it were a piece of the moon."

Fourth: Pleasant Scent (Moving Musk)

We discussed this regarding the body, and we emphasize it here as part of supreme aesthetics. The Prophet ﷺ did not need perfume to beautify himself; rather, perfume needed him to become more fragrant.

  • Pearl Sweat: If he slept and sweated, Umm Sulaym collected his sweat. When asked, "What are you doing?", she said: "It is the best of our perfumes." His sweat was clear as pearls, and its scent was sharper than musk and ambergris.
  • Trace of Handshake: Anas says: "I never smelled ambergris nor musk nor anything more fragrant than the scent of the Messenger of Allah ﷺ." Jabir bin Samura says: "He wiped my cheek, and I found a coolness and scent on his hand as if he had just taken it out of a perfumer's leather bag."

Fifth: Longing and Vision (How Do We See Him?)

After this tour through the gardens of Muhammadan beauty, there is no doubt that the heart yearns and the eye tears in longing to see this luminous face, even in a dream. Is there a way?

Seeing the Prophet ﷺ in a dream is "truth," for he said: "Whoever sees me in a dream has truly seen me, for Satan cannot assume my form." But the condition is to see him in his "true form" mentioned in Hadiths and biographies (which we detailed in this article). Whoever sees him in a form contradicting his descriptions (e.g., seeing him shaven, very short, dark-skinned, or with distorted features), this is not him, but a trick from Satan.

To achieve this blessed vision, the Knowers advise practical steps:

  1. Precise Knowledge: To memorize his descriptions and visualize them in your imagination (as we did today), for the subconscious builds its dreams from what the consciousness stores.
  2. Abundant Salawat: Increasing prayers and peace upon him ﷺ, for it is the connected rope and the mail of love knocking on his door.
  3. Following his Sunnah: To live your life as he lived, in your eating, drinking, sleeping, and morals. Similarity in behavior attracts similarity in spirit.
  4. Sincerity of Longing: That the heart is filled with true love and genuine eagerness, not mere curiosity. To long for him as the thirsty longs for cold water.
  5. Purification of the Heart: A mirror does not reflect the image if it is dirty; purify your heart from malice, envy, and sins, to become a clear mirror in which the Muhammadan lights manifest.

Conclusion:

The Prophet ﷺ was a "Sign of Allah" in his creation and character. A human whom Allah fashioned with His hand, breathed into him of His spirit, and chose over the worlds, making his image a delight for onlookers and his biography a beacon for wayfarers. Knowing his descriptions is not an intellectual luxury, but part of "Faith" and "Love." The more you know him, the more you love him; and the more you love him, the closer you will be to his assembly on the Day of Resurrection.

O Allah, just as You have denied us seeing him in this world, do not deny us seeing him in the Hereafter, and grant us a drink from his noble hand after which we will never thirst again.

The Dissipation of Darkness and the Dawn of Relief: The Birth Pangs of Victory in the Eleventh Year of Prophethood

The eleventh year of the Prophetic Mission (Ba'thah) represents a decisive historical turning point in the biography (Seerah) of the Chosen One (Al-Mustafa [المصطفى]), peace be upon him. It stands as a separating station between the phase of severe Meccan persecution and the early signs of Medinan empowerment. To grasp the greatness of this transformation, we must pause at length before the tragic scene that preceded this moment, where the tribulation reached its peak and darkness surrounded the Prophet ﷺ from all sides, at a time when all earthly means were severed, leaving only the extended rope of Heaven.

First: The Psychological and Realistic Siege and the Accumulation of Afflictions

The Prophet ﷺ entered this phase carrying mountains of worries that would have crushed solid rock. He had just emerged from the siege of the "Glen of Abu Talib" (Shi'b Abi Talib [شِعب أبي طالب]), which lasted three lean years during which the Muslims were reduced to eating tree leaves.

Hardly had he emerged from this siege when the "Year of Sorrow" ('Am al-Huzn [عام الحزن]) ensued with the death of his uncle, Abu Talib [أبي طالب], the political and social fortress who warded off the foolish among Quraysh [قريش], followed by the death of his wife, Khadijah bint Khuwaylid [خديجة بنت خويلد] (may Allah be pleased with her), his psychological sanctuary, emotional refuge, and the primary financial supporter of the call (Da'wah).

With the death of these two pillars, Quraysh inflicted harm upon the Messenger of Allah ﷺ that they had never dared to before. The foolish became emboldened, inflicting physical and emotional abuse. It was then that the Prophet ﷺ decided to go to At-Ta'if [الطائف], traveling over a hundred kilometers on foot, driven by the hope of finding support among the tribe of Thaqif [ثقيف] that he had lost in Makkah.

However, the shock was terrible; they rejected him in the most repulsive manner, inciting their fools and slaves to pelt him with stones until his noble feet bled, while Zayd bin Harithah [زيد بن حارثة] shielded him with his own body until his head was wounded. The Prophet ﷺ returned from Ta'if distressed and unaware of his surroundings until he reached Qarn al-Tha'alib [قرن الثعالب]. The earth had closed its doors in his face, to the extent that he could not enter Makkah except under the protection (Jiwar) of a polytheist man, Al-Mut'im bin Adi [المطعم بن عدي]. This scene embodies the peak of severed material means: the people denied him, his family failed him, his protectors died, and those he invited in Ta'if expelled him.

Second: The Philosophy of "Complaining to Allah" and the Law of Relief

In those darkest moments, when ropes to humanity were cut, the connection to the Lord of humanity was established. Here, the servitude of "desperation" and complaining to Allah alone manifests. It is narrated in tradition that the Prophet ﷺ made his famous supplication at that moment—a supplication which, even if scholars discuss its chain of narration, drips with servitude and prophethood in its meaning: "O Allah, to You I complain of the weakness of my strength, the limitation of my strategy, and my insignificance before people..."

The philosophy of complaint in Islam is precise; it is a complaint to Allah, not about Allah. Complaining to Allah is an act of worship, and neediness toward Him is strength. The Quran and the stories of the Prophets have taught us that this state of total brokenness before Allah is what precedes great relief and clear victory.

  • Ya'qub [يعقوب] (Jacob), peace be upon him: When the affliction intensified, and he lost Yusuf and his brother, and his eyes turned white from sorrow, he did not despair. Instead, he said his immortal words recorded in the Quran: “I only complain of my suffering and my grief to Allah, and I know from Allah that which you do not know.” (Yusuf: 86). The result was that Allah restored his sight, reunited him with his sons, and raised his status.
  • Al-Mujadilah [المجادلة] (The Pleading Woman): That weak woman who came to complain about her husband had no supporter but Allah, so Allah heard her complaint from above seven heavens: “Certainly has Allah heard the speech of the one who argues with you, [O Muhammad], concerning her husband and directs her complaint to Allah.” (Al-Mujadilah: 1).
  • Ayyub [أيوب] (Job), peace be upon him: When he called out with the utmost etiquette: “Indeed, adversity has touched me, and you are the Most Merciful of the merciful.” (Al-Anbiya: 83), the immediate response came: “So We responded to him.”

The Prophet ﷺ reached the stage of "To whom do You entrust me?"—the moment when the heart strips itself of looking to anyone other than Allah, and that is when victory descends. It is mentioned in the Sahihayn (Bukhari and Muslim) that the Angel of the Mountains offered to crush the people of Makkah between the two mountains (Al-Akhshabayn), but the Prophet ﷺ, with his mercy, patience, and vision for the future, chose patience and prayer, hoping that Allah would bring forth from their loins those who would worship Him.

Third: The Journey of Seeking Support and Presenting Himself to the Tribes

After the incident at Ta'if, and with the Hajj season approaching, the Prophet ﷺ did not succumb to despair. Instead, he renewed his attempts using the strategy of seeking Man'ah (protection) and Nusrah (support). Arabs would flock to Makkah and its markets (Ukaz [عكاظ], Majannah [مجنة], and Dhul-Majaz [ذي المجاز]) for pilgrimage and trade, so the Prophet ﷺ saw this as an opportunity to present his call to the tribes.

He would visit them in their encampments, tribe by tribe, saying: "O Banu So-and-so, I am the Messenger of Allah to you. He commands you to worship Allah and not associate anything with Him... and to believe in me, affirm me, and protect me so that I may explain what Allah has sent me with."

He visited many tribes, including: Banu Amir bin Sa'sa'ah [بنو عامر بن صعصعة], Muharib bin Khasfah [محارب بن خصفة], Fazarah [فزارة], Ghassan [غسان], Murrah [مرة], Hanifah [حنيفة], Sulaym [سليم], Abs [عبس], Banu Nasr [بنو نضر], Kindah [كندة], and others.

The responses varied between polite refusal, ugly rejection, and violent repulsion. Among the ugliest responses was that of Banu Hanifah [بنو حنيفة] (the tribe of Musaylimah later on), who rejected him abominably. His uncle Abu Lahab [أبو لهب] followed him like a shadow; whenever the Prophet finished inviting a people, Abu Lahab would stand and say: "O Banu So-and-so, this man only invites you to strip away Al-Lat and Al-Uzza... so do not obey him and do not listen to him."

Amidst this collective rejection and continuous denial, Allah was preparing something else for His Prophet. Divine wisdom dictated that every door in Makkah, every door in Ta'if, and every door of the famous large tribes be closed, so that a door no one had accounted for could be opened—a door coming from a city torn by conflict, through a small group of young men who had no great renown. This fulfilled Allah's sunnah (way): victory comes from where the servant does not expect, and relief is greatest and most beautiful after total despair of earthly means. “Until when the messengers despaired and thought that they had been denied, there came to them Our victory.” (Yusuf: 110).

With this spirit full of certainty despite the blocked horizon, and with total reliance despite human abandonment, the Prophet ﷺ welcomed the Hajj season of the eleventh year, for a new dawn to rise from "Al-Aqabah."

The First Meeting: Seeds of Blessed Goodness at "Al-Aqabah"

In the darkness of the pitch-black night, while Makkah buzzed with the noise of pilgrims and the tribes dozed off after days of rituals, trade, and boasting of lineage, there was a wakeful heart that knew no fatigue and a soul yearning to save humanity from the Fire. It was the Messenger of Allah ﷺ, whom neither the dry responses of tribes nor the repulsion of masters and elites could deter from continuing the search for the "seed" upon which the state of Islam would be established.

This occurred during the Hajj season of the eleventh year of Prophethood, when Divine Decree led the Prophet's steps toward "Al-Aqabah" [العقبة] in Mina, away from the watchful eyes of Quraysh, to make history in a meeting that was simple in appearance but magnificent in its essence and outcomes.

First: Details of the Historic Meeting at Al-Aqabah

While the Prophet ﷺ was walking, he heard the faint voices of men talking. These men were not known Arab chiefs, nor crowned kings, but a "group" (Nafar)—a small number—of young men from Yathrib [يثرب] (Medina), coming for Hajj.

The Prophet ﷺ approached them and began the dialogue with the high etiquette of Prophethood, as Ibn Ishaq narrates in the Seerah:
He asked them: "Who are you people?"
They said: "A group from the Khazraj."
Here, the Prophet ﷺ verified their identity and alliances, asking: "Are you the allies (Mawali) of the Jews?"
They said: "Yes."
He said: "Will you not sit so that I may speak to you?"
They said: "Indeed."

These six sat down—they were youths, not elders—and they were:

  1. As'ad bin Zurarah [أسعد بن زرارة] (from Banu an-Najjar).
  2. Auf bin Al-Harith [عوف بن الحارث] (from Banu an-Najjar).
  3. Rafi' bin Malik [رافع بن مالك] (from Banu Zurayq).
  4. Qutbah bin Amir [قطبة بن عامر] (from Banu Salimah).
  5. Uqbah bin Amir [عقبة بن عامر] (from Banu Haram).
  6. Jabir bin Abdullah bin Ri'ab [جابر بن عبد الله بن رئاب] (from Banu Ubayd bin Ghannam).

The Prophet ﷺ did not waste time with worldly introductions but went straight to the heart of his mission. He invited them to Allah Almighty, presented Islam to them, and recited the Quran. The scene was majestic; the voice of Heaven echoed in the corners of Al-Aqabah, and the words of Allah struck the ears of the hearts of these young men, whose nature (Fitrah) had not been polluted by the arrogance of pride present in the leaders of Quraysh.

As soon as the Prophet ﷺ finished his speech, they looked at one another and said their immortal words that indicated awareness and understanding: "O people, you know, by Allah, that this is indeed the Prophet with whom the Jews threaten you. Do not let them precede you to him."

So they hastened to answer him, believed him, accepted Islam, and said: "We have left our people (meaning the Aws [الأوس] and Khazraj [الخزرج]), and no people have such enmity and evil between them as they do. Perhaps Allah will unite them through you. We will go to them and invite them to your matter, and present to them this religion which we have accepted from you. If Allah unites them upon you, then no man will be mightier than you."

Second: The Lesson of "Not Belittling Missionary Opportunities"

Reflecting on this event reveals a golden rule in the jurisprudence of Da'wah (calling to Islam) and working for this religion: "Do not belittle any act of kindness, and never underestimate any person you invite."

The Prophet ﷺ passed by these individuals who were young, not tribal chiefs like Abdullah bin Ubayy bin Salul [عبد الله بن أبي ابن سلول], nor elderly masters. They were few (only six), the time was late, and the place was secluded. By material standards, this meeting might have seemed "useless" or "marginal." But by the scale of Prophethood, every soul is a goal, and every human is a potential nation.

The Quran taught us in Surah "Abasa" not to be distracted by the elite from the weak or simple people. Here at Al-Aqabah, the Prophet ﷺ applied this lesson practically; he did not abstain from inviting these youths. History proved that these six were better than an earth full of people like Abu Jahl and Abu Lahab.

This gives hope to every preacher and reformer: do not wait for major platforms, massive crowds, or prominent figures to start. A word you speak to a young boy, a novice student, or a simple man on the street might be the seed from which a new Sa'd bin Mu'adh [سعد بن معاذ] or Mus'ab bin Umair [مصعب بن عمير] emerges. The value is not in the grandiosity of the beginnings, but in the sincerity of the intent and the blessing of the results that Allah bestows.

Third: Tribal Ties and Psychological Preparation (The Prophet's Maternal Uncles)

Part of Allah's wisdom in arranging this meeting was the factor of "kinship and lineage." The Prophet ﷺ asked at the beginning: "Are you the allies of the Jews?" and knew they were from Al-Khazraj [الخزرج].

The Khazraj had a strong blood tie to the Prophet ﷺ; his grandfather Abdul-Muttalib's [عبد المطلب] mother was Salma bint Amr [سلمى بنت عمرو], from Banu an-Najjar [بنو النجار] (a clan of Khazraj). Thus, the Khazraj, and Banu an-Najjar specifically, are considered maternal uncles to the Messenger of Allah ﷺ (uncles of his father and grandfather).

The Arabs, even in their ignorance (Jahiliyyah), venerated the rights of kinship. When a man sits with them saying he is a Prophet, and at the same time is the "son of their sister" (in the broad tribal sense), this removes barriers of alienation and unlocks hearts.

The presence of two specifically from Banu an-Najjar among these six (As'ad bin Zurarah and Auf bin Al-Harith) was a comforting psychological factor; they were not listening to a "stranger," but to a man whose blood ran in their veins.

This point emphasizes the importance of utilizing social and family ties in calling to Allah. “And warn, [O Muhammad], your closest kindred.” (Ash-Shu'ara: 214). Not only because they are more deserving of kindness, but because they are often quicker to respond and more protective of the caller than others. Allah prepared this kinship in Medina to be a safe incubator later on; thus, his first stay in Medina was with his uncles from Banu an-Najjar, and they were his protectors and supporters (Ansar).

The blessed council adjourned, and the six returned to Yathrib, carrying not just trade goods, but the "Light of Islam," becoming the first unofficial ambassadors of the new religion. Thanks to this fleeting meeting, the city would transform from "Yathrib," filled with vengeance and blood, to "Al-Madinah Al-Munawwarah" (The Illuminated City), which would welcome the Master of Humanity two years later.

Why the Ansar? Divine and Realistic Factors that Prepared "Yathrib" for the Light

Reflecting on the movement of history and the laws of change, one realizes with certainty that great events do not happen by chance. When Allah Almighty wills a matter, He prepares its causes. The selection of the "Ansar" [الأنصار] (The Aws and Khazraj) to carry the banner of Islam and embrace the Messenger of Allah ﷺ was not a random choice, but the fruit of precise divine arrangement, where religious, political, social, and psychological factors combined to make the people of Yathrib—unlike other Arabs—the most ready to receive the Muhammadan call.

First: The Jewish Factor... The Threat that Turned into Good Tidings

It is one of the strange measures of Destiny that the "Jews," who would later become bitter enemies of the Prophet ﷺ, were the primary cause—without realizing it—in paving the way for the Ansar's faith.

Yathrib housed three major Jewish tribes (Banu Qaynuqa [بنو قينقاع], Banu Nadir [بنو النضير], and Banu Qurayzah [بنو قريظة]), living alongside the Arabs (Aws and Khazraj). As People of the Book, they viewed themselves as possessors of knowledge and scripture, while viewing the Arabs as illiterate pagans.

When disputes and wars occurred between the Jews and Arabs, and the Arabs would sometimes defeat them due to their numbers and bravery, the Jews resorted to a dangerous psychological weapon: "Threatening with the Future." They would say menacingly: "The time has approached for a Prophet to be sent now. We will follow him and kill you with him, like the killing of 'Aad and Iram."

Without intending to, the Jews entrenched two facts in the minds of the Aws and Khazraj:

  1. That a true Prophet would appear in this era.
  2. That this Prophet would be victorious and divinely supported, and whoever follows him will prevail.

This "Istiftah" [الاستفتاح] (seeking victory by mentioning the awaited Prophet) was immortalized by the Quran in a precise verse revealing this strange scene: “And when there came to them a Book from Allah confirming that which was with them - although before they used to pray for victory against those who disbelieved - but [then] when there came to them that which they recognized, they disbelieved in it; so the curse of Allah will be upon the disbelievers.” (Al-Baqarah: 89).

The Jews used to pray for victory over those who disbelieved (the Arab polytheists), but when the Prophet ﷺ appeared, the surprise was that the Arabs were the ones who hastened to believe in him, fearing the Jews would beat them to him as they had threatened. The Ansar said to each other at Al-Aqabah: "O people, you know, by Allah, that this is indeed the Prophet with whom the Jews threaten you. Do not let them precede you to him."

Thus, by Allah's unbeatable plan, the Jews' threat turned into the greatest psychological and religious incentive driving the Ansar to rush into Islam. They became the Supporters (Ansar), while the Jews incurred wrath upon wrath.

Second: The Factor of War (The Day of Bu'ath)... The Dire Need for a Savior and Unifier

Politically and socially, Yathrib was living through an unbearable hell of grinding civil wars between the Aws and Khazraj, which lasted for decades, fueled by Jahiliyyah hatred and the machinations of the Jews who ignited strife to benefit commercially and politically.

These wars peaked in a bloody battle known as the "Day of Bu'ath" [يوم بُعاث], occurring about five years before the Hijrah. In this battle, the two tribes decimated each other, and a large number of the nobility, elders, and leaders who were heads of strife and fanaticism were killed.

The people of Yathrib emerged from the "Day of Bu'ath" completely exhausted; blood flowed like rivers, homes were ruined, and the social fabric was torn. A desperate desire for salvation arose among the wise and especially the youth—a diligent search for an "exit" to restore unity and end this absurd cycle of blood.

They were searching for a leader to unite them, to the point that they almost crowned Abdullah bin Ubayy bin Salul [عبد الله بن أبي ابن سلول] as king to end the conflict, but Allah's will saved something greater for them.

Therefore, when the Prophet ﷺ presented Islam to them—which calls for brotherhood, rejecting tribalism, and holding fast to the rope of Allah—they found in it their missing object. They found the "Savior" who would extinguish the fire of revenge and unite the warring ranks. They expressed this clearly to the Prophet ﷺ at Al-Aqabah, saying: "We have left our people... perhaps Allah will unite them through you."

Regarding this great role of Islam in uniting them, Allah reminded them of His favor in the Quran: “And remember the favor of Allah upon you - when you were enemies and He brought your hearts together and you became, by His favor, brothers. And you were on the edge of a pit of the Fire, and He saved you from it.” (Al-Imran: 103). War was the chronic disease, and Islam was the healing cure.

Third: The Youth Factor... Flexibility and Purity of Nature

The third factor, no less important, is the demographic and psychological nature of the first Ansar to convert. Their grinding wars (especially Bu'ath) led to the death of most of the "Old Chiefs" and traditional leaders who were stubborn in their opinions and usually stood as stumbling blocks to any radical change, clinging to ancestral heritage.

The death of the elders made room for a new generation of "Youth" to lead the scene (like Sa'd bin Mu'adh [سعد بن معاذ] who converted and led his people while in his thirties, As'ad bin Zurarah [أسعد بن زرارة], and Mus'ab bin Umair [مصعب بن عمير] their young ambassador). Youth, by nature, possess hearts that are softer, minds more open, a readiness for adventure and change, and enthusiasm to adopt new truths if proven, far from the calculations of complex interests and social prestige that shackled the elders.

The Prophet ﷺ found fertile ground in the youth of the Ansar, where the roots of Jahiliyyah had not hardened as they had in Makkah, and which were not polluted by false leadership calculations. They were young men searching for truth, peace, and true glory. This "youthful flexibility" caused Islam to spread in Medina like wildfire, until no house remained without Islam entering it, a unique phenomenon that did not happen in Makkah, whose elders (like Abu Jahl and Walid bin Al-Mughirah) resisted for many years.

In Conclusion:

Allah Almighty, through His kindness and planning, made the "plotting of the Jews" (their prophecies), the "evil of war" (Day of Bu'ath), and the "drive of youth" (absence of stubborn elders) a golden triangle preparing the Ansar to receive the Message. It is as if Destiny speaks: The tribulations nations pass through may be the difficult labor pains preceding the birth of the dawn of guidance and empowerment.

The First Pledge of Aqabah: Founding the Muslim Society and Building the Covenant of Faith

A full year after the first meeting that planted the seeds of faith in the hearts of the six Khazraj men, the Hajj season of the twelfth year of Prophethood returned, bearing the fruits of that blessed planting. The delegation from Yathrib this time was not just passing individuals, but a solid core carrying a project to change the face of history, ready to make a deal of loyalty with the Messenger of Allah ﷺ, historically known as the "First Pledge of Aqabah" (Bay'at Al-Aqabah Al-Ula [بيعة العقبة الأولى]).

First: The Blessed Delegation (Numbers and Composition)

At the specified time and place (at Al-Aqabah in Mina), the Prophet ﷺ met the Ansar delegation. This time the number doubled; twelve men attended, indicating the diligent missionary effort the first six had undertaken over a full year in Medina.

What is notable about this delegation's composition is that it was not limited to the Khazraj. For the first time, it included representatives from the tribe of "Aws" [الأوس], a clear signal of the beginning of the thawing of ice between the two warring tribes under the banner of Monotheism (Tawhid).

The delegation consisted of:

  • Ten from Khazraj: (The five who attended the previous year—excluding Jabir bin Abdullah bin Ri'ab who did not attend—along with seven new ones, most notably Ubadah bin As-Samit [عبادة بن الصامت]).
  • Two from Aws: (Abu Al-Haytham bin At-Tayhan [أبو الهيثم بن التيهان] and Uwaym bin Sa'idah [عويم بن ساعدة]).

This diversity (Aws and Khazraj) was an implicit message that Yathrib was coming with all its social weight and that Islam had indeed begun to unite the internal ranks.

Second: Terms of the Pledge (Constitution of Ethics and Values)

These men extended their hands to pledge allegiance to the Prophet ﷺ, not for war and fighting (at this stage), but on a system of ethical and doctrinal values forming the "foundation for building the Muslim society."

Imam Al-Bukhari and Muslim narrated in their Sahih collections from Ubadah bin As-Samit [عبادة بن الصامت] (one of the leaders present) saying: "We were with the Prophet ﷺ in a gathering, and he said: 'Pledge allegiance to me that you will not associate anything with Allah, nor steal, nor commit adultery, nor kill your children, nor bring a slander which you invent between your hands and feet, nor disobey in what is right...'"

This pledge included six main terms:

  1. Monotheism (Tawhid): Not associating anything with Allah. The foundation upon which everything is built and the point of total separation from pagan Jahiliyyah.
  2. Financial Integrity: Not stealing. Preserving rights and wealth, building mutual trust.
  3. Physical Chastity: Not committing adultery. Purifying society from immorality and preserving lineage and honor.
  4. Preservation of Life: Not killing children. Prohibition of Wa'd (burying daughters alive due to shame) or killing children due to fear of poverty, establishing the value of the human being.
  5. Social Honesty: Not bringing slander. Prohibition of lying, fabrication, and accusing chaste women, ensuring clear hearts and social cohesion.
  6. Disciplined Obedience: Not disobeying in what is right (Ma'ruf). Establishing the principle of listening and obeying the Prophetic leadership, restricted by "what is right," to establish the rule "There is no obedience to a creature in disobedience to the Creator."

Third: Discussing the Label "Pledge of Women"

This pledge became famous in biography and history books as the "Pledge of Women" (Bay'at An-Nisa [بيعة النساء]). Some might mistakenly think this name means it was specific to women or diminishes the status of the men who pledged. This is a misunderstanding needing accurate scientific clarification.

Why was it called the Pledge of Women?
The reason is that its terms matched exactly the terms upon which the Prophet ﷺ accepted the pledge from women later after the Conquest of Makkah, revealed in Surah Al-Mumtahanah: “O Prophet, when the believing women come to you pledging to you that they will not associate anything with Allah, nor will they steal, nor will they commit unlawful sexual intercourse...” (Al-Mumtahanah: 12).

Because this pledge (First Aqabah) was devoid of the obligation of "Jihad, fighting, and defense," and was limited to devotional and ethical aspects, the Companions and narrators likened it to the women's pledge which also excludes fighting. The name describes the content of the pledge (peaceful and ethical), not the gender of those pledging, for they were all stalwart men.

Scientific Nuance regarding Narrations (For Benefit):
There is a precise discussion among Hadith commentators and biographers regarding the timing of these terms.

  • Ubadah's Narration: Explicit that these terms were spoken on the "Night of the First Aqabah" (before Hijrah).
  • The Issue: Some scholars (like Ibn Hajar and Ibn Kathir) discussed the idea that the verse of Al-Mumtahanah was revealed later (after Hudaybiyyah or the Conquest), and that Hudud (punishments like for theft/adultery) had not been legislated in detail in Makkah.
  • Reconciliation:
    1. The prevailing view is that the Prophet ﷺ accepted their pledge at the First Aqabah on these "Universal Principles" (prohibition of adultery, theft, killing, shirk), which are constant prohibitions in all laws and the core of the Meccan religion, even before detailed legal punishments were revealed in Medina.
    2. That Ubadah bin As-Samit (the narrator), when recounting later, used the wording of the famous Quranic verse to approximate the meaning, or that Revelation came with this content early on and the Quran confirmed it later.
    3. Another view suggests Ubadah might have combined two gatherings in his narration, but the context "Pledge to me..." in the Sahih strongly suggests these were indeed the terms of the First Aqabah—a pledge of "Founding Faith" before "State and Jihad."

In Conclusion:
The First Pledge of Aqabah was the ethical "cornerstone" of the coming state. The Prophet ﷺ did not ask them to bear arms immediately but asked them to "bear values." He wanted to build the "Muslim Human" before building the "Islamic State," because a state not based on a chaste, honest, united, and obedient society is fragile. With this pledge, the twelve leaders (Nuqaba) returned to Medina not just as Muslims, but as "Preachers" carrying an ethical constitution that would change the face of Yathrib forever.

"His Reward is with Allah": The Philosophy of Recompense and the Reality of the Divine Contract

When the Prophet ﷺ extended his hand to accept the pledge of the Ansar at Al-Aqabah, he was not making a traditional political deal based on exchanging immediate interests or distributing spoils and positions. He was concluding a unique "Divine Contract," its terms in this world (obedience and commitment), and its reward deferred to the Hereafter (Paradise and Good Pleasure). At the conclusion of the pledge terms, the Prophet ﷺ laid down the golden rule of recompense, saying: "Whoever among you fulfills [his pledge], his reward is with Allah."

This concise phrase carries a profound philosophy regarding the nature of the relationship between the believer and his Lord, correcting many misconceptions about "transactional religiosity" that demands worldly gain in exchange for faith.

First: Absence of Worldly Material Promises

It is truly astonishing that the Prophet ﷺ, while in dire need of support, did not entice the Ansar with any material gain. He did not say: "If you support me, you will rule the Arabs, or the treasures of Kisra and Caesar will come to you, or you will have leadership." Although these things happened later, they were not the "Price" offered at the moment of the pledge.

Why? Because Allah wants a people who carry the religion for Allah, not for the world. He wants hearts that sacrifice knowing the price is high (Paradise), not cheap (fleeting goods).

When the Ansar asked clearly in the Second Pledge of Aqabah: "What is for us, O Messenger of Allah, if we fulfill this?" He said one word: "Paradise" (Al-Jannah). They said: "Extend your hand," and they pledged allegiance. They understood the message: Do not expect immediate reward here; you may be killed, your wealth may be looted, you may be opposed by all Arabs, but Allah's commodity is Paradise.

This meaning is confirmed by the verse: “Indeed, Allah has purchased from the believers their lives and their properties [in exchange] for that they will have Paradise.” (At-Tawbah: 111). The contract is clear: Life and wealth for Paradise, not necessarily for worldly prosperity.

Second: "The Good Life"... The Only Worldly Promise

Despite the negation of "Material Guarantees" (like wealth, health, rapid empowerment), there is one Divine promise fulfilled for every believer in this world without exception: the promise of a "Good Life" (Hayat Tayyibah [حياة طيبة]).

Allah says: “Whoever does righteousness, whether male or female, while he is a believer - We will surely cause him to live a good life, and We will surely give them their reward [in the Hereafter] according to the best of what they used to do.” (An-Nahl: 97).

The Good Life here is not necessarily a life of palaces and money, but the life of the heart. It is tranquility, contentment, intimacy with Allah, expansion of the chest, and the sweetness of faith found by the believer even in hardship. It is the state that made the Companions the happiest people while tying stones to their stomachs from hunger, and made Bilal chant "One, One" (Ahad, Ahad) under the rock while tasting a sweetness the masters of Quraysh could not find in their wine gatherings.

This is the "immediate" good news for the believer; the "deferred" is what is with Allah. Thus, the concept must be corrected: You may adhere to religion and your world increases, or you may adhere and be tested with constriction. In both cases, you are promised the Good Life in your heart and Allah's reward in your Hereafter.

Third: Commitment in Ease and Hardship (Negating Conditional Religiosity)

The Pledge of Aqabah establishes the principle of "Total Detachment." The believer does not worship Allah "on an edge"; if good befalls him, he is reassured, but if a trial befalls him, he turns on his face. Rather, he worships Him in prosperity and adversity, in what he loves (Al-Manshat) and in what he dislikes and finds burdensome (Al-Makrah).

In Ubadah bin As-Samit's narration in the Sahihayn regarding the Second Aqabah: "We pledged allegiance to the Messenger of Allah ﷺ to hear and obey in hardship and ease, in what we like and what we dislike, and [even] if we are deprived [of worldly shares]..."

The phrase "Atharatin 'alayna" [أثرة علينا] (even if others are preferred over us) means: We will hear and obey even if others monopolize the world and we are deprived of it. This is the true test of sincerity: Will you remain loyal to the covenant when your personal interest or desire conflicts with Allah's command? Here, the truthful are distinguished from the pretenders.

Fourth: The Concept of "Concealment" and "Expiation"... The Mercy of Penal Law

In the second part of the Pledge hadith, the Prophet ﷺ addressed the issue of violation and committing sin, establishing rules of Divine Justice and Mercy in dealing with sinners. He said ﷺ:
"Whoever commits any of these [sins] and is punished for it in this world, it is an expiation (Kaffarah) for him. And whoever commits any of these and Allah conceals him (Satr), then his matter is with Allah; if He wills, He will punish him, and if He wills, He will forgive him."

Here, two greatnesses of Islam manifest:

  1. The Greatness of Hudud as Expiation (Purification):
    Legal punishments in Islam are not just "deterrents" or "societal vengeance," but mercy for the sinner. Whoever receives the punishment in this world (like a thief whose hand is cut, or an adulterer flogged) has been purified, and Allah does not combine two punishments on him (worldly and hereafter). The punishment is an otherworldly "certificate of innocence." Thus, some Companions (like Ma'iz and the Ghamidi woman) came repenting, asking for purification via punishment, certain that worldly pain is lighter than the Hereafter's punishment.
  2. The Greatness of Concealment and Divine Will (Hope):
    As for the one who sins and Allah conceals him in this world, he is not required to expose himself. Instead, concealment (Satr) and repentance between him and Allah are recommended. Here, the Prophet ﷺ establishes the creed of Ahlus-Sunnah wal-Jama'ah regarding the perpetrator of major sins: he is "under Divine Will." He is not a disbeliever eternally in Hell (as the Kharijites say), nor guaranteed salvation (as the Murji'ah say). His matter is entrusted to Allah; if He wills, He punishes him with His justice, and if He wills, He pardons him with His mercy and grace. This opens a wide door of hope for every sinner, endearing him to his Lord, the Concealer, the Pardoner.

In Conclusion:
The Pledge of Aqabah was not merely a human agreement but a reconfiguration of the Muslim mindset. It taught that the real trade is with Allah, the currency is truthfulness and loyalty, and the profit is guaranteed: a good life in this world, forgiveness or expiation for errors, and a Paradise as wide as the heavens and earth in the Hereafter.

The First Ambassador of Islam: Mus'ab bin Umair and the Founding Mission in Yathrib

Following the Hajj season and the return of the twelve Ansar delegates to Medina, the Prophet ﷺ, with his brilliant leadership wisdom, realized that this "core" needed continuous educational and spiritual care. He did not leave them to return without a guide but decided to send a "Special Envoy" to represent Islam, teach them the Quran, and instruct them in religion. The Prophetic choice fell upon a young man from the best of Quraysh's youth to bear the title of "The First Ambassador of Islam": Mus'ab bin Umair [مصعب بن عمير] (may Allah be pleased with him).

First: Why Mus'ab? (Wisdom of Selection)

Choosing Mus'ab bin Umair for this sensitive mission was not accidental but a carefully studied decision.

  1. Scholarly Competence and Memorization: Mus'ab was among the earliest to embrace Islam. He was educated in the House of Al-Arqam bin Abi Al-Arqam [الأرقم بن أبي الأرقم], memorizing what was revealed of the Quran in Makkah and understanding the spirit and objectives of the religion. He was nicknamed "The Reciter" (Al-Muqri) for his abundant knowledge and quality of recitation.
  2. Personal Traits (Gentleness and Softness): Mus'ab was known for his good manners, calm nature, superior ability in dialogue and persuasion, and patience with harm. These are essential qualities for a preacher entering a complex tribal society like Yathrib, filled with feuds and sensitivities.
  3. Sacrifice and Detachment: Mus'ab was the pampered boy, the most perfumed of Makkah, who left luxury for the sake of Allah, wearing rough clothes. This living model of asceticism and sacrifice was more effective than a thousand speeches; people are influenced by "role models" more than "words."

Second: The Complete Personality (The Preacher-Warrior)

The mental image of Mus'ab bin Umair is often confined to the "Quiet Preacher" or the "Ascetic Youth," but history presents a "Multi-dimensional Personality" that gathered glory from all sides:

  • The Wise Preacher: In less than a year, he managed to shift the course of Medina's history, introducing Islam into most Ansar homes with wisdom and good preaching, without sparking early sedition or conflict.
  • The Learned Scholar: He was the primary religious reference in Medina before the Hijrah, teaching people the Quran, leading them in prayer (he was the first to gather people for Jumu'ah prayer in Medina before the Prophet's arrival), and issuing rulings.
  • The Ascetic Worshipper: He lived a life of austerity and worship. When he was martyred, they could not find a shroud to cover his whole body; if they covered his head, his feet appeared, and if they covered his feet, his head appeared—he who used to wear silk in Jahiliyyah.
  • The Heroic Warrior: This is the great surprise in Mus'ab's character. In the Battle of Uhud, he was the "Standard Bearer of the Emigrants" (Muhajireen). He fought with the ferocity of lions. When his right hand was cut off, he held the flag with his left; when the left was cut, he embraced the flag with his upper arms against his chest while reciting: “Muhammad is not but a messenger. [Other] messengers have passed on before him.” until he fell as a martyr. He combined the gentleness of a preacher in peace with the toughness of a warrior in war, may Allah be pleased with him.

Third: Strategy of Work in Medina (The Successful Duo)

Mus'ab bin Umair arrived in Medina, but he did not work alone. Here lies a great lesson in "Teamwork" and synergy between the "Expat" (The Scholar/Preacher) and the "Resident" (The Influential Local).
Mus'ab stayed as a guest of As'ad bin Zurarah [أسعد بن زرارة] (Leader of Banu an-Najjar and one of the first six). The two men formed an ideal team:

  • Mus'ab's Role (Content): Possesses knowledge, the Quran, eloquence, and legal argument.
  • As'ad's Role (Logistics and Protection): Possesses social connections, knowledge of Medina's geography, ability to secure gatherings, host people, and protect the preacher with his tribal prestige.

As'ad would take Mus'ab around the homes of the Ansar and into their gardens (Hawa'it), gathering people for him to hear the Quran.
Islam began to spread quietly and steadily. It was not spread by the sword or force, but was a "Conquest of Hearts via the Quran." A man would hear the verses, the light would touch his heart, he would convert, then return to his family, and they would convert due to him.
Circles of knowledge spread in Ansar homes, recitation echoed in Yathrib, and the civil society began to form slowly but deeply, preparing for the Grand Moment—the reception of the Prophet ﷺ.
Mus'ab and As'ad's mission was to "pave the road," and they succeeded brilliantly, until it was said: "There remained no house of the Ansar but it contained Muslim men and women."

This dazzling success establishes an important missionary rule: A successful preacher needs a social incubator to support him. A scholar needs someone to open doors. When "Knowledge" integrates with "Status," and the "Word" with "Protection," the impact is immense.

The Conversion of the Masters: The Great Turn in Medina's History

While Islam was spreading gradually among the simple people and youth of Medina, there were fortified citadels yet to open—citadels representing the "center of political and social gravity" in Yathrib. Everyone realized that Islam would not be truly empowered in Medina unless these citadels entered the religion of Allah. These citadels were represented by the masters of Banu Abd al-Ashhal [بنو عبد الأشهل] from the Aws tribe: Sa'd bin Mu'adh [سعد بن معاذ] and Usayd bin Hudayr [أسيد بن حضير].

First: Confrontation in the Garden of Banu Zafar

Mus'ab bin Umair went out with his host As'ad bin Zurarah one day, entering a garden (Ha'it) of Banu Zafar [بنو ظفر], and sat by a well called "Bi'r Maraq." A group of Muslims and those wishing to hear the Quran gathered around them.
The news reached Sa'd bin Mu'adh (Chief of the Aws), who was sitting with his friend and ally Usayd bin Hudayr. Sa'd was furious, seeing this act as a challenge to his authority and a breach of his tribe's sovereignty.
Sa'd said to Usayd: "You have no father! Go to these two men who have come to our dwellings to fool our weak ones. Scold them and forbid them from coming to our dwellings. Were it not that As'ad bin Zurarah is my kin (cousin), I would have sufficed you in this."

Second: Islam of Usayd bin Hudayr (Power of Argument and Etiquette of Dialogue)

Usayd carried his spear and headed toward the garden, boiling with anger. As'ad bin Zurarah saw him coming from afar and recognized the sparks of rage in his eyes. He said to Mus'ab his immortal words that reflect the jurisprudence of Da'wah: "This is the master of his people coming to you; be true to Allah regarding him."

The phrase "be true to Allah regarding him" implies: Summon all your sincerity, wisdom, and reliance on Allah in this moment, for this is a precious catch; if Usayd converts, many will convert with him.
Usayd stood over them, reviling them, saying: "What brought you to us, fooling our weak ones? Leave us if you have any need for your lives."
Here, the wisdom of Mus'ab, the confident and calm preacher, manifested. He did not return the insult, nor was he provoked by the drawn weapon. Instead, he said with a reassuring smile: "Will you not sit and listen? If you are pleased with the matter, you accept it; and if you dislike it, we will stop what you dislike."
This rational response shocked Usayd, who was a wise and fair man (called "The Perfect One" in Jahiliyyah). He planted his spear in the ground and said: "You have spoken fairly."

Usayd sat, and Mus'ab spoke to him about Islam and read the Quran to him. The narrators say: "By Allah, we recognized Islam in his face before he spoke, in its radiance and ease." The Quran touched the core of his tough heart, turning him in moments from a bitter enemy to a loving friend.
Usayd asked: "How beautiful and noble is this speech! What do you do if you wish to enter this religion?"
They told him: "Wash and purify your garment, bear witness to the Truth, then pray."
Usayd stood, washed, purified his garment, bore witness that there is no god but Allah and that Muhammad is the Messenger of Allah, and prayed two units (Rak'ahs).

Third: Strategy of "Provoking Dignity" to Bring Sa'd bin Mu'adh

After converting, Usayd realized the mission was not over; the "Big Head" (Sa'd bin Mu'adh) was still there. If he went and invited him directly, Sa'd might refuse out of pride. Thus, Usayd resorted to a "clever strategy" to lure Sa'd to the Quranic gathering.
He said to Mus'ab and As'ad: "Behind me is a man; if he follows you, no one of his people will remain behind. I will send him to you now."

Usayd returned to Sa'd's gathering with changed features. Sa'd looked at him and said to those around him with the insight of a leader: "I swear by Allah, Usayd has returned to you with a face different from the one he left with."
Sa'd asked him: "What did you do?"
Usayd said (using equivocation): "I spoke to the two men, and by Allah, I saw no harm in them. I forbade them, and they said: 'We will do what you like.' However, I have been told that Banu Harithah [بنو حارثة] have gone out to As'ad bin Zurarah to kill him, knowing he is your cousin, wanting to break your protection."

These words were enough to ignite the fire of chivalry and tribal pride in Sa'd bin Mu'adh's chest. He didn't think of religion or Mus'ab, but of his "cousin" being harmed because of him and his "honor" being touched.
Sa'd rose angrily, grabbed the spear, and rushed to the garden to defend his cousin. But when he arrived, he found As'ad and Mus'ab safe and tranquil. He then realized it was a ploy by Usayd to bring him to the gathering to listen.

Fourth: Islam of Sa'd bin Mu'adh... The Earthquake of Faith

Sa'd stood over them reviling, saying to As'ad bin Zurarah: "By Allah, O Abu Umamah, were it not for the kinship between us, you would not have sought this from me. Do you trample our homes with what we dislike?"
Once again, Mus'ab repeated the same genius offer: "Will you not sit and listen? If you are pleased with the matter and desire it, you accept it; and if you dislike it, we will remove from you what you dislike."
Sa'd said: "You have spoken fairly." He planted his spear and sat.
Mus'ab presented Islam to him and read the opening of Surah Az-Zukhruf (or others). As happened with Usayd, so it happened with Sa'd. Mus'ab says: "By Allah, we recognized Islam in his face before he spoke, in its radiance and ease."
The veil of Jahiliyyah vanished, and the heart of the strong leader submitted to the authority of Truth. He asked the fateful question: "What do you do if you convert?"
They said: "Wash, purify your garment, bear witness to the Truth, and pray two Rak'ahs."
He did so immediately.

With the conversion of Sa'd bin Mu'adh, the Call did not just gain a man; it gained an "Army" and a "State." His conversion was like an earthquake shaking the pillars of polytheism in Medina. He did not hesitate or delay; he transformed instantly from a "receiver" of Truth to a "leader" imposing Truth with strength.
At that decisive moment in that humble garden, the first line in the history of Empowerment (Tamkin) was written, and the countdown began for the Prophet's migration to a city that was no longer strange, but had become, under Sa'd and Usayd's leadership, "Dar al-Islam" [دار الإسلام] (The Abode of Islam).

The Impact of the Truthful Leader: Sa'd bin Mu'adh and the Blessing of a Short Life

The conversion of Sa'd bin Mu'adh [سعد بن معاذ] (may Allah be pleased with him) was not a passing event in the history of the Call, but a radical upheaval in the balance of power in Medina. As soon as Sa'd washed, bore witness to the Truth, and prayed two units, his immense leadership energy and broad social influence shifted from serving tribalism to serving "La ilaha illa Allah" instantly. Here lies the greatness of the leader who realizes his responsibility toward his people, and the blessing of sincerity that shortens distances and time.

First: The Decisive Decision and the Conversion of Banu Abd al-Ashhal

Sa'd returned to his club (his people's gathering) accompanied by Usayd bin Hudayr. When Banu Abd al-Ashhal saw him coming, they said with their insight: "We swear by Allah, Sa'd has returned with a face different from the one he left with."
